1 Executive Summary

APPEARS THIS WAY ON ORIGINAL

14

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib

Product Introduction
Pralsetinib (also known as BLU-667) is an orally bioavailable kinase inhibitor that targets the
rearranged during transfection (RET) receptor tyrosine kinase, as well as several other human
kinases as described in Section 5 (Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology) of this review. Gene
rearrangements (fusions) in RET have been identified as oncogenic drivers in non-small cell lung
cancer (NSCLC).
Pralsetinib is a new molecular entity and is not currently approved in the United States or any
other part of the world. The proposed dosing regimen for pralsetinib is 400 mg orally once daily
on an empty stomach on a continuous schedule. The indication proposed by Blueprint
Medicines Corporation (Blueprint) for pralsetinib is “treatment of patients with rearranged
during transfection (RET)-positive locally advanced or metastatic non-small cell lung cancer
(NSCLC) as detected by an FDA approved test”.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
In the opinion of the review team, the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary
standard for accelerated approval. The recommendation for accelerated approval according to
21 CFR 314.510 Subpart H is based on results from two cohorts of patients with RET fusionpositive NSCLC who received pralsetinib 400 mg once daily in a multicenter, non-randomized,
multi-cohort clinical trial in patients with thyroid cancer, NSCLC, and other advanced solid
tumors (Study BLU-667-1101; ARROW). The confirmed overall response rates (ORR) per Blinded
Independent Review Committee (BIRC) in treatment-naïve patients with metastatic RET fusionpositive NSCLC (n=27) was 70% (95% CI 50, 86) with a median duration of response (DOR) of 9.0
months (95% CI 6.3, not estimable [NE]); 58% of responders had a DOR ≥6 months. In 87
patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy,
the confirmed ORR was 57% (95% CI 46, 68) with median DOR not estimable (95% CI 15.2, NE);
80% of responders had a DOR ≥6 months. The observed results are judged to be clinically
meaningful when considering the intended patient population, patients with metastatic RET
fusion-positive NSCLC, and available therapy.
For NSCLC, ORR may be considered an endpoint reasonably likely to predict clinical benefit
when the treatment effect size is large and the responses are durable (Guidance for Industry:
Clinical Trial Endpoints for the Approval of Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer Drugs and Biologics).
Based on these findings, FDA expects that pralsetinib will have, as described in section 505(d)
of the Act, “the effect it purports or is represented to have under the conditions of use
prescribed, recommended, or suggested in the labeling or proposed labeling thereof.”

Pralsetinib

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)

Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Metastatic non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) is a fatal, incurable disease, with a 5-year survival rate of <10%. The incidence of RET gene fusions
in metastatic NSCLC is 1‐2%. RET fusions are most often found in adenocarcinoma histology, and similar to some other subpopulations of
patients with targetable genomic tumor aberrations (i.e., EGFR mutation, ALK rearrangement), patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC tend to
be nonsmokers and younger than the general population of patients with NSCLC. Selpercatinib, another RET tyrosine kinase inhibitor, was
granted accelerated approval by the FDA for the treatment of patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC in May 2020. There is no
therapy with regular approval specifically for the treatment of metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC. Treatment options for patients with RET
fusion-positive NSCLC include regimens used in an unselected population of patients with NSCLC (i.e., chemotherapy and/or anti-PD-(L)1
antibody).
Pralsetinib is an inhibitor of the RET receptor tyrosine kinase. The proposed dosing regimen is 400 mg orally once daily on an empty stomach.
Blueprint’s proposed indication for pralsetinib is “treatment of patients with rearranged during transfection (RET)-positive locally advanced or
metastatic non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) as detected by an FDA-approved test.”
The primary efficacy data supporting this NDA are from a multicenter, single-arm, open-label, first-in-human, dose escalation and multicohort
expansion study in patients with thyroid cancer, NSCLC, and other advanced solid tumors (ARROW). The primary analysis population for this
review comprises 114 patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC treated with pralsetinib 400 mg once daily in ARROW, including 27
treatment-naïve patients and 87 patients who received previous treatment with platinum-based chemotherapy. The confirmed overall
response rate (ORR) assessed by blinded independent central review (BICR) in treatment-naïve patients was 70% (95% CI: 50, 86) and the
median duration of response was 9.0 months (95% CI: 6.3, not estimable [NE]), with 58% of the 19 responders having observed DOR of ≥ 6
months. In the previously platinum-treated population, the confirmed ORR per BICR was 57% (95% CI: 46, 68) and the median duration of
response was not estimable (95% CI: 15.2, NE), with 80% of the 50 responders having observed DOR of ≥ 6 months. These response rates,
coupled with the durability of responses observed, are consistent with a clinically meaningful benefit when considering the intended patient
population.
Assessment of the anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in the CNS was specified in the statistical analysis plan as a secondary endpoint. Among the

Pralsetinib appears to have an acceptable safety profile when assessed in the context of a life-threatening disease. The most common (≥ 20%)
adverse reactions were fatigue, constipation, musculoskeletal pain, hypertension, diarrhea, cough, pyrexia and edema. The most common
laboratory abnormalities (≥ 20%) worsening from baseline included increased AST, decreased hemoglobin, decreased lymphocytes, decreased
neutrophils, increased ALT, increased creatinine, increased alkaline phosphatase, decreased calcium, decreased sodium, decreased phosphate,
and decreased platelets. Permanent discontinuation of pralsetinib due to adverse reactions occurred in 15% of patients; the most frequent
adverse reactions leading to permanent discontinuation were pneumonitis (1.8%), pneumonia (1.8%), and sepsis (1%). Safety issues identified
as significant and serious during the NDA review were interstitial lung disease/pneumonitis, hypertension, hepatotoxicity, and hemorrhagic
events. These safety concerns are adequately addressed by information in the Warnings and Precautions section and the dose modification
recommendations included in product labeling. Risk of impaired wound healing was also included in the Warnings and Precautions section of
labeling given the potential for pralsetinib to adversely affect wound healing based on inhibition of the vascular endothelial growth factor
(VEGF) signaling pathway. Based on the potential serious risk of GI perforations/fistulas related to this same mechanism of action, the approval
will include a post-marketing requirement (PMR) to further evaluate and characterize this potential risk. There were no significant safety
concerns identified during NDA review requiring risk management beyond labeling or warranting consideration for a Risk Evaluation and
Mitigation Strategy (REMS).
A supplemental premarket application (sPMA) for a companion diagnostic (Oncomine™ Dx Target Test [ODxTT]) was submitted by Life
Technologies Corporation (ThermoFisher Scientific) for contemporaneous review with this NDA.
In the opinion of the reviewers, the submitted evidence meets the statutory evidentiary standard for accelerated approval. Pralsetinib has a
favorable benefit-risk profile in the indicated population based on the high response rate and durable responses observed in a patient
population with a life-threatening disease and an unmet medical need. Given the relatively limited duration of follow-up for both cohorts and
the small number of treatment-naïve patients in the primary efficacy analysis population for this application, the current data are considered

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Lung cancer is the most common cause of cancer death worldwide with an estimated
1.8 million deaths per year and is by far responsible for the largest proportion of total cancer
deaths (18.4%) compared to any other type (World Health Organization, 2019). In the US, lung
cancer is the leading cause of cancer death, and in 2020, it is estimated that 228,820 (116,300
in men and 112,520 in women) cases of lung and bronchial cancer will be diagnosed, and
135,720 deaths (72,500 in men and 53,220 in women) are estimated to occur because of the
disease (Siegel et al, 2020). Survival rates in the US in this patient population are poor. Most
patients with NSCLC present with advanced stage unresectable disease (ESMO Guidelines,
2019) and, if untreated, these patients are expected to die within an average of 9.4 months of
diagnosis; only 18% of all patients with lung cancer are alive 5 years or more after diagnosis
(Campbell et al, 2018; NCCN, 2020). In addition, the disease itself is associated with many
symptoms that need to be managed (e.g., cough, dyspnea, weight loss, chest pain, chronic
obstructive pulmonary disease, bone pain, headaches, anemia, and paraneoplastic syndromes)
(Simoff et al, 2013; Spiro et al, 2007). Pneumonia and pneumonitis are commonly seen in lung
cancer patients and the underlying disease is a confounding factor for these events.
The 2 main categories of lung cancer are NSCLC (approximately 85% of lung cancers) and small
cell lung cancer (Zappa and Mousa, 2016). NSCLC subtypes are adenocarcinoma (most common
among men and women in the US), squamous cell carcinoma (25% to 30% of all lung cancer),
and large cell carcinoma (5% to 10% of lung cancer) (Zappa and Mousa, 2016). Based on
differences in available therapeutic options and other disease characteristics, clinicians also
further categorize patients with NSCLC as having either nonsquamous (i.e., adenocarcinoma,
large cell carcinoma) or squamous cell carcinoma (Travis et al, 2011). Patients with NSCLC are
likely to present with symptoms such as persistent cough, chest pain, wheezing, dyspnoea,
haemoptysis, and weight loss (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020). Diagnosis in the majority
of cases is in patients aged 65 years and older.
Over the course of the last 2 decades, the development of genetic testing has resulted in the
identification of genetic alterations that play key roles as oncogenic drivers and predictors of
responses to therapy in lung cancer. Approximately 75% of lung adenocarcinomas harbor
genetic alterations that promote the RTK/RAS/RAF signaling pathway including drivers such as
KRAS, EGFR, ALK, ROS1, BRAF, MET, NTRK, and RET, among others (Inamura, 2017; Rosell and
Karachaliou, 2016).
26
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
RET is an RTK expressed in several neural, neuroendocrine, and genitourinary tissue types that
normally requires ligand and co-receptor binding for activation (Mulligan, 2014). Aberrant
activation of RET is a critical driver of tumor growth and proliferation across a broad number of
solid tumors (Mulligan, 2014). Oncogenic activation of RET can occur by 2 primary mechanisms
(Drilon et al, 2018). First, chromosomal rearrangements can produce hybrid proteins that fuse
the RET kinase domain with a partner protein that often contains a dimerization domain (Kohno
et al, 2012; Lipson et al, 2012; Romei et al, 2016; Takeuchi et al, 2012). Second, point mutations
can directly or indirectly activate the kinase (Donis-Keller et al, 1993; Hofstra et al, 1994;
Mulligan et al, 1993). Both mechanisms of oncogenic activation result in constitutively active,
ligand-independent RET kinase activity and activation of downstream signaling pathways
(Drilon et al, 2018).
As the prevalence of tumor genomic sequencing has increased, it has become apparent that
oncogenic RET alterations occur in a variety of solid tumors. RET fusions as a result of
chromosomal rearrangement have been found in tumors including NSCLC, papillary thyroid
cancer, colon, breast, pancreatic, ovary, salivary carcinomas, and inflammatory myofibroblastic
tumors, and suggest that RET fusions are oncogenic in many tumor types (Antonescu et al,
2015; Ballerini et al, 2012; Kang et al, 2017; Kohno et al, 2012; Lipson et al, 2012; Medico et al,
2015; Santoro et al, 1992; Stransky et al, 2014; Takeuchi et al, 2012; Wang et al, 2016).
Oncogenic RET fusions have been identified in 1% to 2% of NSCLC and the RET fusions are
typically found in adenocarcinoma histology (though occasionally squamous) (Lin et al, 2015;
Takeuchi, 2019). These RET-fusion positive NSCLC tumors tend to lack mutations in other
known driver genes and are commonly found in a younger population of nonsmokers (Jordan et
al, 2017; Lin et al, 2015; Lipson et al, 2012; Michels et al, 2016; Stransky et al, 2014; Takeuchi et
al, 2012; Tsuta et al, 2014). Activating RET mutations have also been observed at high
frequency (90%) of advanced MTC as well as a variety of other solid tumors, including ovarian,
colorectal, and breast cancer (Kato et al, 2017; Romei et al, 2018).
The FDA’s Assessment:
As the Applicant notes, RET gene fusions have been identified in 1 to 2 % of NSCLC and RET
fusions are commonly found in adenocarcinoma histology, younger population and
nonsmokers. In addition, FDA notes that RET fusions in lung adenocarcinoma have also been
found to be associated with poor differentiation, solid subtype, and smaller T stage (≤ 3 cm)
with N2 disease (Wang R. et al.). The most common RET fusions in lung cancer (>80%) are
kinesin family members 5B (KIF5B)-RET followed by CCDC6, NCOA4, TRIM33, CLIPl, and ERCl
(Drilon A et al. and Kohno T et al.). RET fusion is usually mutually exclusive to the mutations or
rearrangements of other genes, including EGFR, KRAS, ALK, and ERBB2 (Kohno T et al.).

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
As genetic alterations became known, targeted therapies focusing on the specific molecular
drivers have been developed to treat patients in a more individualized manner (Rosell and
Karachaliou, 2016; Sequist and Neal, 2020). For patients with tumors harboring specific
oncogenic drivers such as ALK, EGFR, BRAF, and ROS1, several targeted therapies (including
erlotinib, gefitinib, afatinib, dacomitinib, osimertinib, crizotinib, brigatinib, ceritinib, alectinib)
have demonstrated significant improvements in outcomes in randomized clinical trials,
achieving ORRs of 50% to 83% and median PFS of 8 to 26 months, compared to 16% to 45% and
5 to 8 months for standard platinum-based chemotherapeutic regimens (Camidge et al, 2018;
Mok et al, 2009; Peters et al, 2017; Ramalingam et al, 2016; Rosell et al, 2012; Sequist et al,
2013; Shaw et al, 2016; Solomon et al, 2014; Soria et al, 2017; Soria et al, 2018). Accordingly,
some of the most widely followed treatment guidelines routinely call for molecular testing and
prefer first-line therapy consisting of targeted agents for patients with actionable oncogenic
drivers (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020).
Yet, despite the strong evidence that RET is an oncogenic driver in 1% to 2% of NSCLC patients,
there are no approved therapies that selectively and potently target RET alterations in any of
the solid tumors in which RET has been identified. Although many of the MKIs (cabozantinib,
vandetanib, sunitinib, sorafenib, alectinib, lenvatinib, nintedanib, ponatinib, regorafenib) have
some limited RET inhibitory activity, when these were studied in RET-fusion positive NSCLC
patients, they were found to be inferior (ORR of 16% to 47% and median PFS of 2.3 to
7.3 months) to the responses and survival outcomes seen with the selective TKIs in other
oncogene-driven NSCLC forms (Drilon et al, 2013; Drilon et al, 2016; Gautschi et al, 2017; Lee et
al, 2017; Mazières et al, 2019; Offin et al, 2019; Yoh et al, 2017). Additionally, the studies with
these MKIs were often small and thus hard to interpret (ESMO Guidelines, 2019), and none of
these targeted agents are specifically approved for RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients.
Due to the lack of available therapies specifically targeting RET, diagnostic testing for RET is not
yet standard of care, and RET-fusion positive patients with advanced NSCLC are treated in
accordance with guidelines for oncogene driver-negative nonsquamous NSCLC (ESMO
Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020). According to these guidelines, recommended first-line therapy
in oncogene driver-negative NSCLC consists of cytotoxic platinum doublet chemotherapy with
or without immune checkpoint inhibition or bevacizumab, and second line therapy involves the
use of a checkpoint inhibitor or cytotoxic chemotherapy. Subsequent therapy for refractory
patients is best supportive care or enrollment in a clinical trial.
However, the use of these treatment options in EGFR- and ALK-negative patients with advanced
NSCLC and a nonsquamous histology (i.e., in-patient populations similar to the RET-positive
patients) does not provide the same level of efficacy typically achieved with targeted therapies
for oncogenic drivers. In the first-line treatment setting, intravenous platinum-doublet cytotoxic
28

chemotherapy of EGFR- and ALK-negative patients with advanced NSCLC is associated with a
response rate of only 15% to 31% and a median PFS of 4.5 to 6.5 months for patients with
nonsquamous histology (Gandhi et al, 2018; Hellmann et al, 2018; Mok et al, 2019; Sandler et
al, 2006; Scagliotti et al, 2008). Addition of intravenous PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitors (pembrolizumab
or atezolizumab) to the platinum-based chemotherapy in a population of patients with
nonsquamous histology unselected for PD-L1 expression increased the ORR to 46% to 48% with
a median PFS of 7.0 to 8.8 months (Gandhi et al, 2018; West et al, 2019), and for patients with
squamous histology, pembrolizumab plus platinum-based chemotherapy increased the
response rate from 38% to 58% with a median PFS from 4.8 to 6.4 months (Paz-Ares et al,
2018); but these responses are still not as high as the ORRs achieved with driver-targeted
therapies in patients with tumors harboring specific oncogenes. Pembrolizumab is also
approved as monotherapy (regardless of histology) in the first-line setting, but only for patients
with NSCLC expressing PD-L1 levels of 50% or more and who are negative for EGFR, ALK, ROS1,
and BRAF V600E; an ORR of 39% to 45% and median PFS of 7.1 to 10.3 months were reported in
this population (Mok et al, 2019; Reck et al, 2016). In patients with TPS levels ≥ 1%, the survival
advantage with pembrolizumab monotherapy was similar to that with platinum-based
chemotherapy in ORR (27% for both arms) and PFS (5.4 months for pembrolizumab and
6.5 months for chemotherapy) (Mok et al, 2019). Another first-line treatment option for
patients without contraindications is bevacizumab plus platinum-based chemotherapy (with or
without atezolizumab); however, although this regimen has demonstrated survival
improvements, it is associated with an increased risk of treatment-related bleeding events and
death (Sandler et al, 2006; Socinski et al, 2018).
Second-line treatment options for nonsquamous and/or squamous NSCLC are not much better.
Single-agent checkpoint inhibition in this setting results in an ORR of 14% to 20% and a PFS of
2.3 to 5.2 months (Borghaei et al, 2015; Brahmer et al, 2015; Fehrenbacher et al, 2018; Herbst
et al, 2016). Single-agent treatment with docetaxel produced ORRs of 9% to 14% and a median
PFS of 2.8 to 4.2 months (Borghaei et al, 2015; Brahmer et al, 2015; Fehrenbacher et al, 2018;
Garon et al, 2014; Hanna et al, 2004). The combination of docetaxel with ramucirumab,
gemcitabine, vinorelbine produced ORRs of between 7% and 23% with a median PFS of
between 2.8 and 4.5 months (Garon et al, 2014; Takeda et al, 2009; William et al, 2010).
Furthermore, although immune checkpoint inhibition is now recommended for the treatment
of NSCLC patients without a known oncogenic driver, the NCCN guidelines state that the
presence of an oncogenic driver is a contraindication for treatment with PD-L1 inhibitors
(NCCN, 2020). Poor responses have been seen with checkpoint inhibitors in patients with
ALK/EGFR/ROS mutations (Gainor et al, 2016; Mazières et al, 2019; Miura and Sunaga, 2018) as
well as in RET-fusion positive lung cancers, where an ORR of 0% to 6.3%, a best response of PD
in 62% to 72% of patients, and PFS of 2.1 to 3.4 months have been reported (Mazières et al,
2019; Offin et al, 2019; Sabari et al, 2018; Tufman et al, 2018). This low response rate to
29
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

Pralsetinib
immunotherapy in RET-fusion positive patients is seen even in those expressing PD-L1 for a
substantial portion of the RET-fusion positive lung cancers, which may be attributed to the
overall lower tumor mutation burden in RET-fusion positive lung cancers compared to lung
cancers that do not have activation of a dominant driver (Mazières et al, 2019; Offin et al, 2019;
Sabari et al, 2018; Tufman et al, 2018).
Depending on the histology, molecular pathology, performance status, age, comorbidities, and
patient preferences, the available standard treatment options offer varying degrees of efficacy,
but all are associated with serious risks. Infections are common complications associated with
cancer treatments, which weaken the immune system. As a result, pulmonary infections
frequently develop in lung cancer patients. Myelosuppression is another well-known risk factor
for infections and is a common occurrence in patients treated with cytotoxic chemotherapeutic
regimens. Cough and dyspnoea are also expected and prominent features of NSCLC patients.
The intravenous platinum-doublet chemotherapies (cisplatin or carboplatin combined with
pemetrexed) are known to cause severe and dose-limiting nausea, vomiting, myelosuppression
(thrombocytopaenia, anaemia, and neutropaenia), extravasation reactions, nephrotoxicity,
ototoxicity, and neurotoxicity in addition to the risk of interstitial pneumonitis, alopecia, severe
embryo-fetal toxicity, and sterility (ESMO Guidelines, 2019; NCCN, 2020). Treatment with
intravenous immune checkpoint inhibitors are associated with the immune-mediated toxicity
(Postow and Wolchok, 2019). These AEs can affect multiple organ systems, causing
immune-mediated pneumonitis, colitis, hepatitis, nephritis, epidermal necrolysis, and
endocrinopathies that can be life-threatening or fatal (Jingjie et al, 2019; Raju et al, 2018; Yang
et al, 2017). As mentioned above, some of the existing MKIs that have some anti-RET activity,
while not approved for NSCLC patients, are sometimes used. While many of these TKIs have
fewer side-effects compared to platinum-based chemotherapy or immunotherapy, they are still
associated with significant toxicity due to off-target effects, particularly liver injury,
hypertension, neutropenia and leukopenia, hand-foot syndrome and QT prolongation,
pneumonitis, and gastrointestinal events (constipation, diarrhoea, and nausea) requiring early
dose interruptions and reductions (Ai et al, 2018; Chaar et al, 2018; Drilon et al, 2018; Dy and
Adjei, 2013; Zhou et al, 2015).
Thus, the currently recommended treatment options for advanced NSCLC patients with
documented RET-fusions do not offer the efficacy that has been achieved with other NSCLC
patients with identified oncogenes (such as EGFR and ALK) and existing non-targeted therapy
for these patient are associated with significant toxicity and safety risks. To date, no selective
RET-directed targeted therapies have received regulatory approval for treatment of molecularly
defined populations of patients with RET-mutant or RET-fusion solid tumors. Given the modest
overall clinical benefit of standard therapy in RET-dependent tumors, RET-fusion positive NSCLC
remains an unmet need that requires new therapeutic options, ideally that are able to

selectively and potently inhibit RET in vivo while limiting the known safety concerns associated
with other TKIs.
The Applicant’s Position:
There is currently no FDA approved treatment specifically for patients with RET-fusion positive
locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC. The modest clinical benefit and significant toxicity
observed with current non-specific treatment options in these patients highlights an area of
unmet medical need that requires new therapeutic options that are able to selectively and
potently inhibit RET.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant that RET-fusion positive, locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC is
a population of unmet medical need.
On May 8, 2020, FDA granted accelerated approval for selpercatinib (RETEVMO™, LOXO
Oncology Inc.) for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC.
Approval was based on the ORR results from a multicenter, open-label, multi-cohort clinical
trial (LIBRETTO-001, NCT03157128) in patients whose tumors had RET alterations.
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s listing of treatment options for advanced or metastatic NSCLC,
irrespective of RET-fusion status. The following tables summarize FDA-approved agents for
first- and second-line treatment of patients with advanced or metastatic NSCLC, irrespective of
RET-fusion status.

Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
Life Technologies Corporation (ThermoFisher Scientific; TFS) submitted sPMAP160045/S019 on
April 1, 2020 to CDRH to update the indication of use for the Oncomine™ Dx Target Test
(ODxTT) to include the detection of RET fusions in NSCLC to select patients for treatement with
pralsetinib.
The Oncomine™ Dx Target Test is a qualitative in vitro diagnostic test that uses targeted high
throughput, parallel-sequencing technology to detect single nucleotide variants (SNVs) and
deletions in 23 genes from DNA and fusions in ROS1 and RET from RNA isolated from FFPE
tumor samples from patients with NSCLC using the Ion PGM™ Dx System.
In Study BLUE-6667-1101, patients were required to have RET-fusion detected by local
assessment of tumor tissue and/or blood in order to be eligible. An archival or new tumor
tissue sample was required to be available for retrospective central analysis of RET gene status.
Clinical validation of the ODxT Test was performed by retrospectively testing Formalin-Fixed
Paraffin-Embedded (FFPE) NSCLC clinical samples obtained from Study BLU-667-1101 and
procured samples from commercial vendors. The purpose of the clinical validation study was to
provide evidence to demonstrate the effectiveness of ODxTT in reporting RET fusions, to aid in
identifying NSCLC patients eligible for treatment with pralsetinib.
The sPMA P160045/S019 is currently under review by the Office of In-Vitro Diagnostic and
Radiologic Health (OHT7), CDRH and is expected to be contemporaneously approved with the
approval of pralsetinib for the proposed indication.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Rearranged during transfection (RET) is a receptor tyrosine kinase that functions in the early
development of the human enteric nervous system and genitourinary tract (Mulligan 2014).
Activating RET mutations including V804 gatekeeper mutations have been observed in
medullary thyroid cancer (MTC) and multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2 (Romei Ciampi et al.
2016); oncogenic RET fusions including KIF5B-RET and CCDC6-RET have also been identified in
1-2% of patients with NSCLC (Lin, Wang et al. 2015). Pralsetinib (BLU-667 or X581238) is a small
molecule drug with an established pharmacological class of kinase inhibitor that primarily
targets wild-type (WT) RET (IC50s between 0.14 and 0.43 nM) as well as kinase-activating RET
mutations (V804L, V804M, and M918T) and the coiled-coiled domain containing 6 (CCDC6)-RET
fusion (IC50s between 0.33-0.45 nM) in biochemical activity assays at concentrations below
those achievable at the intended clinical pralsetinib dose of 400 mg (free Cmax of approximately
154 nM based on ~97.1% protein binding). In vitro, pralsetinib also inhibited the enzymatic
activity of other human kinases including DDR1, JAK1-2, TRKC, FLT3, TRKA, and PDGFRb at IC50
concentrations ≤100 nM, all lower than those achieved clinically at the 400 mg dose. Further,
despite variability in some of the in vitro assays suggesting a degree of uncertainty of the
effects of pralsetinib for these targets, pralsetinib inhibited fibroblast growth factor receptor 1
(FGFR1) in biochemical inhibition assays and vascular endothelial growth factor receptor 2
(VEGFR2) in both biochemical and cellular assays at clinically relevant concentrations. Typical
side effects associated with both FGFR and VEGFR were present in both clinical trials and
nonclinical studies providing additional support for these kinases as pralsetinib targets.
In vitro incubation of pralsetinib with Ba/F3 cells expressing fusions of KIF5B with RET or RET
variants (V804L, V804M, and V804E) or with cancer cell lines endogenously expressing RET
mutations (C634W and M918T) or CCDC6-RET resulted in reduced RET phosphorylation and
downstream signaling and/or inhibition of viability. In contrast, pralsetinib did not substantially
inhibit the viability of unmanipulated, RET-negative Ba/F3 cells. Pralsetinib exhibited in vivo
anti-tumor activity in mice bearing subcutaneously (SC)-implanted tumor xenografts bearing
RET activating mutations, including the cabozantinib resistant mutation V804L, or patientderived NSCLC and colorectal cancer (CRC) xenografts expressing KIF5B-RET and CCDC6-RET,
respectively. Further, pralsetinib improved survival in mice implanted intracranially with tumor
models expressing KIF5B-RET or CCDC6-RET, suggesting that pralsetinib may have activity
against RET-dependent CNS metastases.

40
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Pralsetinib did not inhibit in vitro hERG potassium currents at relevant concentrations or induce
QTc prolongation in the 13-week repeat-dose study in cynomolgus monkeys. Single oral
administration of ≥25 mg/kg pralsetinib resulted in increased blood pressure with concomitant
decreases in heart rate in male Sprague-Dawley rats. Increased blood pressure is attributed to
inhibition of VEGFR2, and hypertension has been seen in patients given pralsetinib.
There were no unique human metabolites detected in vitro in hepatic microsomes or
hepatocytes and no major metabolites detected in humans at levels ≥10% of radioactivity that
might require further toxicological assessment. Following single oral administration, 14C­
pralsetinib-derived radioactivity distributed widely to rat tissues especially the small intestine
wall, liver, adrenal gland, uveal tract, and pigmented skin. Pralsetinib crossed the blood-brain
barrier in rats and may be present at concentrations expected to inhibit RET at the
recommended dose of 400 mg.
The Applicant evaluated the safety of pralsetinib using Sprague-Dawley rats and Cynomolgus
monkeys in GLP-compliant repeat-dose toxicology studies of up to 13 weeks duration. There
were no mortalities in the 13-week rat and monkey studies at doses up to 20 mg/kg/day (120
mg/m2/day) and 10 mg/kg/day (120 mg/m2/day), respectively. Exposure increases, particularly
AUC increases were generally greater than dose proportional, especially in rats; the greater
than dose proportional increase occurred in all reviewed repeat-dose rat studies, including
embryo-fetal development studies. Target organs in both species included the bone marrow,
with findings of decreased cellularity histopathologically consistent with decreases in red blood
cells (RBCs) and mild decreases in white blood cells (WBCs), kidney (decreased weight and
tubular basophilia/mineralization), and lung (macrophage aggregation). Additional histologic
findings in the 28-day toxicology studies for both species included hemorrhage and necrosis in
the heart (although necrosis was associated with sepsis in monkeys). Hyperphosphatemia and
tissue mineralization were also common findings in animals and may be related to FGFR
inhibition. In rats there was also some evidence of liver toxicity including mild increases in
enzymes (ALT/ALP/AST), decreased liver weight, and focal necrosis. In monkeys, the
gastrointestinal (GI) tract was an additional target organ with intussusception in both studies as
well as extensive GI inflammation and/or ulceration leading to bacterial sepsis and death in the
28-day study at the dose levels ≥15 mg/kg (≥1.1 times the clinical AUC of 43900 ng*hr/mL).
Monkeys also had additional lung findings of pleural fibrosis, and bronchioalveolar
hyperplasia/metaplasia in the 28-day study at the 15 mg/kg dose level. Consistent with
nonclinical findings, anemia, decreased WBCs, hyperphosphatemia, and increased AST/ALT
have been seen clinically and the GAVRETO label includes a warning for pneumonitis.
Bone (increased physeal thickness in the sternum and femur) and teeth (incisor fractures and
histologic dentin matrix alteration, ameloblast/odontoblast degeneration, mixed cell
inflammation, odontoblast necrosis) findings were present in rats at exposures approximately

0.9 times the clinical AUC of 43900 ng*hr/mL (confirmed by the FDA clinical pharmacology
team) at the 400 mg human dose and may be related to VEGFR/FGFR inhibition. Although
similar toxicities were not seen in the 13-week monkey study, treatment with 15/7.5 mg/kg
pralsetinib in the 28-day study resulted in physeal dysplasia in the femur of monkeys. Recovery
was not assessed in the 13-week studies, but increased physeal thickness in the femur and
incisor degeneration did not show evidence of complete recovery in the 28-day rat study. The
Applicant has not reported drug-related fractures or tooth abnormalities in clinical trials and
pralsetinib-induced physeal dysplasia and teeth findings are unlikely to be relevant to human
adults with closed growth plates; however, these findings may be relevant in pediatric patients
and are included in Section 8.4 of the label.
The Applicant did not conduct carcinogenicity or fertility studies to support the currently
proposed indication (NSCLC), and these studies are not needed to support a marketing
application for a drug intended to treat patients with advanced cancer. Oral administration of
pralsetinib in general toxicology studies resulted in dose-related findings in reproductive organs
that may impair male and female fertility. In the 13-week rat toxicology study, treatment with
pralsetinib at dose levels ≥10 mg/kg (approximately 0.9 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg
dose) resulted in degeneration of the corpus luteum in the ovary; cellular debris and reduced
sperm in the epididymis; tubular degeneration/atrophy in the testis correlating with small/soft
testis; and reduced epididymis and testis weights. These results suggest some potential for
reduced fertility in people. Pralsetinib was not mutagenic in an in vitro bacterial reverse
mutation (Ames) assay or clastogenic in either the in vitro micronucleus assay in TK6 cells or the
in vivo bone marrow micronucleus assay in rats. In addition, pralsetinib did not demonstrate
phototoxic potential in an in vitro neutral red uptake phototoxicity assay.
The Applicant conducted a GLP-compliant dose range-finding embryo-fetal development study
in Sprague-Dawley rats. Because this study demonstrated clear findings at exposure levels
below the clinical exposure at the 400 mg human dose, this study was sufficient to characterize
the potential developmental risk. Once daily oral administration of pralsetinib to pregnant rats
during the period of organogenesis (gestation Days 6 to 17) resulted in 100% post-implantation
loss (early resorptions) at doses ≥20 mg/kg (approximately 1.5 times the clinical AUC at the 400
mg dose) accompanied by reduced maternal body weight/food consumption and reduced
gravid uterine weight. The reduced maternal body weight appeared to be a result of fetal loss
rather than maternal toxicity. Post-implantation loss and decreased gravid uterine weight were
also observed in one rat at the 10 mg/kg dose level. At pralsetinib doses ≥5 mg/kg
(approximately 0.2 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg dose) there was an increase in fetal
visceral malformations (absent kidney and ureter, absent/narrow uterine horn, malpositioned
kidney/testis, retroesophageal aortic arch), skeletal malformations (vertebral anomaly with or
without associated rib anomaly, vertebral centra/rib/costal cartilage anomalies), visceral
variations [renal papilla(e) not developed and/or distended ureter(s), small kidney/thymus,

renal malformations/variations were consistent with the role of RET in renal organogenesis. The
pharmacology/toxicology team recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in the label for
GAVRETO. Consistent with the recommendations for teratogenic or embryo-lethal but non­
genotoxic drugs described in the FDA Guidance for Industry Oncology Pharmaceuticals:
Reproductive Toxicity Testing and Labeling Recommendations, and considering the pralsetinib
steady-state half-life of 22.2 hours, FDA recommends advising males of reproductive potential
to use effective contraception during treatment with GAVRETO and for 1 week after the final
dose. Given that the FDA clinical pharmacology team identified pralsetinib to be a CYP3A
inducer, which may compromise the efficacy of hormonal contraception, FDA recommends
advising females of reproductive potential to use effective non-hormonal contraception during
treatment with GAVRETO and for 2 weeks after the final dose to account for the total duration
of potential CYP3A induction (i.e. 14 days) after the final dose. The Applicant did not evaluate
the presence of pralsetinib in milk; however, because of potential adverse effects of GAVRETO
on a breastfeeding child, the review team recommends advising patients not to breastfeed
during treatment with GAVRETO and for 1 week after the final dose. There are no outstanding
issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective that would prevent the approval of
GAVRETO for the treatment of adult patients with metastatic RET-fusion positive NSCLC as
detected by an FDA approved test.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
The Applicant’s Position:
There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology
for pralsetinib.

Pharmacology
Primary pharmacology- Completed by FDA
A. In Vitro Studies
The Applicant investigated the ability of pralsetinib (BLU-667) to bind to 468 kinases in an in
vitro KINOMEscan® screen (Studies #BLU-005-03-s and #BLU-005-04-p). At a concentration of
1000 nM, pralsetinib prevented ligand binding (>90% inhibition) for ~10% of kinases in the
panel including RET and the RET mutations M918T, V8044L, and V804M. Binding affinity was
subsequently determined for 31 WT kinases. Pralsetinib exhibited the highest binding affinity
for RET (KD = 0.39 nM) and JAK2 (KD = 0.52 nM) and also bound to FGFR1, FLT1/VEGFR1, FLT3,
JAK1, JAK3, TRKA, and TRKC with KD values ≤10 nM. Pralsetinib bound to an additional 17
kinases with KD values ≤160 nM. In an in vitro kinase screen, incubation with 300 nM pralsetinib
inhibited the enzymatic activity of 22 out of the 374 human kinases tested by >50% (Study
#BPM-0022). Follow-up assays determined that pralsetinib was the most selective for RET (IC50

IC50 concentrations lower than those achieved clinically at the 400 mg dose level of pralsetinib
(free Cmax at the 400 mg clinical dose is approximately 154 nM based on ~97.1% protein

and FGFR1 at IC50 values of 144-160 nM.

The Applicant further characterized the in vitro activity of pralsetinib in biochemical activity
assays (Study #BPM-0015). At IC50 concentrations lower than those achieved clinically at the
400 mg dose level (approximately 154 nM) pralsetinib inhibited the kinase activity of WT RET as
well as the V804L, V804M, and M918T kinase-activating RET mutations identified in MTC and
the CCDC6-RET oncogenic fusion observed in papillary thyroid cancer and NSCLC . Although
several approved multi-kinase inhibitors with RET activity (in particular vandetanib) generally
exhibited reduced in vitro activity against the V804L and V804M mutations in the gate-keeper
position of the RET kinase domain compared to WT RET, pralsetinib inhibited WT RET, V804L
RET, and V804M RET with similar IC50 values (Table 6). Pralsetinib also inhibited recombinant

The Applicant evaluated the effects of pralsetinib on in vitro RET signaling in cellular assays
(Study #BPM-0016). As assessed by immunoblotting, incubation with ≥10 nM pralsetinib for 90
minutes reduced the phosphorylation of RET (Y1062/Y905) and the downstream signaling
protein Shc (Y239/240) in Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-RET (data not shown), the most
prevalent RET fusion protein in NSCLC. AlphaLISA demonstrated that pralsetinib inhibited RET
autophosphorylation in Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-RET with an IC50 of 5 nM compared to
higher IC50 values seen with the approved kinase inhibitors cabozantinib (IC50 = 61.9 nM),
vandetanib (IC50 = 833.1 nM), and alectinib (IC50 = 522.3 nM).
To evaluate the effect of pralsetinib on in vitro cell proliferation, investigators incubated Ba/F3
cells expressing KIF5B-RET or KIF5B-RET variants (V804L, V804M, and V804E) isolated from a
cabozantinib resistance screen with pralsetinib for 48 hours and then measured cell viability
using the CellTiter-Glo Assay. As shown in Table 7, pralsetinib inhibited the in vitro viability of
Ba/F3 cells expressing WT KIF5B-RET and all three RET mutations tested at clinically relevant
concentrations, but did not substantially inhibit the viability of RET-negative Ba/F3 cells (IC50 =
1873.1 nM; data not shown). The approved kinase inhibitors cabozantinib, vandetanib, and
alectinib did not substantially inhibit the proliferation of Ba/F3 cells expressing KIF5B-V804L,
KIF5B-V804M, or KIF5B-V804E. The Applicant also evaluated the activity of pralsetinib in cancer
cell lines that endogenously express activating RET fusions or mutations (Study #BPM-0017).
Pralsetinib inhibited the phosphorylation of RET (Y1062), Shc, and ERK1/2 and decreased mRNA
expression of the RET-dependent target genes DUSP6 and SPRY4 in LC2/ad NSCLC cells
expressing CCDC6-RET, TT MTC cells expressing RET C634W, and MZ-CRC-1 MTC cells expressing
RET M918T (data not shown). As measured by BrdU incorporation, incubation with pralsetinib
for 48 hours inhibited the proliferation of LC2/ad, TT, and MZ-CRC-1 cells with IC50 values of 3.7­
15.4 nM (Table 7).

The Applicant investigated the in vivo anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in several
subcutaneously-implanted tumor xenograft models expressing activating RET fusions or
mutations (Study #CPB-P15-5515, CPB-P16-5665, CPB-P16-5645, and E0400-U1608). When the
mean tumor volume was ~151-239 mm3, nude mice began receiving vehicle, pralsetinib, or
cabozantinib. Pralsetinib exhibited in vivo anti-tumor activity against Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET, TT MTC
(C634W RET), and CR2518 CRC (CCDC6-RET) xenografts at dose levels ≥3 mg/kg twice daily and
≥20 mg/kg once daily (Table 8) without inducing significant toxicity based on mortality or body
weight loss. Cabozantinib exhibited similar anti-tumor activity as pralsetinib at the same dose
level (60 mg/kg once daily) in the TT and CR2518 xenograft models but was not well tolerated
in the Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET and TT xenograft models, resulting in early mortalities. Notably,
pralsetinib exhibited in vivo anti-tumor activity at dose levels ≥10 mg/kg twice daily and 20
mg/kg once daily against cabozantinib-resistant Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET (V804L) xenografts.

Further, oral administration of ≥3 mg/kg twice daily and ≥20 mg/kg once daily pralsetinib
resulted in dose-dependent inhibition of RET (Y1062) and Shc (Y239/240) phosphorylation in
Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET and Ba/F3-KIF5B-RET (V804L) xenografts compared to vehicle beginning at 4
hours post-dose (Study #BPM-0020). Across five experiments, the IC50, IC75, and IC90 values for
in vivo inhibition of RET phosphorylation were 117 ng/mL, 299 ng/mL, and 769 ng/mL,
respectively. Approximately 90% inhibition of RET phosphorylation for 12 hours correlated with
complete TGI in these RET-driven Ba/F3 xenograft models.
The Applicant investigated the in vivo anti-tumor activity of pralsetinib in female BALB/c nude
mice (8/group) inoculated intracranially in the right frontal lobe of the brain with Ba/F3-KIF5B­
RET-luc cells (Study #CPB-P18-21802). Twice daily oral administration of ≥10 mg/kg pralsetinib
inhibited intracranial tumor growth (data not shown) and improved survival compared to
vehicle (Figure 2A). Median survival for mice dosed with 0, 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg pralsetinib twice
daily was 16.5, 17, 34, and >42 days, respectively. Similarly, twice daily oral administration of
≥10 mg/kg pralsetinib inhibited intracranial tumor growth (data not shown) and improved
survival compared to vehicle (Figure 2B) in the intracranial orthotopic CR2518 (CCDC6-RET) CRC
PDX model in female BALB/c nude mice (Study #E0400-U1804). Taken together, these data
suggest that pralsetinib may have activity against RET-dependent CNS metastases.

Secondary Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
The applicant conducted stand-alone in vitro secondary pharmacology studies with pralsetinib.
Pralsetinib was demonstrated to be highly selective against over 450 kinases, as well as against
a panel of 55 transmembrane or soluble receptors, ion channels, and monoamine transporters
(Report 100023499, 100023915). In this latter study, > 50% inhibition of 5-HT2A and Na+ channel
site 2 occurred at a 10 µM screening concentration. The clinical significance of this is unknown.
There were no apparent 5-HT2A or Na+ channel site 2 effects in GLP-compliant, repeated-dose
toxicology studies in rats and monkeys.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See the Primary Pharmacology section above for FDA’s review of in vitro kinase screens
investigating pralsetinib binding to 468 kinases (Studies #BLU-005-03-s and #BLU-005-04-p) and
enzymatic activity against 374 kinases (Study #BPM-0022). Although pralsetinib was the most
selective for RET in these screens, pralsetinib also bound to and/or inhibited other kinases at
clinically relevant concentrations.
The Applicant evaluated off-target activity of pralsetinib on 55 receptors, transporters, and
enzymes in Study #100023499/100023915 using in vitro binding assays. Incubation with 10 µM
pralsetinib resulted in >50% inhibition of binding to 5-HT2A(h) and Na+ channel site 2. Follow-up
studies determined IC50 values of 3.6 µM and 3.4 μM for 5-HT2A(h) and Na+ channel site 2,
respectively. Given these high concentrations relative to the clinical free concentration, these
targets are unlikely to be clinically significant for pralsetinib.

Safety Pharmacology
The Applicant’s Position:
The applicant conducted stand-alone in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology studies to assess
the potential effects of pralsetinib on cardiovascular function.
A stand-alone in vitro safety pharmacology study was conducted to assess the potential for
pralsetinib to prolong the QT interval (Report CPB-25-15-010A-0169). The half-maximal
inhibitory concentration for the inhibitory effect of pralsetinib on human ether à go go-related
gene channel potassium current was 5.18 µM (Hill coefficient = 0.92), suggesting a low
potential for QT prolongation. This low level of concern corresponds with negative ECG findings
in the GLP-compliant 28-day and 13-week repeated-dose toxicology studies in male and female
monkeys.
A stand-alone in vivo safety pharmacology study was conducted in conscious
radiotelemetry-instrumented rats, where a single oral pralsetinib dose of either 10 mg/kg/day
or 25 mg/kg/day were given orally. In this study a mild increase in blood pressure was
observed, with a corresponding decrease in HR. This increase in blood pressure was attributed
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
to VEGFR inhibition (Report
-124581,
-124606). The no-observed-effect level in this
study was 10 mg/kg (60 mg/m2).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions. The IC50 value of 5.18 µM (n=2)
determined in the non-GLP in vitro hERG channel study (Study #CPB-25-15-010A-0169) suggests
minimal risk of QT prolongation through this pathway in humans given pralsetinib. Though the
Applicant did not submit a definitive GLP-compliant in vitro study, the available GLP-compliant
safety studies have provided sufficient safety data along with this supportive study.
In non-GLP Study #
-124581, male Sprague-Dawley rats (6/group) received single doses of 0,
50, or 200 mg/kg pralsetinib; heart rate, arterial blood pressure (systolic, diastolic, and mean),
pulse pressure, and body temperature were measured continuously for at least 2 hours pre­
(b) (4)

cardiovascular parameters were measured continuously for at least 24 hours pre-dosing
through 24 hours post-dosing. Single oral administration of ≥25 mg/kg pralsetinib resulted in
increased mean systolic, diastolic, and mean blood pressure compared to baseline and/or
controls with concomitant decreases in heart rate. Single oral administration of 200 mg/kg
pralsetinib resulted in decreased mean body temperature compared to baseline and control.
Increased blood pressure did not show evidence of recovery during the 24-hour monitoring
period at dose levels ≥50 mg/kg. Single oral administration of 10 mg/kg pralsetinib did not

result in drug-related effects on the cardiovascular parameters tested. ECG parameters were
not assessed in these studies, but were included in the repeat-dose toxicology studies in
monkeys.

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
Pralsetinib was well-absorbed based on the high oral bioavailability (~100%) observed in rat,
dog, and monkey. Maximum plasma concentrations were reached after ~2 hours. Plasma
clearance was low, with a t1/2 of ~3.5 hours. The Vss was moderate and was equal to or up to
4-fold higher than the volume of total body water, suggesting extensive distribution of
pralsetinib into extravascular tissues. Exposure to pralsetinib after repeated oral dosing was
more than dose-proportional in rats and nearly dose-proportional in monkeys, with no clinically
relevant sex differences. Pralsetinib was highly bound to plasma proteins in vitro and widely
distributed to a majority of tissues in vivo, including the brain. Pralsetinib was metabolized in
the liver primarily by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II) and eliminated mainly via the
hepatobiliary route and active secretion through gut wall in rats. Pralsetinib has the potential to
cause PK DDIs via induction and inhibition of P450 enzymes and drug transporters.
Absorption
The absolute oral bioavailability of pralsetinib in rat, dog, and monkey was high, which was
consistent with the cellular permeability comparable to that with drugs that exhibit moderate
to good oral absorption in human. While pralsetinib was both inhibitor and substrate of P-gp
and BCRP, efflux transport in the gut by these transporters does not appear to limit oral
bioavailability (Report 19BLUPP1R1).
Following a single oral dose of 10 mg/kg (rats) or 1 mg/kg (dogs and monkeys), bioavailability of
pralsetinib was ~100% in rats, dogs, and monkeys (Reports CPB-P15-10033R02,
CPB-P15-10204D03, CPB-P15-10204K04). The absorption was rapid with Tmax of 2.0 hours in
monkeys and dogs and 2.7 hours in rats. Mean Cmax was lowest in monkeys (396 ng/mL) and
highest in rats (2345 ng/mL) and mean AUC0–last was lowest in monkeys (2917 h•ng/mL), and
similar in dogs (16,910 h•ng/mL) and rats (18,797 h•ng/mL).
Following a single intravenous administration of 1 mg/kg (rats) or 0.5 mg/kg (dogs and
monkeys), mean plasma clearance of pralsetinib was low in rats (14.6 mL/min/kg), dogs
(2.0 mL/min/kg), and monkeys (6.5 mL/min/kg), corresponding to ~26%, 6.4%, and 15% of the
hepatic blood flow, respectively (Reports CPB-P15-10033R02, CPB-P15-10082D01, CPB-P15­
10082K01). Mean t1/2 was 3.5 hours in rats and dogs and 3.7 hours in monkeys. Mean AUC0–last
was 1137 h•ng/mL in rats, 1389 h•ng/mL in monkeys, and 4184 h•ng/mL in dogs. Mean Vss was
moderate (ranged from 0.5 L/kg in dogs to 3.3 L/kg in rats), suggesting an extensive distribution
to tissues.

Bioavailability did not appear to be calculated in the monkey or dog study reports, but FDA
agrees that it is approximately 100% based on AUC values. P-gp and BCRP studies were
reviewed by the clinical pharmacology team rather than the nonclinical team; see Section 6 for
FDA’s assessment of these studies.
Toxicokinetic Data from General Toxicology Studies
Repeated oral administration of pralsetinib for 7 days (male rats, dogs, and monkeys) and
28 days and 13 weeks (male and female rats, monkeys) resulted in exposure to pralsetinib (Cmax
and AUC0–24) that increased more than dose-proportionally in rats and dogs (Reports

The observed sex differences are specific to rats and may not manifest in humans. No sex
differences in exposure were noted in monkeys. In rats, accumulation of pralsetinib in plasma
was minor after 28 days of dosing (< 2-fold) and notable after 13 weeks of dosing (2.32- to

FDA Additional Comment on Toxicokinetic Data from General Toxicology Studies:
FDA did not review the 7-day toxicology studies in rats, dogs, or monkeys because they are not
relevant for this stage of development. Thus, the Applicant’s conclusions from these studies
were not verified by the FDA. See Section 5.5.1 General Toxicology for FDA’s review of the
toxicokinetic data from the 13-week toxicology studies.
Toxicokinetic Data from Embryofetal Toxicology Study
Following repeated oral administration of pralsetinib to pregnant rats, the increase in maternal
exposure (Cmax and AUC0–24) was greater than dose-proportional on Gestation Days 6 and 17
(Report 00124766). Accumulation was minor (< 2-fold) except for 5 mg/kg/day dose (2.5-fold).
Distribution
In vitro, pralsetinib was highly bound (≥ 95%) to plasma proteins in mouse, rat, dog, monkey,
and human plasma (Report 1905093), with unbound fraction in human plasma at 2.9%.
Pralsetinib did not show preferential distribution to red blood cells in all nonclinical species, as
well as in humans and was stable in human plasma and blood. Together, these data suggest
that plasma is an appropriate matrix for measuring systemic exposure of pralsetinib (Report
CPB-P15-10118).
Pralsetinib distributed widely to majority of tissues in rats, including the brain (Report Key
1598). Although pralsetinib showed potential to bind to melanin, no specific toxic effects in

melanin-containing tissues were observed in the GLP-compliant repeated-dose toxicology

There was a high unbound concentration of pralsetinib in the striatal interstitial fluid of rats,

concentration of pralsetinib in the brain at the therapeutic doses in humans is predicted to be
high enough to inhibit RET protein expressed by metastatic NSCLC cells in the brain.
FDA Additional Comment on Brain Distribution:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion that pralsetinib distributed to the rat brain in Study
#1598, but also points out that 14C-pralsetinib-derived radioactivity was below the lower limit of
quantitation (BQL) following single oral administration of 30 mg/kg 14C-pralsetinib to rats in
Study #00124834. Overall FDA agrees that pralsetinib crosses the blood-brain barrier in rats and
may be present at concentrations expected to inhibit RET.
Metabolism
The in vitro metabolite profile of pralsetinib was generally comparable across mouse, rat, dog,
monkey, and human (Report CPB-P15-10033). In vivo, all human metabolites were present in
either rats and/or monkeys (Reports BLU-R5482AM1, BLU-R9705).
In vitro, pralsetinib metabolism in human primary hepatocytes and liver microsomes is mainly
mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4 (Phase II) (Report BLU-R9667). The proposed
metabolic pathway in vitro and in vivo includes oxidation/hydroxylation of the pralsetinib
pyrazolpyridine ring system (M549a and M449c), oxidation of cyclohexyl moiety (M549c), direct
N-glucuronidation (M709), and glutathione conjugation with or without defluorination (Reports
BLU-R9667, BLU-R5482AM1, BLU-R9705). Metabolite M709 generated from direct
N-glucuronidation of pralsetinib appeared as the major metabolite observed in human primary
hepatocytes.
FDA Additional Comment on Metabolism:
Human metabolism, including CYP3A4 and UGT1A4 metabolism studies, was reviewed in detail
by FDA’s clinical pharmacology team; see Section 6 for FDA’s assessment of these studies.
Metabolites M449c and M549c were not detected in nonclinical metabolic studies #BLU-R9667,
BLU-R5482AM1, or BLU-R9705; however, M549a,b,d,e,f,g,h were oxidative metabolites
detected in these studies. The role of CYP3A4 and UGT1A4 were not specifically discussed in
Study #BLU-R9667.
Excretion
Following oral administration of pralsetinib to rats or monkeys, pralsetinib was excreted mainly
as the unchanged parent compound via the hepatobiliary route with a minor amount
eliminated by an active intestinal epithelial excretion (Reports BLU-R5482AM1, 00124835). The

majority of the dose was excreted within 48 hours. Urinary elimination represented only a

Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions
Based on in vitro data, pralsetinib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and
UGT1A4 (Phase II). Concomitant treatment with drugs that are inhibitors or inducers of CYP3A4
enzyme may alter pralsetinib plasma exposure (Reports BLU-R9696 and BLU-R5500).
Pralsetinib was a direct inhibitor of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 and a time-dependent
inhibitor of CYP3A4/5. Pralsetinib is also an inducer of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5.
Therefore, pralsetinib has a potential to affect the plasma exposures of concomitant
medications that are substrates of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 at clinically relevant
concentrations.
In vitro, pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate. Therefore, P-gp and BCRP inhibitors may
decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase its plasma
concentration. Pralsetinib is an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, MATE1, and
MATE2-K and may alter the PK of drugs that are substrates of these transporters at clinically
relevant concentrations.
FDA Additional Comment on Pharmacokinetic Drug Interactions:
Pharmacokinetic drug interaction studies were reviewed by the clinical pharmacology team as
part of their assessment, so these studies were not reviewed by the nonclinical team. See
Section 6 for FDA’s assessment of these studies.
The FDA’s Assessment (all data presented by FDA):
FDA generally agreed with the Applicant’s summaries of the nonclinical ADME/PK data;
however, see the FDA additional comments below some subheadings. All of the in vitro PK drug
interaction studies (e.g. cytochrome P450/UGT1A4 metabolism and transporter studies) were
reviewed by the clinical pharmacology team as part of their assessment, so these studies were
not reviewed by the nonclinical team. See Section 6 for FDA’s assessment of these studies.

Major Findings
Following single oral administration of 30 mg/kg (100
µCi/kg) 14C-pralsetinib, 14C-pralsetinib-derived radioactivity
was detected in whole blood and plasma for 0.25-48 hours
post-dose. In non-pigmented male SD rats, 14C-pralsetinib­
derived radioactivity was widely distributed to tissues with
a Tmax of 2-6 hours post-dose. Distribution of 14C-pralsetinib
was highest in the small intestine wall, liver, and adrenal
gland. Radioactivity was BQL in the brain and eye (lens). In
pigmented male Long Evans (LE) rats, distribution of 14C­
pralsetinib was highest in the uveal tract (Cmax = 210000 ng
equiv./g) and pigmented skin; radioactivity was also
observed in the adipose and eye (lens). Tmax in these tissues
was 2-24 hours. 14C-pralsetinib-derived radioactivity was
still measurable in the uveal tract at 504 hours post-dose
(68096 ng equiv./g) in LE rats, was eliminated more slowly
in the uveal tract of LE rats compared to SD rats, and was
~20-fold higher in the uveal tract of pigmented LE rats
compared to non-pigmented SD rats, suggesting an affinity
for melanin-containing tissue.
Twenty-four hours after a single oral dose of 10 mg/kg
pralsetinib to male SD rats surgically implanted with
microdialysis probes in the striatum region of the brain, the
mean concentration of pralsetinib in brain tissue,
cerebrospinal fluid (CSF), plasma (collected via the jugular
vein), and striatal interstitial fluid (ISF) was 5.58 ng/mL,
0.37 ng/mL, 113.6 ng/mL, and 1.35 ng/mL, respectively. PK
parameters for plasma and striatal ISF are shown below.
These data suggest that pralsetinib crosses the blood-brain
barrier in rats after oral administration.

Major Findings
The calculated hepatic extraction ratios in mice, rats, dogs,
monkeys, and humans were 0.231, 0.063, 0.31, 0.17, and
0.309, respectively, suggesting that pralsetinib is slowly
metabolized in rodent and monkey hepatocytes and
moderately metabolized in dog and human hepatocytes.
Pralsetinib was metabolized in human, dog, rat, mouse, and
monkey liver microsomes. Pralsetinib was the most stable
in human liver microsomes and the least stable in monkey
liver microsomes.
18 pralsetinib metabolites were identified. In vitro
metabolic pathways included oxidation, defluorination,
glucuronidation, and glutathione conjugation. The only
human metabolite present in vitro at >10% of total
radioactivity was M709 (31-55%), which was generated
from direct N-glucuronide of pralsetinib. There were no
unique human metabolites, although M707 and
predominantly M709 were present at higher levels in
human hepatocytes compared to animal hepatocytes.

Following single oral or IV administration of 30 mg/kg [14C]
pralsetinib, pralsetinib primarily underwent oxidation, N­

Following oral administration, fecal and urinary excretion
accounted for 91.75% and 1.55% of the dose, respectively.
No metabolites were detected in plasma. M549a/M549b
(45% of urine radioactivity; 0.7% of dose) and M709 (30%

(6% of feces radioactivity and dose), and M563b/M709
(23% of feces radioactivity; 21% of dose) were detected in
feces. Following single IV administration of [14C]pralsetinib,
biliary, fecal, and urinary excretion accounted for 43.96%,
37.62%, and 5.44% of the dose in bile duct-cannulated rats,
respectively.

A Pharmacokinetic and Excretion Mass
Balance Study of [14C]BLU-667 in
Cynomolgus Monkeys Following a
Single Oral (Gavage) Dose / 00124835

Following single oral administration of 10 mg/kg [14C]
pralsetinib, the drug primarily underwent oxidative
metabolism, glucuronidation, and cysteine conjugation in
male monkeys. The majority of radioactivity was excreted
in the feces (42-89%); urine accounted for ≤5% of the dose.
M549b (1%) and M549g/M453/M709 (co-eluted at 1.4­
9.1%) were detected in plasma at <10% of plasma
radioactivity. M549b (7-29% of urine radioactivity) and
M549g/M453/M709 (45-65% of urine radioactivity) were
detected in urine at ≤2% of the dose. M549b was the major
metabolite detected in feces (11-21% of feces radioactivity;
6-16% of dose), and M652, M531, M549d, M549e, M549h,
and M563b were detected in feces at ≤6% of the dose (2­
9% of feces radioactivity).
M531, M453, M549b, M563b, and M709 were detected in
human plasma, urine, and/or feces at levels ≤5%. There
were no major metabolites detected in human plasma,
urine, or feces at levels ≥10% of radioactivity. Overall,
considering all the species tested, there were no humanspecific metabolites or metabolites present at significantly
higher levels in humans than in animals used for
toxicological assessment. In addition, there are no major
human metabolites identified. No additional toxicological
assessment of metabolites is warranted.
Following single oral administration of 10 mg/kg (100
µCi/kg) [14C]pralsetinib to male cynomolgus monkeys,
[14C]pralsetinib equivalents were quantifiable in plasma
from 0.25-168 hours post-dose, and the whole blood-to­
plasma AUClast ratio was 0.825. Pralsetinib plasma exposure
(AUClast = 29400 ng·hr/mL) was generally lower than that of
[14C]pralsetinib equivalents (AUClast = 53200 ng·hr/mL),
suggesting the presence of [14C]pralsetinib-derived
metabolites. The majority of [14C]pralsetinib-derived
radioactivity was excreted in the feces, with 3.77% and
71.4% of the total administered [14C]pralsetinib-derived
radioactivity being recovered in urine and feces,
respectively, over 168 hours post-dose.

Pralsetinib

Toxicology
General Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
All pivotal toxicology studies were conducted in accordance with GLP and ICH S9 guidance using
the proposed clinical regimen of daily oral dosing. Repeated-dose toxicology studies in rats and
monkeys of 28 days and 13 weeks duration to support registration of pralsetinib are
summarized below.
A GLP-compliant 28-Day Once Daily Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of
(b) (4)
Pralsetinib in Sprague-Dawley Rats with a 14-Day Recovery Period (Report
-124570)
In the GLP-compliant 28-day repeated-dose toxicology study, pralsetinib was administered at
oral doses of 10, 20, 30, and 75 mg/kg/day to Sprague Dawley rats (15 animals/sex/group).
Dose-limiting toxicity and severely toxic dose effects in rats at 75 mg/kg/day (450 mg/m2/day)
were attributed to inanition, metabolic perturbations, and contributions by hematologic and
tissue effects. The severely toxic dose eliciting ≥ 10% lethality in the rat was 30 mg/kg/day
(180 mg/m2/day). Off target, nonseverely toxic pharmacologic effects included thickened
physeal cartilage (femur), hyperphosphatemia with corresponding mineralization in the
glandular stomach, incisor tooth degeneration, and decreased bone marrow cellularity with
corresponding lower erythron parameters. These effects were either resolving or resolved after
the 14-day recovery period with the exception of incisor tooth degeneration.
A GLP-compliant 13-Week Once Daily Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of
Pralsetinib in Sprague-Dawley Rats (Report 00124770)
In the GLP-compliant 13-week repeated-dose toxicology study in rats (10 animals/sex/group),
pralsetinib was administered at oral doses of 5, 10, and 20 mg/kg/day. There were no new
severely toxic findings, nor was there an increase in the severity of known adverse findings. At
the highest dosage administered (20 mg/kg/day), the following new adverse findings were
observed: testicular degeneration in males, decreased lymphoid cellularity in the thymus in
males and females, and degeneration of the corpus luteum in females. Because of these
findings, the NOAEL was 10 mg/kg/day (60 mg/m2/day).
FDA Additional Comment on Rat Toxicology Studies:
Decreased lymphoid cellularity in the thymus was also seen in the 28-day rat toxicology study.
In addition to the new adverse findings identified in the 13-week study highlighted by the
Applicant, broken teeth and incisor fractures were present at the 20 mg/kg dose level. There
was also an increase in the severity of incisor degeneration in the 13-week study compared to
the 28-day study. FDA does not insist on identification of a NOAEL in toxicology studies for
oncology drugs but based on decreased weight and testicular findings at doses ≥10 mg/kg the
pralsetinib NOAEL appears to be 5 mg/kg/day (30 mg/m2/day) in the 13-week rat toxicology

Pralsetinib in Cynomolgus Monkeys with a 14-Day Recovery Period (Report
-124571)
In the GLP-compliant 28-day repeated-dose toxicology study, pralsetinib was administered at
oral doses of 5, 15, and 40 mg/kg/day to Cynomolgus monkeys (5 animals/sex/group). Dosage
level of 40 mg/kg/day was lethal. Dosage levels of 5 and 15 mg/kg/day were reduced to 2.5 and
7.5 mg/kg/day due to morbidity/mortality. The lethal dose effects of pralsetinib-related
mortality in monkeys at 40 mg/kg/day (480 mg/m2/day) were attributed to bacterial sepsis
secondary to gastrointestinal inflammation and/or ulceration. Dose-limiting toxicity in the
monkey was attributed to inanition, changes in food consumption, decreased body weights,
serum chemistry, hematologic and tissue effects. No functional effects on the cardiovascular
system were noted based on ECG analyses and in life examinations. The HNSTD in the monkey
was 7.5 mg/kg/day (90 mg/m2/day). Off-target nonseverely toxic pharmacologic effects
included decreased bone marrow cellularity, physeal dysplasia, and decreased cellularity of
lymphoid organs which corresponded with changes in the hemogram. All effects in the HNSTD
group were resolving or resolved after the 14-day recovery period.
A GLP-compliant 13-Week Once Daily Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of
Pralsetinib in Cynomolgus Monkeys (Report 00124768)
In the GLP-compliant 13-week repeated-dose toxicology study in monkeys
(4 animals/sex/group), pralsetinib was administered at oral doses of 2, 5, and 10 mg/kg/day. All
findings observed in this study were considered to be nonadverse. The NOAEL was
10 mg/kg/day (120 mg/m2/day).
Additional studies
Toxicity and toxicokinetic of pralsetinib was studied in non-GLP-compliant 7-day oral gavage
studies, which were conducted preparatory to the GLP-compliant 28-day oral gavage studies, in
male Sprague Dawley rats and Cynomolgus monkeys. The NOAEL in rats was 15 mg/kg/day
(90 mg/m2/day) and in monkeys 10 mg/kg/day (120 mg/m2/day).
FDA Additional Comment on Additional Studies (7-day Toxicity Studies):
FDA did not review the 7-day toxicology studies in rats, dogs, or monkeys because they are not
relevant for this stage of development. Thus, the Applicant’s conclusions from these studies
were not verified by the FDA.

Reference ID 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Mineralization was present in multiple other tissues in rats including, but not limited, to the
glandular stomach, aorta, heart, and spinal cord. Additional histologic findings in the heart
included minimal to moderate hemorrhage and minimal to mild necrosis at the 75 mg/kg dose
level; these findings were not present in recovery group animals. In the 28-day monkey
(b) (4)
toxicology study (#
-124571), once daily oral administration of pralsetinib at the 40 mg/kg
dose level (approximately 3.3 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg dose) resulted in 100%
mortality by Day 5, and one monkey dosed with 15 mg/kg pralsetinib (approximately 1.1 times
the clinical AUC at the 400 mg dose) was found dead on Day 4. These deaths were due to
extensive GI inflammation and/or ulceration leading to bacterial sepsis. Additional target
organs in the 28-day monkey study that were not seen in the 13-week monkey study included
the brain and spinal cord (gliosis), femur (physeal dysplasia), heart (hemorrhage and necrosis
with bacteria at exposures approximately ≥2.6 and ≥1.1 times the clinical AUC at the 400 mg
dose, respectively), bone marrow, liver (Kupffer cell brown pigment, hepatocellular vacuolation,
subcapsular fibrosis), mandibular lymph node (decreased lymphoid cellularity, neutrophilic
lymphadenitis), pancreas (acinar secretory depletion, hemorrhage), salivary gland (acinar
secretory depletion, inflammation), and spleen (reduced lymphoid cellularity). In addition,
treatment with ≥15/7.5 mg/kg pralsetinib resulted in extensive histologic findings in the GI tract
including mucosal atrophy, typhlitis, enteritis, colitis, intussception, and hemorrhage.
Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
All genetic toxicology studies were conducted in accordance with GLP and currently accepted
guidelines. Pralsetinib was not genotoxic in the in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies.
A GLP-compliant Salmonella Escherichia coli/Mammalian Microsome Reverse Mutation Assay
(b) (4)
(Ames Test) of Pralsetinib (Report
-124573)
Pralsetinib was negative for mutagenic activity in the GLP-compliant in vitro 5-strain Ames
bacterial reverse mutation assay, with and without metabolic activation (rat liver S9).
A GLP-compliant In Vitro Micronucleus Assay of Pralsetinib with TK6 Cells (Report 00124797)
Pralsetinib did not induce micronuclei in vitro in TK6 cells in the 4-hour treatment with and
without metabolic activation and the 27-hour treatment without metabolic activation.
A GLP-compliant In Vivo Micronucleus Assay of Pralsetinib by Oral Gavage in Sprague Dawley
Rats (Report 00124769)
Pralsetinib did not induce bone marrow micronuclei in vivo in Sprague Dawley rats
(6 animals/sex/dose group) that were administered oral doses of pralsetinib of up to
300 mg/kg/day (1800 mg/m2/day) for 2 consecutive days.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA confirmed the Applicant’s presentation of the results of the submitted genotoxicity studies
and agrees with the conclusions. Standard positive controls confirmed the sensitivity and
validity of the assays.
Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted as such studies are not required to support the
registration of a drug intended for treatment of patients with advanced cancer, consistent with
the ICH S9 guidance. Pralsetinib was not mutagenic in a bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames
test) and did not induce micronuclei in vitro in TK6 cells or in vivo in rats. There was no
evidence of hyperplastic, preneoplastic, or neoplastic lesions in the 28-day and 13-week
repeated-dose toxicology studies in rats and monkeys.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. FDA agrees that carcinogenicity studies
are not needed to support the use of pralsetinib in the currently proposed indication per ICH
S9.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Studies on reproductive function, early embryonic development, pre- and postnatal
development, and effects in juvenile animals were not conducted in accordance with ICH S9
guideline. Embryofetal developmental toxicity was investigated in rats. Consistent with ICH S9,
a confirmatory study in a second species was not conducted because teratogenicity and fetal
lethality was observed in the rat embryofetal study.
A GLP-compliant Oral Gavage Enhanced Dose Range-Finding Study of the Effects of Pralsetinib
on Embryofetal development with Toxicokinetics in Sprague Dawley Rats (Report 00124766)
In the GLP-compliant embryofetal development toxicology study, pralsetinib was administered
at oral doses of 5, 10, 20, and 30 mg/kg/day to pregnant Sprague Dawley rats (10 animals/dose
group) from Gestation Days 6 to 17; dosage levels were selected based on findings from the
28-day repeated-dose toxicology study in rats. Pralsetinib was tolerated in pregnant rats up to
the highest tested dose of 30 mg/kg/day (180 mg/m2/day). Because of this, 30 mg/kg/day was
the NOAEL for maternal toxicity. The NOAEL for embryofetal development could not be
determined due to adverse effects on fetal morphology and/or intrauterine survival at all
dosage levels. At 5 and 10 mg/kg/day (30 and 60 mg/m2/day), multiple visceral (kidney and
ureter abnormalities) and skeletal (rib, costal cartilage, and vertebral anomalies) malformations
were observed. The renal malformations were attributed to on-target inhibition of RET
73
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
signaling, as RET is known to be an essential component of renal organogenesis in utero. Mice
homozygous for a mutation in the RET gene display renal agenesis or severe dysgenesis
(Schuchardt et al, 1994). Ureteric bud growth from the Wolffian duct into the adjacent
mesenchyme depends on sequential expression of the ligand glial cell line derived neurotrophic
factor and its receptor, RET (Costantini and Shakya, 2006). Postimplantation losses of 100%
were observed at dosage levels ≥ 20 mg/kg/day (≥ 120 mg/m2/day) and were attributed to
pralsetinib’s secondary pharmacologic effect of inhibiting VEGFR, an effect that has been
observed with other anti-VEGF compounds due to their antiangiogenic effects (Patyna et al,
2008).
The FDA’s Assessment (all data presented by FDA):
Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
Fertility, early embryonic development, and juvenile animal studies were not conducted or
needed to support the use of pralsetinib in the currently proposed indication (NSCLC) per ICH
S9.
Embryo-Fetal Development
The Applicant did not submit GLP-compliant embryo-fetal development studies in two species.
Rather, consistent with ICH S9, since a GLP-compliant embryofetal development study in rats
demonstrated clear positive results at exposure multiples below the human exposure at the
clinical dose of 400 mg, FDA agreed that this study was sufficient to convey the developmental
risk to patients. FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the embryofetal study,
and FDA’s assessment is below.
Study title/ number: An Oral (Gavage) Enhanced Dose Range-Finding Study of the Effects of
BLU-667 on Embryo/Fetal Development with Toxicokinetics in Sprague Dawley Rats /
00124766
Key Study Findings
• Pralsetinib induced 100% embryo-fetal mortality due to post-implantation loss (early
resorptions) at dose levels ≥20 mg/kg (approximately 1.5 times the exposure based on
AUC at the human dose of 400 mg) accompanied by reduced gravid uterine weight
• Pralsetinib was teratogenic at doses ≥5 mg/kg (approximately 0.2 times the exposure
based on AUC at the human dose of 400 mg)
