Pralsetinib

Other Toxicology Studies
The Applicant’s Position:
Local Tolerance Studies
Stand-alone studies for local tolerance were not conducted and are not deemed necessary.
Local tolerance of pralsetinib was characterized in GLP-compliant 28-day and 13-week
repeated-dose (oral administration) toxicology studies in rats and monkeys.
In Vitro Phototoxicity Evaluation
Pralsetinib was classified as nonphototoxic in 1 non-GLP- and 1 GLP-compliant in vitro
phototoxicity evaluation in 3T3 fibroblasts using the neutral uptake assay. No lesions in the
epithelium of the skin or the eye were observed in 28-day or 13-week the repeated-dose
toxicology studies in rats and monkeys.
Studies on Impurities
In silico assessment of drug substance impurities of pralsetinib, in vitro bacterial reverse
mutation assay of drug substance impurities of pralsetinib, and in vivo GLP-compliant impurity
qualification studies of drug substance impurities of pralsetinib in Sprague Dawley rats showed
no specific concerns around impurity safety.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of local tolerance and conclusions regarding
phototoxicity. In GLP-compliant phototoxicity Study #20143108, the Applicant evaluated the
phototoxic potential of pralsetinib by measuring the relative reduction in viability of BALB/c 3T3
mouse fibroblasts exposed to pralsetinib and ultraviolet radiation compared to the viability of
fibroblasts exposed to pralsetinib in the absence of ultraviolet radiation. Pralsetinib did not
demonstrate phototoxic potential [Photoirritancy Factor (PIF) = 1] compared to the positive
control promethazine (PIF = 75.82) under the conditions of this in vitro assay. The criterion for
“phototoxic” is PIF >5.
Studies on Impurities
FDA generally agreed with the Applicant’s conclusion that the studies evaluating pralsetinib
impurities (see below) did not demonstrate any specific safety concerns.
14-day Impurity Qualification Studies
The Applicant conducted two GLP-compliant 14-day oral repeat-dose toxicology studies in
Sprague-Dawley rats evaluating the potential toxicity of four impurities. In Study #00124665, 10

6 Clinical Pharmacology
Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Pralsetinib is a tyrosine kinase inhibitor targeting oncogenic rearranged during transfection
(RET) fusions and mutations. The proposed indication is for the treatment of adult patients with
metastatic, RET fusion-positive, non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC). The proposed dosing
regimen is 400 mg pralsetinib taken orally once daily (QD) on an empty stomach (i.e., at least 1
hour before and 2 hours after a meal).
The registrational trial (Study BLU-667-1101; ARROW) is an open-label, single arm, Phase 1/2
trial which determined the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) in the dose-escalation phase and
assessed the clinical efficacy and safety of pralsetinib in 179 patients with locally advanced or
metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC in the dose-expansion phase. The overall response rate
(ORR) (95% CI) was 70% (50%, 86%) in treatment naïve patients and 57% (46%, 68%) in patients
previously treated with platinum chemotherapy.
The clinical pharmacology package supporting this NDA included the following studies and data
analyses: single- and repeat-dose pharmacokinetic (PK) studies of pralsetinib in healthy subjects
and cancer patients, a mass balance study, a food effect study, drug-drug interaction (DDI)
studies with itraconazole, rifampin, and esomeprazole, and a QTc prolongation assessment,
exposure-response (E-R) analyses for efficacy and safety, and population PK (PopPK) analyses.
The proposed dosing regimen for pralsetinib is based on efficacy and safety data from Study
BLU-667-1101 and supported by E-R analyses for efficacy and safety. No large mean increase in
QTc (> 20 ms) was detected at the recommended dosing regimen. Administration of pralsetinib
on an empty stomach is acceptable as food increased pralsetinib exposure by 122%. No
clinically significant differences in the exposure of pralsetinib were observed when pralsetinib
was coadministered with esomeprazole. Based on the observed 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib
exposure when pralsetinib was coadministered with itraconazole, coadministration of
pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inhibitors should be avoided, and the current pralsetinib dose
should be reduced by half when co-administered with combined (dual) P-gp and strong CYP3A
inhibitors. Based on the observed 68% decrease in pralsetinib exposure when pralsetinib was
co-administered with rifampin, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers
should be avoided, and if this coadministration is unavoidable, the current pralsetinib dose
should be doubled on Day 7 of coadministration.
Exposure-safety analyses showed that increases in pralsetinib exposure correlate with time to
Grade 3+ anemia or pneumonia. No exposure-efficacy relationship for Kaplan-Meier (KM)
estimated progression-free survival (PFS) and the probability of ORR were observed. Intrinsic

The following clinical pharmacology studies will be conducted under post-marketing
requirements (PMRs): a dedicated hepatic impairment study in subjects with moderate and
severe hepatic impairment; DDI studies for pralsetinib as a victim when co-administered with a
P-gp inhibitor, a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, and a combined P-gp and moderate CYP3A
inhibitor; DDI studies for pralsetinib as a perpetrator when co-administered with a cocktail
enzyme substrates of CYP3A4/5, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9, and a cocktail transporter substrates of
P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE-1, and MATE-2K. A DDI study of pralsetinib as a victim
when coadministered with a moderate CYP3A inducer will be conducted under post-marketing
commitment (PMC).
The Office of Clinical Pharmacology has reviewed the clinical pharmacology package supporting
NDA 213721. This NDA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. PMRs and PMCs
are detailed in Table 10.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
Pharmacokinetics
Absorption

At the recommended dose of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC,
the median Tmax of pralsetinib was 4 hours after single-dose
administration and at steady state (Study BLU-667-1101).

Distribution

Human plasma protein binding: 97.1% (Report 1905093).
At the recommended dose of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC,
the geometric mean Vz/F of pralsetinib was 228 L after
single-dose administration and 268 L at steady state
(Study BLU-667-1101).

Metabolism and
Elimination

Pralsetinib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 and, to a
lesser extent, by CYP2D6 and CYP1A2 (Phase I) and UGT1A4
(Phase II) (Report BLU-R9696, BLU R5500). Unchanged pralsetinib
is the predominant component in plasma, urine, and feces, while
its metabolites from oxidation and glucuronidation are detected
in small to trace amounts (~5%) (Study BLU-667-0103). Excretion
in feces (72.5%) is the major elimination pathway for pralsetinib,
potentially via hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion
(Section 5.4).
The mean elimination t1/2 of 14.7 hours after single-dose
87
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

Pralsetinib
administration and 22.2 hours at steady state (400 mg QD)
supported the QD dosing regimen (Study BLU-667-1101). The
geometric mean CL/F was 9.10 L/h after administration of 400 mg
QD at steady state.
Steady State

Based on the mean elimination t1/2, steady state exposures of
pralsetinib are estimated to be reached after 3 to 5 days of
treatment.

Accumulation

At steady state, pralsetinib exhibits minor accumulation (< 2-fold)
compared with single-dose administration (Study BLU-667-1101).

Dose Proportionality

Dose-dependent increases in systemic exposure were observed
over the dose range of 60 to 600 mg in patients with NSCLC
(Study BLU-667-1101), but dose proportionality could not be
concluded due to low numbers of patients receiving doses other
than 400 mg (PK Report BLUE201904). In healthy subjects, dose
proportionality was concluded between single doses of 200 and
400 mg (PK Report BLUE202004).

Interindividual Variability Approximately 50% to 70% for Cmax and AUC parameters (Study
BLU-667-1101).
Healthy Subjects Versus
Patients

A population PK analysis estimated that the bioavailability of
pralsetinib was 43% higher in patients with NSCLC than in healthy
subjects (PK Report BLUE201906). This higher exposure in
patients with NSCLC could be due to differences between the
populations analyzed in terms of fasting status. Food has a
significant effect on pralsetinib exposure. The healthy subjects
fasted for 10 hours before and up to 4 hours after pralsetinib
administration, whereas the patients with NSCLC fasted for
2 hours before and 1 hour after pralsetinib administration.

Effect of Intrinsic Factors
Age, Sex, Race, and Body
Size Descriptors

Based on population PK analysis, age, sex, race, and body size
descriptors (body weight, lean body weight, BSA) have no
clinically relevant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report
BLUE201906).

Hepatic Impairment

Based on population PK analysis, mild hepatic impairment (total
bilirubin ≤ ULN and AST > ULN or total bilirubin > 1 to 1.5 times
ULN and any AST) have no statistically significant effect on the PK
of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906).

Renal Impairment

Based on population PK analysis, mild to moderate (creatinine
clearance 30 to 89 mL/min estimated by Cockcroft-Gault) renal
88
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Pralsetinib
impairment have no statistically significant effect on the PK of
pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906).
Effect of Extrinsic Factors
Food-drug Interactions

Food has a significant effect on pralsetinib exposure (> 2-fold
increase in exposure following a high-fat meal, compared with
fasted conditions) (Study BLU-667-0101).

Drug-drug Interactions

The oxidative metabolism of pralsetinib is predominantly
mediated by CYP3A4. Therefore, concomitant treatment with
drugs that are CYP3A inhibitors or inducers can increase (with
inhibitors) or decrease (with inducers) the exposure to
pralsetinib, as confirmed by a clinical study with concomitant
administration of pralsetinib with itraconazole (a strong CYP3A
inhibitor, yielding a 3.5-fold increase in pralsetinib AUC) or
rifampin (a strong CYP3A inducer, yielding a 68% decrease in
pralsetinib AUC) (Study BLU-667-0104). Population PK analysis
predicted that weak CYP3A4 inhibitor use has no relevant effect
on pralsetinib AUC, and that CYP3A4 weak inducer use has a 26%
decrease in pralsetinib AUC, which was not clinically relevant (PK
Report BLUE201906).
Pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate (Report 19BLUPP1R1).
P-gp or BCRP inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal
secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase the plasma
concentration of pralsetinib.
The aqueous solubility of pralsetinib is pH-dependent. Therefore,
gastric pH-elevating agents may impact the plasma exposure of
pralsetinib. Based on the results of a clinical DDI study in healthy
subjects (Study BLU-667-0105) and the population PK analysis of
pralsetinib data including patients with NSCLC (PK Report
BLUE201906), the effect of gastric acid-reducing agents on the
bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically relevant.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Pharmacology
Potential Effect on
Cardiac Repolarization

The potential for pralsetinib to prolong the QT interval was
assessed in patients with RET-fusion NSCLC, thyroid cancer, and
other RET-altered advanced solid tumors who were treated with
pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD (Study BLU-667-1101).
Treatment with pralsetinib was not associated with evidence of
QTc prolongation. The estimated mean change from baseline in
QTcF was −0.9 ms (with a 2-sided 90% CI upper bound of 4.0 ms)
at the observed mean Cmax for pralsetinib in the patients
analyzed. No effect on HR or cardiac conduction (PR interval and
QRS duration) was observed.

Exposure-response analyses
Efficacy Endpoints

Although statistically significant trends were not observed in the
E-R analyses of various efficacy endpoints (E-R Report
BLUE201905), treatment with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD
demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy in patients with NSCLC
(Study BLU-667-1101). Further, compared with lower doses, the
mean Ctrough value observed with 400 mg QD was closer to the
predicted brain IC90 of pralsetinib for RET inhibition in humans
(1514 ng/mL); the geometric mean (range) Ctrough value with
400 mg QD was 1150 ng/mL (179 to 4280 ng/mL). Initiating
pralsetinib treatment at a dose of 400 mg QD was associated with
a rapid decline in brain lesion size, and none of the patients
treated with this dose developing new CNS metastases during the
study.

Safety Endpoints

Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with the
time to Grade ≥ 3 anemia or pneumonia AEs, and with decreases
in hemoglobin and ANC and minor increases in AST and ALT (E-R
Report BLUE201905).

The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology of pralsetinib has been well characterized. The data included in the
dossier describe the PK properties following single- and multiple-dose administration of
pralsetinib in patients with locally advanced and metastatic NSCLC, after single-dose
administration of pralsetinib in healthy subjects, and population PK analyses of data from
studies in patients with NSCLC and healthy subjects, together with E-R analyses of data from
patients with NSCLC. Results from in vitro human biomaterial studies and from in vivo clinical
pharmacology studies (food effect, mass-balance, DDI) in healthy subjects, together with results
90
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
from the registration-enabling study in patients with NSCLC, were used to describe the ADME
properties of pralsetinib in humans and assess intrinsic and extrinsic factors that may have a
potential effect on pralsetinib PK. Results from these studies indicate that no dose adjustment
is warranted on the basis of age, sex, race, total or lean body weight, BSA, or mild or moderate
renal impairment. Although hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major
routes of elimination for pralsetinib, and hepatic impairment may result in increased plasma
concentrations of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) in
patients with mild hepatic impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
The increase in pralsetinib exposure observed under fed conditions can be clinically relevant.
Therefore, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least
2 hours after a meal. Because concomitant treatment with a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong
CYP3A inducer affected the exposure to pralsetinib, the concomitant use of pralsetinib with
strong CYP3A4 inhibitors or strong CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided. If coadministration with
a strong CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the pralsetinib dose should be reduced. No dose
adjustment is warranted when pralsetinib is coadministered with gastric acid-reducing drugs.
Pralsetinib has no clinically relevant or statistically significant effect on the QT interval.
Exposure-response analyses in patients with NSCLC revealed no relevant or consistent
relationships between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy or safety endpoints, likely
due to the wide range of pralsetinib exposures observed with the 400 mg QD dose (e.g., as
indicated by the range in Cave values from 74.5 to 4287 ng/mL in the population used to analyze
E-R relationships for efficacy endpoints). The mean Ctrough value achieved with the 400 mg QD
dose was close to the predicted brain IC90 of pralsetinib for RET inhibition in humans
(1514 ng/mL), and this dose was associated with rapid declines in brain lesion size and
prevention from developing new CNS metastases during the study.
The clinical results of Study BLU-667-1101 demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy and
adequate safety of the 400 mg QD dose, and therefore support use of this dose in the target
indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. FDA notes that dose-dependent increases in
systemic exposure could be concluded over the range of 200 mg to 400 mg QD. Refer to Section
6.3.1 for additional details. Also, in the DDI study with itraconazole (a dual P-gp and strong
CYP3A inhibitor), the observed increase in pralsetinib AUC0-INF was 2.5-fold instead of the 3.5­

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data:
The pralsetinib dose and regimen (400 mg QD) was selected on the basis of a Phase 1/2 study
that evaluated the efficacy and safety of pralsetinib (Study BLU-667-1101). Based on the MTD
and RP2D determined from Phase 1 of the study in patients with RET-fusion NSCLC, thyroid
cancer, and other RET-altered advanced solid tumors, and on the efficacy and safety results
obtained in Phase 2 of the study, the recommended dose of pralsetinib for the treatment of
patients with RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC is 400 mg QD. Pralsetinib is
taken under fasted conditions, at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal.
The long mean t1/2 of pralsetinib of 14.7 hours after single-dose administration and 22.2 hours
at steady state with the RP2D of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC meant that sufficient
exposure was achieved to ensure efficacy could be obtained with the QD dosing regimen.
Covariate analysis of the population PK model for pralsetinib demonstrated that body weight
and BSA had no relevant effect on the exposure to pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906), thereby
warranting a flat dosing regimen (i.e., no dose adjustment is warranted for variability in patient
body weight or BSA).
Administration of pralsetinib with food increased the Cmax and AUC0–∞ by 104% and 122%,
respectively, relative to fasted conditions (Study BLU-667-0101). Because this increase in
exposure could be clinically relevant, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least
1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal.
The Applicant’s Position:
The recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD administered under fasted conditions (at least
1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal) is expected to provide adequate exposure of
pralsetinib to ensure efficacy in the treatment of patients with locally advanced or metastatic
NSCLC, accompanied by an acceptable safety profile.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position on the recommended dosing regimen, i.e.,
the 400 mg QD dosing regimen administered at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a
meal. The proposed dosing regimen was the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) in the dose finding
phase of Study BLU-667-1101 (n=62). The efficacy results from the dose expansion phase of

Study BLU-667-1101 using the proposed dosing regimen, demonstrated that ORR in the RET

and with decreases in hemoglobin and neutrophil counts as well as with minor increases in AST
and ALT liver enzymes.
6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data:
Based on population PK analysis, age, race, body size, and sex have no clinically relevant effect
on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906). Although hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal
secretion are potentially major routes of elimination for pralsetinib (Section 5.4), and hepatic
impairment may result in increased plasma concentrations of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic
function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) in patients with mild hepatic impairment had no
statistically significant effect on PK parameters. The renal excretion of pralsetinib is minor
(Study BLU-667-0103) and, consistent with this finding, markers of renal function (serum
creatinine, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular filtration rate) in patients with mild to
moderate renal impairment had no statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
The oxidative metabolism of pralsetinib is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4, so that
concomitant treatment with drugs that are CYP3A inhibitors or inducers can increase (with
inhibitors) or decrease (with inducers) the exposure to pralsetinib, as confirmed by a clinical
study in healthy subjects with concomitant administration of pralsetinib with itraconazole (a
strong CYP3A inhibitor) or rifampin (a strong CYP3A inducer) (Study BLU-667-0104). The
population PK analysis of pralsetinib data indicated that patients taking a CYP3A4 weak inducer
are predicted to have a 26% decrease in pralsetinib Cmax,ss and AUC0–τ,ss (PK Report
BLUE201906). Based on the results of a clinical DDI study in healthy subjects (Study BLU-667­
0105) and the population PK analysis of pralsetinib data including patients with NSCLC, the
effect of gastric acid-reducing agents on the bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically
relevant.
The Applicant’s Position:
No therapeutic individualization is recommended on the basis of age, sex, race, total or lean
body weight, or BSA. Based on population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is required for
patients with mild or moderate renal impairment or for patients with mild hepatic impairment.
93
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Because coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a CYP3A inducer
affects pralsetinib plasma concentrations, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A
inhibitors or inducers should be avoided. If coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A
inhibitor cannot be avoided, the starting dose of pralsetinib should be reduced from 400 mg QD
to 200 mg QD. No dose adjustment is needed when pralsetinib is co-administered with gastric
acid-reducing drugs.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that no dosage adjustment for pralsetinib is needed in
patients with mild or moderate renal impairment, patients with mild hepatic impairment, or
when pralsetinib is coadministered with acid-reducing agents. FDA agrees with the Applicant’s
position that coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inhibitors or inducers should be
avoided.
FDA recommends that coadministration of pralsetinib with combined (i.e., dual) P-gp and
strong CYP3A inhibitors be avoided based on the observed 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib
exposure when pralsetinib was coadministered with itraconazole (Study BLU-667-0104). If this
coadministration cannot be avoided, the current pralsetinib dose should be reduced by half.
Refer to Section 6.3.2 for additional details.
FDA recommends that coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers be avoided
based on the observed 68% decrease in pralsetinib exposure when pralsetinib was
coadministered with rifampin (Study BLU-667-0104). If this coadministration cannot be
avoided, the current pralsetinib dose should be doubled starting on Day 7 of coadministration.
Refer to Section 6.3.2 for additional details.

6.3.1.

General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics

Data:
Drug Product
Pralsetinib is to be administered orally QD. The commercia l formu lation is an HPMC capsule,
intended to be marketed at a strength of 100 mg. The drug product manufacturing process

General ADME Characteristics

Absorption: Pra lsetinib is cl assified as a Biopharmaceutics Classification System Class II
compound (low solubi lity, high permeability). Absolute ora l bioavai lability was not assessed in
95
Version date: July 24, 2019 (All NDA/BlA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as '7he Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
humans. The Papp A-to-B of pralsetinib (0.1 to 10 µM) in C2BBe1 (Caco-2) cells ranged from 8.81
to 21.2 × 10-6 cm/s (Report 19BLUPP1R1), indicating moderate-to-good oral absorption in
humans. Across the range of single doses administered to patients with NSCLC (60 to 600 mg),
pralsetinib was rapidly absorbed, with a median Tmax of approximately 2 to 4 hours
(Study BLU‑667‑1101). The oral bioavailability of pralsetinib in healthy subjects was increased
when given under fed conditions. Compared with fasted conditions, Cmax increased by 104%
and AUC0–∞ increased by 122% when pralsetinib was administered after a standardized high-fat
meal (Study BLU‑667‑0101).
Distribution: Extensive in vitro plasma protein binding of pralsetinib was observed in human
plasma (97.1%) (Report 1905093). Pralsetinib did not show preferential partitioning into red
blood cells in whole blood; the human blood-to-plasma ratio was 0.6 to 0.7
(Study BLU‑667‑0103). In patients with NSCLC treated with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD,
the geometric mean Vz/F was relatively large, at 268 L at steady state (Study BLU‑667‑1101),
indicating extensive distribution from plasma into tissues. The unbound brain-to-plasma ratio
for pralsetinib was ~0.14 in rats (Report Key 1598), indicating a potential for adequate brain
penetration of pralsetinib.
Metabolism: In vitro, pralsetinib underwent limited to moderate metabolism in liver
microsomes and hepatocytes. Oxidative metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I)
and glucuronidation through UGT1A4 (Phase II). After administration of [14C]pralsetinib to
healthy subjects, unchanged pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in
plasma, urine, and feces, while its metabolites from oxidation and glucuronidation were
detected in small to trace amounts (~5%) (Study BLU-667-0103). No relevant interconversion
between pralsetinib and its (S)-enantiomer occurred (Memo 805-M10868).
Elimination: Unchanged pralsetinib accounted for 66.1% and 4.83% of the administered
radioactive dose excreted in feces and urine, respectively (Study BLU-667-0103). Based on
nonclinical and clinical mass balance studies, hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion in
feces are potentially the major elimination pathways for pralsetinib (Section 5.4).
Noncompartmental Pharmacokinetic Analysis
In patients with locally advanced and metastatic NSCLC, at the recommended dose of
400 mg QD, the median Tmax of pralsetinib was 4 hours (Study BLU-667-1101). The mean t1/2 of
14.7 hours after single-dose administration and 22.2 hours at steady state supported the QD
dosing regimen. Steady state exposures are estimated to be reached after 3 to 5 days of
treatment, with minor accumulation (< 2-fold) compared with single-dose administration. The
geometric mean CL/F was 9.10 L/h at steady state. The interindividual variability ranged from
approximately 50% to 70% for Cmax and AUC parameters. Dose-dependent increases in systemic

Pralsetinib
exposure were observed over the dose range of 60 to 600 mg, but dose proportionality could
not be concluded; this could be due to low numbers of patients receiving doses other than
400 mg, compared with other doses (PK Report BLUE201904). In healthy subjects, dose
proportionality was seen between single doses of 200 and 400 mg (PK Report BLUE202004).
Population Pharmacokinetic Analysis
A population PK model was developed for pralsetinib to describe the concentration-time data
obtained in studies conducted in healthy subjects and patients with NSCLC, and to identify and
quantify covariate effects that describe variability in the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report
BLUE201906). The pralsetinib data were best described by a 1-compartment disposition and
linear elimination model, with absorption modeled via 4 or 7 transit compartments, dependent
on the capsule manufacturing process used.
The covariates included in the final model were Asian race on V/F, disease status (NSCLC) on F,
age on CL/F, concomitant CYP3A4 weak inducer use on F, patients with NSCLC administered
Process I capsules on F, and Process I capsules on KTR. Overall, the magnitude of the effect of
the tested covariates on pralsetinib AUC and Cmax was small (< 0.5-fold effect). Taking into
consideration the observed interindividual variability in pralsetinib steady state PK in patients
receiving 400 mg QD (%CV of 48.0% for CL/F and 46.9% for V/F), the model estimates of
changes in pralsetinib PK related to these covariates are not considered to be clinically relevant.
Effect of Intrinsic Factors
Specific Populations: Based on population PK analysis, age, race, sex, body size descriptors
(body weight, lean body weight, BSA), and disease status (healthy subject, patient with NSCLC)
have no clinically relevant effect on the PK of pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906). Although
covariate analysis of the population PK model predicted statistically significant increases in
pralsetinib exposure in patients with NSCLC (43% higher exposure than in healthy subjects), in
the elderly (≤ 15% increase in exposure with 80 years, compared with 60 years), and with Asian
race (7% increase in Cmax compared with non-Asian races), these covariate effects are expected
to have a < 0.5-fold effect on pralsetinib exposure and the clinical impact is considered to be
minor in patients with NSCLC.
Hepatic Impairment: As hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major
elimination pathways for pralsetinib (Section 5.4), hepatic impairment may result in increased
plasma concentrations of pralsetinib. In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, markers of
hepatic function (ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) in patients with mild hepatic impairment had no
statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).

Pralsetinib
Renal Impairment: A clinical mass-balance study in healthy subjects demonstrated that the
renal excretion of pralsetinib is minor, with only 6% of the administered dose ([14C]pralsetinib)
recovered in urine (Study BLU-667-0103). Consistent with this finding, in the population PK
analysis of pralsetinib, markers of renal function (serum creatinine, creatinine clearance,
estimated glomerular filtration rate) in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment had no
statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
Effect of Extrinsic Factors
Food-drug Interactions
The exposure to pralsetinib in plasma was increased when administered under fed conditions
(Study BLU-667-0101). The Cmax and AUC0–∞ values for pralsetinib were increased by 104% and
122%, respectively, in healthy subjects after a standardized high-fat meal, as compared with
results obtained after overnight fasting. In addition, food delayed the absorption of pralsetinib,
with a statistically significant median treatment difference for Tmax of approximately 6 hours
between the administration of pralsetinib under fed and fasted conditions. The mean t1/2 values
were similar between the 2 treatments.
Drug-drug Interactions - Effect of Other Drugs on Pharmacokinetics of Pralsetinib
CYP3A Inhibitors: Pralsetinib Phase I oxidative metabolism is predominantly mediated by
CYP3A4, so strong CYP3A inhibitors may affect the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. In a clinical
DDI study (Study BLU-667-0104), coadministration of itraconazole (200 mg twice daily on Day 1
followed by 200 mg QD for 13 days), a strong CYP3A inhibitor, with a single 200 mg dose of
pralsetinib increased pralsetinib Cmax by 1.8-fold and AUC0–∞ by 3.5-fold, relative to a 200 mg
dose of pralsetinib administered alone. In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib,
concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitor use was not among the covariates that had a statistically
significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
CYP3A Inducers: Pralsetinib Phase I oxidative metabolism is predominantly mediated by
CYP3A4, so CYP3A inducers may affect the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. Coadministration of
rifampin (600 mg QD for 16 days), a strong CYP3A inducer, with a single 400 mg dose of
pralsetinib decreased pralsetinib Cmax by 30% and AUC0–∞ by 68%, relative to a 400 mg dose of
pralsetinib administered alone (Study BLU-667-0104). In the population PK analysis of
pralsetinib, although concomitant use of weak CYP3A4 inducers was a significant covariate in
the final model, yielding a predicted 26% decrease in AUC0–τ,ss and Cmax of pralsetinib, the
clinical impact of this difference was relatively minor in patients with NSCLC.
Gastric Acid Reducing Agents: The aqueous solubility of pralsetinib is pH-dependent, with

antacids) may therefore reduce the bioavailability of pralsetinib. Coadministration of
esomeprazole (40 mg QD for 6 days), a PPI, with a single 400 mg dose of pralsetinib had little
effect on the total clearance of pralsetinib, thereby resulting in only a modest decrease in
systemic exposure to pralsetinib. Based on geometric mean ratios, the decreases were 25% for
Cmax and 15% for AUC0–∞ (Study BLU-667-0105). In the population PK analysis of pralsetinib,
concomitant use of PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids was not among the covariates that had a
statistically significant effect on PK parameters.
Transporters: Pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate (Report 19BLUPP1R1). P-glycoprotein or
BCRP inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially
increase the plasma concentration of pralsetinib.
Drug-drug Interactions - Effect of Pralsetinib on Pharmacokinetics of Other Drugs
CYP450 Substrates: Pralsetinib is a direct inhibitor of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 at
clinically relevant concentrations, and is also a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A4/5 (Reports
1812081, 1811301, 1910112). Pralsetinib is an inducer of CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4/5 at
clinically relevant concentrations.
Transporter Substrates: Pralsetinib is an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1,
MATE1, and MATE2-K at clinically relevant concentrations (Report 19BLUPP1R1).
Cardiac Safety
The ability of pralsetinib to prolong the QT interval was assessed in a subset of patients with
RET-fusion NSCLC, thyroid cancer, and other RET-altered advanced solid tumors who were
treated with pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD (Study BLU-667-1101). Treatment with
pralsetinib was not associated with evidence of QTc prolongation. The estimated mean change
from baseline in QTcF was −0.9 ms (with a 2-sided 90% CI upper bound of 4.0 ms) at the
observed steady state geometric mean Cmax of 2090 ng/mL for pralsetinib in the subset
analyzed. No effect on HR or cardiac conduction (PR interval and QRS duration) was observed.
Exposure-response Analyses
Exposure-response analyses in patients with NSCLC revealed no relevant or consistent
relationships between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy or safety endpoints (E-R
Report BLUE201905). A time-dependent E-R analysis of pralsetinib in patients with NSCLC
(Study BLU-667-1101) revealed a trend of increasing pralsetinib exposure being associated with
increasing PFS, albeit not statistically significant. Initiating pralsetinib treatment at a dose of

The Applicant’s Position:
The pralsetinib drug product is administered as 100 mg HPMC capsules. No absolute
bioavailability study was conducted, but in vitro data and nonclinical in vivo data indicate that
pralsetinib has a high degree of oral bioavailability (refer to Section 5). Rapid in vivo absorption
was observed, with a median Tmax of 4 hours with the 400 mg dose in patients with NSCLC. The
oral bioavailability of pralsetinib in healthy subjects was increased 2-fold when given under fed
conditions. As this increase can be clinically relevant, patients should take pralsetinib at least
1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal. The mean Vz/F of 268 L at steady state with the
400 mg dose in patients with NSCLC indicated extensive distribution from plasma into tissues.
The unbound brain-to-plasma ratio for pralsetinib of ~0.14 in rats indicates a potential for
adequate brain penetration of pralsetinib.
Pralsetinib oxidative metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4 (Phase I) and UGT1A4
(Phase II). In vivo, unchanged pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in
plasma, urine, and feces, and hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major
routes of elimination for pralsetinib (Section 5.4). Metabolites were detected in small to trace
amounts (~5%). Therefore, in accordance with regulatory guidance, no further exploration of
the PK of pralsetinib metabolites was necessary.
Based on population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is warranted according to age, race, sex,
or body size descriptors. Markers of hepatic function and renal function were also not among
the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters, indicating that no
dose adjustment is warranted for patients with mild hepatic impairment, or with mild or
moderate renal impairment. The PK and safety of pralsetinib in patients with moderate or
severe hepatic impairment, or in patients with severe renal impairment or end-stage renal
disease, have not been studied and a recommended dose for these patients has not been
established.
Because pralsetinib metabolism is mainly mediated by CYP3A4, and concomitant administration
with a strong CYP3A inhibitor (itraconazole) or a strong CYP3A inducer (rifampin) affected the
exposure to pralsetinib, the concomitant use of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors or
strong CYP3A4 inducers should be avoided. If coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong
CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the starting dose of pralsetinib should be reduced from
400 mg QD to 200 mg QD. No dose adjustment is warranted when pralsetinib is coadministered

Treatment with pralsetinib at the recom mended starting dose of 400 mg QD was not associated

Exposure-response analyses in patients with NSCLC revealed no relevant relationships between

Clinical Pharmacology Questions

Data:

6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of
effectiveness?

Efficacy Endpoints: In the E-R primary efficacy population of patients with NSCLC in Study BLU­
667-1101, no relationships were observed between increasing pralsetinib exposure and efficacy
endpoints, except for PFS in the time-dependent E-R model (E-R Report BLUE201905). This
showed that increasing pralsetinib exposure was associated with a minor improvement in PFS,
with a hazard ratio of 0.93 (95% CI: 0.81, 1.07, p = 0.30) for every 400 ng/mL increase in
pralsetinib Cave, but this result was not statistically significant.
Systemic Tumor Kinetics: In support of the 400 mg QD dose, time course analyses of systemic
tumor kinetics in patients with NSCLC in Study BLU-667-1101 indicated that the overall relative
reduction in tumor size appeared to correlate with the starting dose of pralsetinib and the
adherence rate. Patients who started on a 400 mg QD dose with an adherence of ≥ 90% had the
greatest and most sustained percentage reduction in tumor size over time (E-R Report
BLUE201905).
CNS Tumor Kinetics: Among the 11 patients who had measurable brain lesions at baseline and
postbaseline in Study BLU-667-1101, the majority of patients (9 of 11) started on a dose of
400 mg QD and demonstrated a rapid decline in size of brain lesion after 2 months of therapy.
The only patient who started on a dose of 60 mg QD showed a 50% increase in brain lesion size
by Month 2 of therapy.
A time-dependent Cox proportional hazard model showed that a 400 ng/mL increase in Cave
resulted in a decrease in the hazard of CNS PD (hazard ratio: 0.69, 95% CI: 0.38, 1.26, p = 0.23).
The lack of statistical significance is likely due to the small number of patients in the
subpopulation with matching PK data (n = 10).
CNS Metastases: Among the patients with NSCLC who had no CNS involvement before entry
into Study BLU-667-1101, none of the 77 treated at 400 mg QD developed new CNS
involvement during the study (Section 8.1.2). Among the 8 patients without CNS involvement
103

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
who received lower pralsetinib starting doses in the efficacy population, 1 patient developed
new CNS involvement. This patient was treated at 100 mg QD for the first 5.5 months, then
escalated to 300 mg QD before developing new CNS disease at 7.3 months.
Brain IC90 for RET Inhibition: With doses of ≥ 400 mg QD at steady state in patients with NSCLC,
the geometric mean Ctrough value for pralsetinib was close to the predicted brain IC90 for RET
inhibition by pralsetinib in humans (1514 ng/mL); the geometric mean (range) Ctrough value with
the 400 mg QD dose was 1150 ng/mL (179 to 4280 ng/mL) (Study BLU-667-1101). Stochastic
simulations using the population PK model for pralsetinib indicated that only the 400 mg QD
dose resulted in a high probability (39.8% of patients) of achieving a plasma Ctrough above the
predicted brain IC90 for RET inhibition (1514 ng/mL) at the end of the dosing interval, as
compared with lower dose levels (PK Report BLUE201906).
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes, the clinical pharmacology program does provide supportive evidence for the effectiveness
of pralsetinib.
The time-dependent E-R analysis of pralsetinib for the E-R primary efficacy population of
Study BLU-667-1101 revealed a trend of increasing pralsetinib exposure being associated with
increasing PFS, albeit not statistically significant. Although statistically significant trends were
not observed in the E-R analyses of various efficacy endpoints, treatment with pralsetinib at a
dose of 400 mg QD demonstrated clinically relevant efficacy, as indicated by the median PFS of
12.7 months (Section 8.1.2). Any potential relationships between pralsetinib exposure and
efficacy endpoints may not be apparent due to the wide range of pralsetinib exposures
observed with the 400 mg QD dose (e.g., as indicated by the range in Cave values from 74.5 to
4287 ng/mL in the E-R primary efficacy population used to analyze the E-R relationship for PFS).
Focusing on the efficacy of pralsetinib in patients with CNS metastases, a graphical analysis
indicated the importance of 400 mg QD as the starting dose for prevention of CNS metastasis in
patients with NSCLC, as compared with lower doses, and the observation that none of the
77 patients treated at 400 mg QD developed new CNS involvement during the study indicated
that a starting dose of 400 mg QD is the most appropriate regimen for the treatment and
prevention of CNS metastasis in patients with NSCLC. Further, patients who started treatment
with the 400 mg QD dose with an adherence of ≥ 90% had the greatest and most sustained
percentage reduction in tumor size over time, as compared with lower doses and lower
adherence rates. These results concurred with stochastic simulations using the population PK
model for pralsetinib, indicating that only a dose of 400 mg QD resulted in a high probability
(39.8% of patients) of achieving a Ctrough value above the brain IC90 of pralsetinib for RET
inhibition (1514 ng/mL), as compared with lower dose levels.

6.3.2.2 Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
Data:
Based on the MTD and RP2D determined from Phase 1 (dose escalation phase) of
Study BLU-667-1101 patients with NSCLC, and the efficacy and safety results obtained in
Phase 2 (expansion phase) of the study, the recommended dose of pralsetinib for the
treatment of patients with RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC is 400 mg QD.
The long mean t1/2 of pralsetinib of 14.7 hours after single-dose administration and 22.2 hours
at steady state with the RP2D of 400 mg QD in patients with NSCLC meant that sufficient
exposure was achieved to ensure efficacy could be obtained with the QD dosing regimen.
Covariate analysis of the population PK model for pralsetinib demonstrated that body weight
and BSA had no relevant effect on the exposure to pralsetinib (PK Report BLUE201906), thereby
warranting a flat dosing regimen (i.e., no dose adjustment is warranted for variability in patient
body weight or BSA).
Administration of pralsetinib with food increased the Cmax and AUC by 104% and 122%,
respectively, relative to fasted conditions (Study BLU-667-0101). Because this increase in
exposure could be clinically relevant, patients should be instructed to take pralsetinib at least
1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal to mitigate the risk of achieving an unacceptably
high exposure and toxicity.
Treatment with pralsetinib at the recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD was not associated
with evidence of QTc prolongation.
In the E-R analysis of safety in patients with NSCLC, there were few consistent trends (E-R
Report BLUE201905). Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with the time to
Grade ≥ 3 anemia or pneumonia AEs, and with decreases in hemoglobin and ANC and minor
increases in AST and ALT. The decreases observed for hemoglobin likely correlate with the
Grade ≥ 3 anemia AEs and both were related to increasing pralsetinib exposure. Overall, the
results of Study BLU-667-1101 indicated that pralsetinib at a dose of 400 mg QD has an
acceptable safety profile in patients with NSCLC. No risks were identified that were not
expected with a protein kinase inhibitor being used as an antineoplastic agent in this patient
population.
105
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes, the proposed dosing regimen is appropriate for the general patient population for which
the indication is being sought.
Based on the considerations above for the clinical pharmacology of pralsetinib, the
recommended starting dose of 400 mg QD administered under fasted conditions (at least
1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal) is expected to provide adequate exposure of
pralsetinib to ensure efficacy in the treatment of patients with locally advanced or metastatic
NSCLC.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. The proposed dosing regimen was identified
as the MTD in the dose finding phase of Study BLU-667-1101 (n=62). Based on efficacy results
from the dose expansion phase of Study BLU-667-1101 using the proposed dosing regimen, the
ORR in the RET fusion-positive NSCLC population is 57% (95% CI: 46%, 68%; n=87) in patients
with prior platinum treatment and 70% (95% CI: 60%, 86%; n=27) in treatment-naïve patients.

Data:

6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?

Although covariate analysis of a population PK model predicted that patients with advanced
age (80 versus 60 years), or Asian race (versus non-Asian patients), would experience increased
pralsetinib exposure (< 0.5-fold), the clinical impact of these covariates is not considered to be
relevant in patients with NSCLC (PK Report BLUE201906). The covariates of sex, total or lean
body weight, and BSA were not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect
on PK parameters.
As hepatobiliary and gastrointestinal secretion are potentially major routes of elimination for
pralsetinib (Section 5.4), hepatic impairment may result in increased plasma concentrations of
pralsetinib. However, in the population PK analysis of pralsetinib, markers of hepatic function
(ALT, AST, albumin, bilirubin) were not among the covariates that had a statistically significant
effect on PK parameters.
A clinical mass-balance study in healthy subjects demonstrated that the renal excretion of
pralsetinib is low, with only 6% of the administered dose ([14C]pralsetinib) recovered in urine
(Study BLU-667-0103). Consistent with this finding, in the population PK analysis of pralsetinib,
markers of renal function (serum creatinine, creatinine clearance, estimated glomerular
filtration rate) were not among the covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK
106
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
parameters.
The Applicant’s Position:
No, an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy for pralsetinib treatment is not
required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors.
Based on population PK analysis, no dose adjustment is warranted on the basis of age, sex,
race, total or lean body weight, or BSA. Further, no dose adjustment is warranted for patients
with mild hepatic impairment, or with mild or moderate renal impairment. The PK and safety of
pralsetinib in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment, or in patients with severe
renal impairment or end-stage renal disease, have not been studied and a recommended dose
for these patients has not been established.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position. A hepatic impairment study in subjects with
moderate and severe hepatic impairment compared to subjects with normal hepatic function is
required as a PMR. The PopPK analysis included 18 patients with mild hepatic impairment and
suggested no effect of mild hepatic impairment on the exposure of pralsetinib. In the mass
balance study, 6% of the pralsetinib radioactive dose was recovered in the urine. Based on the
exposure-safety analysis, higher pralsetinib exposure was associated with a higher risk of Grade
3+ anemia and pneumonia. In addition, AST increased in 41% of patients (Grade 3/4 in 6%) and
ALT in 29% of patients (Grade 3/4 in 3.4%) in Study BLU-667-1101.
6.3.2.4 Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is
the appropriate management strategy?

Data:
Food-drug Interaction

The exposure to pralsetinib in plasma was increased when administered under fed conditions.
The Cmax and AUC0–∞ values for pralsetinib were increased by 104% and 122%, respectively, in
healthy subjects after a standardized high-fat meal, as compared with results obtained after
overnight fasting (Study BLU-667-0101).
Drug-drug Interactions - Effect of Other Drugs on Pharmacokinetics of Pralsetinib
Pralsetinib Phase I metabolism is predominantly mediated by CYP3A4. Therefore, CYP3A
inhibitors and inducers may affect the plasma exposure of pralsetinib. The aqueous solubility of
pralsetinib is pH-dependent, with higher pH resulting in lower solubility. Drugs that elevate the
107
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
gastric pH (e.g., PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids) may therefore reduce the bioavailability of pralsetinib.
CYP3A Inhibitors: Coadministration of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A inhibitor, with a single dose
of pralsetinib increased pralsetinib Cmax by 1.8-fold and AUC0–∞ by 3.5-fold, relative to
pralsetinib administered alone (Study BLU-667-0104). In the population PK analysis of
pralsetinib, concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitor use was not among the covariates that had a
statistically significant effect on PK parameters (PK Report BLUE201906).
CYP3A Inducers: Coadministration of rifampin (600 mg QD for 16 days), a strong CYP3A inducer,
with a single 400 mg dose of pralsetinib decreased pralsetinib Cmax by 30% and AUC0–∞ by 68%,
relative to a 400 mg dose of pralsetinib administered alone (Study BLU-667-0104). In the
population PK analysis of pralsetinib, although concomitant use of weak CYP3A4 inducers was a
significant covariate in the final model, yielding a predicted 26% decrease in the AUC and Cmax
of pralsetinib, the clinical impact of this covariate was relatively minor in patients with NSCLC.
Gastric Acid Reducing Agents: Coadministration of esomeprazole, a PPI, with a single dose of
pralsetinib had little effect on the total clearance of pralsetinib, thereby resulting in only a
modest decrease in systemic exposure to pralsetinib. Based on geometric mean ratios, the
decreases were 25% for Cmax, and 15% for AUC (Study BLU-667-0105). In the population PK
analysis of pralsetinib, concomitant use of PPIs, H2RAs, or antacids was not among the
covariates that had a statistically significant effect on PK parameters. Based on these results,
the effect of gastric acid-reducing drugs on the bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically
relevant.
Transporters: Pralsetinib is a dual P-gp/BCRP substrate (Report 19BLUPP1R1). P-gp or BCRP
inhibitors may decrease the gastrointestinal secretion of pralsetinib and potentially increase the
plasma concentration of pralsetinib. However, despite pralsetinib being both an inhibitor and a
substrate of P-gp and BCRP, 100% bioavailability of pralsetinib was observed in preclinical
species (Section 5.4). Further, dose-dependent increases in pralsetinib exposure were observed
over the dose range of 60 to 600 mg in patients with NSCLC, along with a dose-proportional
increase in pralsetinib exposure over the dose range of 200 to 400 mg in healthy subjects
(Section 6.2.1). Together, these results suggest that the impact of P-gp or BCRP inhibitors on
pralsetinib exposure is minimal.
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes, there are clinically relevant food-drug and drug-drug interactions for pralsetinib, and
appropriate management strategies are proposed.
The potential clinical relevance of the 2-fold increase in exposure when pralsetinib is
108

Pralsetinib
administered under fed conditions cannot be disregarded because it could effectively increase
pralsetinib exposure at the therapeutic doses either approaching or in excess of that observed
at the MTD of 400 mg QD. The safety, efficacy, and PK data for pralsetinib in patients with
NSCLC were obtained after treatment under fasted conditions. Therefore, patients should be
instructed to take pralsetinib at least 1 hour before and at least 2 hours after a meal.
Because coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor increases pralsetinib
plasma concentrations, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inhibitors should be
avoided. If coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inhibitor cannot be avoided, the
starting dose of pralsetinib should be reduced from 400 mg QD to 200 mg QD. Because
coadministration of pralsetinib with a strong CYP3A inducer decreases pralsetinib plasma
concentrations, coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers should be avoided.
Based on the results of a clinical DDI study and the population PK analysis of pralsetinib
including data from patients with NSCLC, the effect of gastric acid-reducing drugs on the
bioavailability of pralsetinib is not clinically relevant and no dose adjustment is needed in the
presence of gastric acid-reducing drugs.
No management strategies are proposed for coadministration of pralsetinib with P-gp and BCRP
inhibitors given that the effect on pralsetinib exposure is predicted to be minimal when these
agents are coadministered with pralsetinib. In addition, the majority of CYP3A4 inhibitors and
inducers are also P-gp inhibitors and inducers, and hence the management strategy applied to
strong CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers should adequately cover the interactions mediated by
P-gp.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position regarding food effect, coadministration with
gastric acid-reducing drugs and recommendation to avoid coadministration of strong CYP3A
inhibitors and inducers.
Coadministration of pralsetinib 200 mg QD with itraconazole 200 mg QD (a dual P-gp and
strong CYP3A inhibitor) in Study BLU-667-0104 increased pralsetinib Cmax by 84% (90% CI: 50%,
125%) and AUC0-INF by 251% (90% CI: 185%, 332%). The significant increase in pralsetinib
exposure cannot be explained solely by strong CYP3A inhibition, given that unchanged
pralsetinib was the predominant radioactive component in plasma, urine, and feces, with <5%
metabolites from oxidation detected in the mass balance study. FDA recommends that patients
avoid coadministration of pralsetinib with combined (dual) P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitors; if
such a coadministration cannot be avoided, reduce the current pralsetinib dose as
recommended in Table 11. Of note, Study BLU-667-1101 eligibility criteria excluded patients

Coadministration of rifampin 600 mg QD (dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inducer) with a single
pralsetinib dose of 400 mg in Study BLU-667-0104 reduced pralsetinib Cmax by 30.1% (90% CI:
19.6%, 39%) and AUC0-INF by 68.2% (90% CI: 63%, 73%). Of note, strong CYP3A inducers are also
inducers of P-gp via pregnane X receptor (PXR) activation. FDA recommends that
coadministration of pralsetinib with strong CYP3A inducers be avoided; if the coadministration
cannot be avoided, double the current pralsetinib dose starting on Day 7 of coadministration;
after the inducer has been discontinued for at least 14 days, resume the pralsetinib dose taken
prior to initiating the strong CYP3A inducer. In Study BLU-667-0101, 37% of patients with RET
fusion-positive NSCLC had brain metastasis that likely require treatment with antiepileptics.
Enzyme-inducing antiepileptics often have strong CYP3A induction effects (e.g., carbamazepine,
phenytoin). This recommendation ensures that patients with brain metastasis on enzymeinducing antiepileptics achieve pralsetinib mean Ctrough, ss levels of the 400 mg QD regimen and
close to the predicted brain IC90 of pralsetinib for RET inhibition in humans (1514 ng/mL).
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s position that the impact of P-gp inhibitors on
pralsetinib exposure is minimal as efflux of pralsetinib was completely inhibited in the presence
of Cyclosporin-A or Valspodar in MDR1-MDCK and C2BBe1 cells in Study 19BLUPP1R1. In the
DDI Study BLU-667-0104, a 2.5-fold increase in pralsetinib AUC0-INF was observed when
coadministered with itraconazole (a dual P-gp and strong CYP3A inhibitor) which cannot be
explained solely by inhibition of CYP3A as discussed in Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.2.

Coadministering pralsetinib with sensitive substrates of CYP3A4/5,

rosuvastatin is warranted. The treatment modifications of
rosuvastatin in 21 patients in Study BLU-667-1101 were assessed;

667-1101 and may not be sufficiently sensitive, as the primary
source of evidence, to replace a clinical drug interaction trial. Of
note, signs of statin toxicity may be masked by pralsetinib adverse
events (i.e., liver enzyme elevations). Dyslipidemics are the most
commonly used category of drugs for supportive care in lung
cancer; this assessment would provide an anti-hyperlipidemia
treatment option with appropriate dosing recommendations when
coadministered with pralsetinib.

shows that pralsetinib is a weak CYP3A4/5 inducer. Of note, combined
oral contraceptives are contraindicated with strong and moderate
CYP3A inducers.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that the studies listed in the table above are pertinent to the evaluation of efficacy
and safety of pralsetinib to support the proposed indication.
The primary clinical study to support the evaluation of efficacy and safety for the proposed
indication is Study BLU-667-1101 (ARROW). The submission contains an interim Clinical Study
Report (CSR 1) dated February 26, 2020, with the data cutoff date of November 18, 2019. CSR 1
presents efficacy results for 132 NSCLC RET-fusion -positive patients who received pralsetinib at
a starting dose of 400 mg once daily (QD) by July 11, 2019 [in Group 1 or 2 of the dose
expansion (Phase 2) part, or in the dose-finding (Phase 1) part of the study] and safety results
for 404 patients who received at least one dose of pralsetinib enrolled in either the dosefinding part (Phase 1) and dose expansion part (Phase 2).
On June 11, 2020, the Applicant submitted a 90-day safety update and a 90-day efficacy update
for the ARROW study
The 90-day efficacy update with the data cut-off date of February 13, 2020 contains analysis of
the efficacy population of NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD, enrolled as of July 11, 2019
(N=132) and additional efficacy analyses for the population of patients with “RET-altered
measurable disease”, comprised of 125 patients treated at 400 mg QD. The proposal to limit
the efficacy population to include only patients with measurable disease was discussed at the
Applicant Orientation Meeting on May 14, 2020. FDA requested that the Applicant submit the
pertinent analysis for review and consideration for inclusion in the USPI.
Per FDA’s request, the Applicant submitted a revised dataset package (ADaM and SDTM) with
the 90-day data update based on the cutoff date of February 13, 2020. The datasets were
received on June 18, 2020 under SDN 22.
A revised Assessment Aid with extensive revisions to Section 8.2, Review of Safety was
submitted by the Applicant on June 26, 2020 (SDN 26) to update the safety tables based on the
data-cut off of February 13, 2020.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
Efficacy claims in this application are based on the results from the pivotal Phase 1/2
Study BLU-667-1101. Efficacy results of pralsetinib from this study are provided for the
intended population of patients with RET-fusion positive NSCLC dosed on or before 11 July 2019
(data cutoff of 18 November 2019). The focus of this application is on the proposed indication
of RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC.
FDA Reviewer’s comment: As stated in section 7.1, the initial efficacy population, as agreed
upon at the time of the pre-NDA meeting, consisted of efficacy data from 132 patients with
RET-altered NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD, enrolled by July 11, 2019. Additional efficacy
analyses for the “RET-altered measurable disease population”, comprised of 125 patients
treated at 400 mg QD were submitted on June 11, 2020 for inclusion in the USPI. FDA’s primary
efficacy is based on the subpopulation of patients with measurable metastatic RET fusionpositive NSCLC who had received prior platinum-based chemotherapy treated at 400 mg QD,
enrolled by July 11, 2019 (N=87) and on the subpopulation of patients with measurable
metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC who had not received prior treatment for their metastatic
disease (N=27). The efficacy analyses submitted by the Applicant in the following sections
represent data from 132 patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD,
enrolled by July 11, 2019, with measurable and non-measurable disease.
A Phase 1/2 Study of the Highly-Selective RET Inhibitor, Pralsetinib, in
Patients with Thyroid Cancer, NSCLC, and Other Advanced Solid Tumors
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study BLU-667-1101 is a Phase 1/2, open-label, FIH study designed to evaluate the safety,
tolerability, PK, pharmacodynamics, and antineoplastic activity of pralsetinib in patients with
advanced, unresectable, RET-altered NSCLC, MTC, and other RET-altered solid tumors. The
study included a Phase 1 dose escalation part to determine the MTD and RP2D of pralsetinib,
followed by a Phase 2 expansion part to assess the clinical efficacy of pralsetinib in specific
tumor types and treatment settings (measured primarily by ORR), and further define the safety
and tolerability at the RP2D. Phase 1 ran until the RP2D was determined, at which point Phase 2
was initiated and is still ongoing. An overview of the study design is available in Figure 3.

In Phase 1, patients were enrolled in 6 centers across North America and Europe. In Phase 2,
patients were enrolled in 53 centers across North America, Europe, and Asia.
Choice of Control Group
There is no currently accepted standard of care for RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients.

Diagnostic Criteria
Patients with confirmed oncogenic RET fusion as determined by local testing of tumor or ctDNA
in blood were enrolled.
Key Inclusion Criteria
Phase 1
• Men or women ≥ 18 years with pathologically documented, definitively diagnosed
unresectable advanced solid tumor;
• Progressed to standard therapy or not adequately responded/intolerant to standard
therapy or Investigator determined standard therapy to be inappropriate or there was no
accepted standard therapy;
• ECOG performance status of 0 to 1.
Phase 2 – RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients
• Group 1: Men or women ≥ 18 years with unresectable, pathologically documented,
definitively diagnosed locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC with a RET fusion previously
treated with a platinum-based chemotherapy;
• Group 2: Men or women ≥ 18 years with unresectable, pathologically documented,
definitively diagnosed locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC with a RET fusion not
previously treated with a platinum-based chemotherapy, including those who have not had
any systemic therapy. Up until Amendment 9, naïve patients were only allowed if the
Investigator had determined that treatment with standard therapy was not appropriate.
With Amendment 9, prior platinum chemotherapy in the neoadjuvant and adjuvant setting
was permitted if the last dose of platinum treatment was ≥ 4 months before the first dose
of pralsetinib (introduced after cutoff).
• Both groups:
o Had an oncogenic RET fusion, as determined by local testing of tumor or ctDNA in
blood;
o Had measurable disease as per RECIST v1.1;
o ECOG performance status of 0 to 1.
Key Exclusion Criteria
• Known primary driver alteration other than RET;
• Medical history of myocardial infarction or unstable angina within the previous 6 months;
clinically significant, uncontrolled cardiovascular disease (including congestive heart failure
NYHA Grade III or IV); uncontrolled hypertension; or clinically significant, uncontrolled
arrhythmias;
• Previous treatment with selective RET inhibitor (except Group 6); any systemic anticancer
therapy and all forms of radiotherapy within 14 days or 5 half-lives before first dose of
pralsetinib; immunotherapy or other antibody therapy within 28 days before first dose of

Dose Selection
Initial dose level chosen to be studied for Phase 1 was based on nonclinical data from 28-day
repeated-dose toxicology studies conducted in rats and monkeys. Based on the overall data
from Phase 1, a pralsetinib dose of 400 mg QD was determined to be the recommended
starting dose for Phase 2.
Study Treatments, Treatment Assignment, and Blinding
As this was an open-label study, there was no randomization or blinding. Pralsetinib was
administered continuously daily without interruption (unless due to interruption or
discontinuation due to toxicity, progression, or other reasons).
In Phase 1, pralsetinib was administered once daily (30 to 600 mg) or twice daily (100/100 mg
or 200/100 mg) to determine the MTD and RP2D. Patients were assigned to a dose cohort
according to a dose escalation scheme prespecified in the protocol. In Phase 2, pralsetinib was
administered at RP2D (400 mg QD).
Dose Modification, Dose Discontinuation
Management guidance and dose modifications during both phases were provided in the
protocol.
In Phase 1, intra-patient dose escalation and dose modification for AEs were allowed. To
minimize the number of patients treated at potentially inactive doses, intra-patient dose
escalation was permitted after a patient had completed ≥ 2 cycles of treatment without
experiencing Grade 3/4 AEs related to pralsetinib or a DLT. Patients who experienced a DLT
interrupted administration of pralsetinib and were followed until the DLT resolved to Grade ≤ 1
or until the patient’s baseline value was achieved, if higher. After resolution of the AE, with a
maximum of 2 weeks’ dose interruption, the patient could resume therapy with a reduction of
1 dose level. A maximum of 3 dose reductions were permitted for any patient.
124
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
In Phase 2, dose modification for AEs, ADRs, and dose re-escalation after resolution of ADRs
were allowed. For AEs, pralsetinib dose reductions to < 100 mg QD were not permitted. If a
patient required dose reduction < 100 mg QD, study treatment was discontinued.
Administrative Structure
Safety was monitored on a regular basis, with the Sponsor Clinical Study Team hosting regular
teleconferences with Investigators. In addition, the Sponsor’s Safety Management Team
reviewed and managed cumulative safety data at periodic intervals (approximately quarterly)
and ad hoc, as needed.
During Phase 1, the Sponsor’s Clinical Study Team and the Investigators met at the end of each
dose cohort to review all data. Dose escalation decisions were based on all relevant, available
data, and not solely on DLT information.
During Phase 2, all available data were reviewed approximately every 1 to 2 months at a safety
review meeting that included the Investigators and the Sponsor’s Clinical Study Team. After
Protocol Amendment 9, a Safety Review Committee was introduced in light of the growing
enrollment size. The Independent Data Monitoring Committee (3 members independent of the
study) replaced the Safety Review Committee in December 2019. A BICR assessment was
instated to assess tumor scans.

resolution of toxicities. Additional safety tests (e.g., hematology, chemistry, and ECG) could have been performed whenever clinically indicated, at the

Were performed for women of childbearing potential within 7 days of C1D1. A serum pregnancy test was performed at Screening; thereafter, a serum or

and CT or MRI with IV contrast of the abdomen, brain (MRI was preferred), and pelvis, were performed. In addition, for patients with thyroid cancer, CT with
IV contrast or MRI of the neck were performed. At subsequent timepoints, all body regions that contained sites of disease (target or non-target) at Screening
were imaged. For each patient, the same method for tumor imaging used at baseline was used throughout the study. Disease response assessment was also
performed every 3 months (±14 days) after EOT in patients without documented progressive disease.

Only during Phase 2, EORTC QLQ-C30 was administered on D1 of each cycle for C1 through C12.

SAEs that were assessed as possibly or probably related to study treatment that occur > 30 days posttreatment were also reported.
S
C1D2 and C1D16 were no longer required for patients enrolled under Protocol Amendment 9.

Pralsetinib
Dietary Restrictions/Instructions
Pralsetinib was administered with a glass of water in a fasted state, with no food intake from
2 hours before until 1 hour after study drug administration.
Concurrent Medications/Rescue Medications
During the treatment period, the following medications, treatments, and procedures were
prohibited: other anticancer therapies or investigational therapeutic agents; neutrophil growth
factor within 14 days before Cycle 1 Day 1 and throughout Cycle 1 (unless patient experienced
DLT of neutropenia); strong inhibitors and inducers of CYP3A4, strong dual P-gp and CYP3A4
inhibitors. Radiation therapy to target lesions or surgical removal of target lesions was
considered indicative of PD.
The following medication was not prohibited but was advised to be used with caution: sensitive
substrates CYP2C9 substrates, strong CYP2D6 inhibitors, and strong CYP1A2 inhibitors.
All other medications and treatments were permitted, including palliative and supportive care
for disease-related symptoms.
Treatment Compliance
Patients returned all unused capsules (or empty bottles) on Day 1 of each treatment cycle or at
the next scheduled visit. Compliance was assessed based on return of unused capsules (or
empty bottles).
Patient Completion, Discontinuation, or Withdrawal
Completion/Discontinuation/Withdrawal from Study Treatment
Patients could continue treatment until precluded by toxicity, noncompliance, closure of the
study by Sponsor, or any reason for discontinuation as per protocol. Patients with PD could
remain on treatment if in the opinion of the Investigator the patient has benefited from the
pralsetinib therapy, and it was clearly in the best medical interest of the patient to remain on
treatment.
Patients had to discontinue from study treatment at any time for any of the following reasons:
withdrawal of consent to continue study treatment or pregnancy. Patients could discontinue
from study treatment for any of the following reasons: AE, disease progression, protocol
deviation, Investigator’s decision, or loss to follow-up.
Completion/Discontinuation/Withdrawal from Study
A patient was considered to have completed the study if he/she has completed all required
visits shown in schedule of assessments (Table 15).

Patients were withdrawn from study at any time for any of the following reasons: withdrawal of
consent to continue study, death, or loss to follow-up.
Before Protocol Amendment 9, patients who discontinued treatment were considered to have
completed the study after a 30-day post-treatment follow-up visit or, for patients that had not
yet experienced disease progression on study, after disease progression/initiation of new
therapy. As Protocol Amendment 9 added follow-up for OS assessments, expected reasons for
withdrawal from study were limited to withdrawal of consent, death, or loss to follow-up.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the description of Study BLU-667-1101 provided by the Applicant.
Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
Primary Efficacy Endpoint
Phase 1: Not applicable.
Phase 2:
The primary efficacy endpoint was the confirmed ORR based on RECIST v1.1 as assessed by BICR
of local imaging. For BICR of local imaging, CT and magnetic resonance imaging scans were sent
to a central radiology laboratory for assessment of response and progression by 2 board
certified radiologists (if necessary, adjudication was performed by a third board certified
radiologist) blinded to patient identification and starting dose. Central assessment provided an
objective, unbiased, independent review of pertinent radiological data using uniform
assessment rules to ensure a standard definition for all patients across this multicenter study.
Disease response to treatment was assessed using radiographic assessments according to
RECIST v1.1. These tests and evaluations are standard and appropriate for the evaluation of
cancer patients and were based on appropriate response criteria that are widely accepted as
valid and reliable measures of response to treatment.
Secondary Efficacy Endpoints
Phase 1:
Secondary efficacy endpoints were
• Confirmed ORR based on RECIST v1.1 as assessed by BICR of local imaging;
• Correlation of RET gene status in plasma and/or tumor tissue with ORR, CBR, DOR, PFS, OS,
and DCR.

Phase 2:
Secondary efficacy endpoints were
• DOR, CBR, DCR, PFS, and OS;
• Correlation of RET gene status in plasma and/or tumor tissue with ORR, CBR, DOR, PFS, OS,
and DCR.
Exploratory Efficacy Endpoints
Phase 1: Not applicable.
Phase 2:
The exploratory efficacy endpoints were
• Quality of life assessed by the EORTC QLQ-C30 questionnaire;
• CNS metastases activity analysis in RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients who had measurable
(target) lesion(s) in CNS/brain at baseline.
FDA Clarification: In a response to an FDA information request, the Applicant indicated that
“CNS metastases activity analysis” was incorrectly listed here as exploratory. In that response,
Blueprint stated that the analysis of CNS response was added as a secondary analysis in version
2 of the statistical analysis plan prior to database lock and analysis of CNS response has been
added as a secondary endpoint in the most recent global protocol amendment (Protocol
(b) (4)
Amendment 13 dated 08 July 2020) submitted to IND
on 15 July 2020.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA considers the endpoints of CBR, DCR and “correlation of RET gene status in plasma and/or
tumor tissue with ORR, CBR, DOR, PFS, OS, and DCR” to be exploratory in nature. Additionally,
FDA considers the time to event endpoints of PFS and OS to be not interpretable in a single arm
study, and any corresponding analyses were considered descriptive and were not confirmed by
FDA review.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The statistical analysis plan was finalized prior to the database lock.
A data cutoff was established, and all analyses were performed based on the data up to this
data cutoff date. Data from Phase 1 and 2 were pooled by starting dose level. Data from all
centers were pooled.
The primary efficacy analyses were based on BICR of local tumor imaging. Primary efficacy
