analyses were performed in the efficacy population subset of RET-fusion positive NSCLC
patients exposed to ≥ 1 dose of pralsetinib on or before 11 July 2019 who were treated at a
pralsetinib starting dose of 400 mg QD (data cutoff of 18 November 2019).
Efficacy analyses were also conducted for treatment groups of RET-fusion positive NSCLC
patients receiving any prior systemic treatment, prior platinum treatment, and no prior
platinum treatment (including prior non-platinum systemic treatment and no prior systemic
treatment). Note that enrollment is still ongoing for Group 2 (NSCLC patients with no prior
platinum treatment, including those who had not had any systemic treatment). According to
the initial study design, Group 2 patients without prior systemic treatment had to be ineligible
for standard platinum-based therapy before enrolling into the study. Note that prior selective
RET-inhibitor-treated NSCLC patients (Group 6) were explored separately and were not
included in the efficacy analyses.
Secondary efficacy analyses were conducted in the efficacy population subset of RET-fusion
positive NSCLC patients exposed to ≥ 1 dose of pralsetinib on or before 11 July 2019 who were
treated at a pralsetinib starting dose of 400 mg QD (data cutoff of 18 November 2019).
In addition, efficacy was evaluated in the subpopulation of RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients
with CNS metastases treated with pralsetinib at 400 mg QD. This subpopulation included all
RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients who had measurable (target) lesion(s) in the brain at
baseline according to RECIST v1.1 by BICR data and had not had any brain radiotherapy or
radiosurgery within 2 months before pralsetinib treatment.
Selected efficacy analyses were conducted for the subgroups of age (< 65 years, ≥ 65 years),
sex, geographic region (US, Europe, Asia), race group (Asian, non-Asian), and RET genotype as
determined by local testing and/or central analysis of ctDNA/tumor tissue (KIF5B, CCDC6, and
“other”), and prior anticancer therapies (prior PD-1/PD-L1 or not, prior MKIs or not).
The FDA’s Assessment:
Efficacy analysis was conducted based on two separate data cutoffs, the original data cutoff
date of November 18, 2019, and one corresponding to the 90-day efficacy update, which had a
data cutoff date of February 13, 2020. A further update for duration of response was provided
based on data cutoff of May 22, 2020 for patients who had a confirmed response by the time of
the 90-day efficacy update. The timing of the final analysis was not pre-specified in the
protocol. The primary analysis of efficacy was conducted separately in patients who received
prior platinum-based chemotherapy and in patients who were treatment-naïve. A supportive
analysis was conducted in the population of patients with measurable disease at baseline
(which included patients who had prior treatment with a therapy other than prior platinumbased chemotherapy).
134
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The final version of the statistical analysis plan, dated October 30, 2019, included assumptions
for sample size calculation in the expansion phase for prior platinum therapy and the
treatment-naïve cohorts. In cohort 1, which includes patients with RET-fusion positive NSCLC
who were previously treated with platinum-based chemotherapy, assuming a null hypothesis
that ORR=0.23 against an alternative hypothesis of ORR=0.5 with two-sided type I error rate of
0.05, a sample size of 80 will provide more than 95% power. Similarly, in cohort 2, which
includes treatment-naïve patients with RET-fusion positive NSCLC, assuming a null hypothesis
that ORR = 0.48 against an alternative hypothesis of ORR=0.61 at two-sided type I error rate of
0.05, a sample size of 170 will provide more than 90% power. There was no pre-defined plan for
handling missing response assessments for ORR analysis.
For a single arm study, FDA’s efficacy analysis is based on ORR of adequate magnitude and a
clinically meaningful duration of response. A statistically significant ORR compared to a
historical rate may not necessarily be clinically meaningful.
Protocol Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The study protocol was amended 13 times during the study. Amendments 1, 2, 4.1, 7, and 9
were incorporated globally. These changes are not expected to have had an impact on the
integrity of the trial or interpretation of the results. Key changes were as follows:
Amendment 1 (dated 09 January 2017):
• Inclusion criteria now include only patients with unresectable disease;
• Exclusion criteria now exclude patients with NSCLC with a targetable mutation in EGFR, ALK,
or ROS1;
• Enrollment stopping rule was incorporated that terminated further enrollment to the study
if there was an excess of permanent treatment discontinuations due to pralsetinib-related
AEs;
• Strong inhibitors of CYP1A2, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4, as well as inducers of CYP3A4 and strong
dual P-gp and CYP3A4 inhibitors were included in the list of prohibited medications;
Amendment 2 (dated 02 February 2018)
• Exclusion criterion 1 was revised to exclude NSCLC patients with known BRAF mutations.
Amendment 4.1 (dated 25 July 2018)
• Based on the favorable tolerability and encouraging efficacy observed in Phase 1, the
sample size and overall enrollment for Phase 2 was increased to further evaluate the safety,

efficacy, and PK of pralsetinib in RET-altered patients treated at the MTD/RP2D;
Group 6 was added in Phase 2 to assess pralsetinib safety and efficacy in patients previously
treated with a selective RET inhibitor;
The allowed ECOG performance status was changed to be 0 to 1;
Quality of life assessments added.

Amendment 7 (dated 12 December 2018)
• Phase 2: Definitions of Groups 1 to 4 were adjusted to reflect previous treatment with
approved standard of care agents. Group 5 was split into new Group 5 and new Group 7 to
reflect that solid tumor patients with RET-fusions and RET-mutations may be distinct clinical
and/or regulatory populations;
• Phase 2: Sample size increased to 80 patients in Group 1, 40 patients in Group 2, 60 patients
in Group 3, and 40 patients in Group 5 to test the efficacy hypotheses for each group
(increasing the study’s total sample size to 360 patients);
• Phase 2: Safety Review Committee was added;
• Exclusion criteria added to exclude patients with Grade 2 or serum phosphorus at baseline
or with clinically significant interstitial lung disease or interstitial pneumonitis.
Amendment 9 (dated 03 July 2019)
• The study Phase was updated from “1” to “1/2” to better describe the study design with the
increased patient population and hypothesis testing;
• Phase 2 Group 2 sample size was increased to 200 patients due to the encouraging initial
data in treatment-naïve RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients, to allow for enrollment of
RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients with no prior treatment (increasing the study’s total
sample size to 527 patients);
• In Group 2, the requirement for NSCLC patients not to be candidates for standard
platinum-based chemotherapy was removed. Patients were permitted to have prior
platinum chemotherapy in the neoadjuvant and adjuvant setting if the last dose of platinum
was ≥ 4 months before the first dose of study drug;
• Stopping rules were updated to account for increased sample size;
• Dose modification for ≥ Grade 3 pneumonitis was modified (patients had to be permanently
discontinued from study treatment, unless the Investigator discussed with the Sponsor and
believed the risk-benefit justified retreatment);
• Clarification was added that patients enrolled in Phase 1 and treated at the RP2D were
pooled together with the appropriate Phase 2 groups for analyses;
• Statistics section was updated to align with the statistical analysis plan.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with description of study amendments for Study BLU-667-1101 provided by the
Applicant. In addition, FDA notes that post-progression follow-up was revised in Amendment 9

Protocol Violations/Deviations
Data:
Of the 404 patients treated at all doses/schedules in the safety population, 121 patients
(30.0%) had an important protocol deviation, most commonly related to study
conduct/procedures (at any timepoint in 14.9% of patients and at Screening in 8.9% of
patients). All other important protocol deviations occurred in < 5.0% of patients. A total of
12 patients (3.0%) had a major protocol deviation based on medical review.
Of the 132 NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD in the efficacy population, 45 patients (34.1%)
had an important protocol deviation, most commonly related to study conduct/procedures (at
any timepoint in 12.9% of patients, during Screening in 10.6% of patients, and relating to study
restrictions/withdrawal criteria in 6.1% of patients). All other important protocol deviations
occurred in < 5.0% of patients. A total of 4 patients (3.0%) had a major protocol deviation based
on medical review.

No deviations were identified that would affect the interpretation of results or conclusion of
the primary analysis. Primary efficacy analysis was conducted in the efficacy population, with

The FDA’s Assessment:
A total of 522 instances of protocol deviations were reported in the efficacy population
composed of patients with RET fusion-positive metastatic NSCLC with measurable disease,
treated at 400 mg QD enrolled by date (N=114), of which 3 events were considered to be major
by the Applicant, 55 were considered important deviations and 464 non-important deviations.
The three major protocol deviations were: patient ID
had driver mutation in
ctDNA (EGFR deletion that removes exons 2 & 3 that code for part of the extracellular domain),
(b) (6)
patient ID
had history of brain metastases with baseline brain scan after 1st dose
(b) (6)
on study), and patient ID
had history of asymptomatic brain metastases with no
baseline brain scan.
(b) (6)

The most common protocol deviations (388/519, 75%) were due to delayed or missing
laboratory, ECG, serum for PK or imaging at the protocol specified time points.

FDA Clarification: FDA discovered a discrepancy in the dataset for the two patients in the prior
platinum therapy subgroup reported as having stage IIIB NSCLC at screening. Blueprint
acknowledged the discrepancy and provided additional information for these two patients.
One patient had stage IIIB disease at screening with involvement of left hilar, subcarinal and
right paratracheal lymph nodes, while the other had stage IV, M1a disease (metastatic) at
screening with tumors in the left upper lobe and right upper lobe, as well as involvement of
right hilar lymph nodes.
FDA Clarification: The Applicant indicated that their calculation of patients with “CNS
metastases” included patients who had medical history of metastases site in CNS/Brain from
CRF data, or patients whose target or non-target lesion(s) location were in CNS/Brain,
brainstem, cerebellum, frontoparietal, frontal lobe and frontotemporal at baseline according to
RECIST v1.1 by BICR data. Hence according to the Applicant's calculation, 10 out of 27 (37%)
patients had "CNS metastases" in the treatment-naive subgroup.

Pralsetinib
Treatment Compliance
Data:
Median relative dose intensity, which provides a measure of total dose administered relative to
expected dose, was 91.9% across all NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD. There were no
clinically relevant differences among the prior treatment groups of NSCLC patients treated at
400 mg QD.
Most NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD in the efficacy population received concomitant
medications during the study (99.2%). The most common concomitant medications were in the
Anatomic Therapeutic Chemical class of Analgesics and Antipyretics (63.6%), followed by
Opioids (53.0%), and Constipation Medications (52.3%). There were no clinically relevant
differences in the types of concomitant medications among the prior treatment groups of
NSCLC patients.
Important protocol deviations related to dosing were reported in 2 patients (1.5%).
The Applicant’s Position:
Compliance was high in the efficacy population. There were only 2 cases of protocol deviations
related to dosing that were considered important. This was considered unlikely to affect
efficacy results.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In Study BLU-667-1101, patients were dispensed the prescribed number of study drug bottles
to allow for dosing for a full cycle, or until the next scheduled visit. Patients were instructed to
return all unused drugs or the empty bottles on day 1 of each treatment cycle or the next
scheduled visit. Compliance with the dosing regimen was assessed based on return of unused
drug or empty bottles.
Issues with investigational product handling/retention to assess compliance were reported in 6
out of 114 patients in the efficacy population. The patient ID and the reason for the deviation
are listed in the following table.

Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
Data:
ORR in patients with RET-fusion positive NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD overall was 56.8%
(95% CI: 47.9, 65.4): 8 patients (6.1%) with CR and 67 patients (50.8%) with PR (Table 30).
Beyond that, 41 patients (31.1%) had SD and 10 patients (7.6%) had PD. ORR results were
similar among NSCLC patients irrespective of prior treatment: ORR was 54.4% (95% CI: 44.3,
64.2) in NSCLC patients with any prior systemic treatment, 53.3% (95% CI: 42.6, 63.7) in NSCLC
patients with prior platinum treatment, and 65.5% (95% CI: 45.7, 82.1) in NSCLC patients with
no prior systemic treatment.
RET genotype was determined by local testing and/or central analysis of ctDNA.
ORR in NSCLC patients with KIF5B genotype (N=94) was 59.6% (95% CI: 49.0, 69.6): 4 patients
(4.3%) with CR and 52 patients (55.3%) with PR. Beyond that, 28 patients (29.8%) had SD and
7 patients (7.4%) had PD.
ORR in NSCLC patients with CCDC6 genotype (N=22) was 63.6% (95% CI: 40.7, 82.8): 3 patients

The Applicant’s Position:
Based on ORR analysis pralsetinib was highly active in RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients
treated at 400 mg QD, across all lines of prior therapy. It must be noted that according to the
initial study design, patients without prior systemic treatment had to be ineligible for standard
platinum-based therapy before enrolling into the study. As a result, these patients had more
advanced cancer and a poorer prognosis. Despite the poor prognosis of these patients, a high
ORR was achieved.

Pralsetinib
Duration of Response
Median DOR in the 75 NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD with a confirmed response has not
been reached at the time of data cutoff (95% CI: 11.3, -), with 61 patients (81.3%) still in
response and 14 patients (18.7%) with progression (Table 32). Median (range) time to first
response was 1.81 months (1.0 to 9.1 months). Estimated DOR rates were 86.0% at 6 months
(95% CI: 77.0, 95.1) and 74.6% at 9 months (95% CI: 61.3, 87.8) overall. A total of 41 patients
(54.7%) had DOR ≥ 6 months overall (64.3% in the any prior treatment group, 61.2% in the prior
platinum group, and 26.3% in the no prior treatment group), 22 patients (29.3%) had DOR
≥ 9 months overall (35.7% in the any prior treatment group, 30.6% in the prior platinum group,
and 10.5% in the no prior treatment group), and 6 patients (8.0%) had DOR of ≥ 12 months
overall (10.7% in the any prior treatment group, 10.2% in the prior platinum group, and no
patients in the no prior treatment group).
Of the 34 patients with estimated DOR rate of < 6 months, 20 patients were still in response
(10 patients in any prior treatment group, 9 patients in the prior platinum group, and
10 patients in the no prior treatment group).
DOR results were similar among NSCLC patients irrespective of prior treatment: Median DOR
has not been reached at the time of data cutoff (95% CI: 11.3, -) in NSCLC patients with any
prior systemic treatment, with 46 patients (82.1%) still in response and 10 patients (17.9%) with
progression. Estimated DOR rates were 85.8% (95% CI: 75.9, 95.6) at 6 months and 78.0%
(95% CI: 64.3, 91.6) at 9 months in this group. Median DOR in NSCLC patients with prior
platinum treatment was also not reached (95% CI: 11.3, -), with 39 patients (79.6%) still in
response and 10 patients (20.4%) with progression. Estimated DOR rates were 83.7% (95% CI:
72.6, 94.8) at 6 months and 73.9% (95% CI: 57.8, 90.0) at 9 months in this group. Median DOR
in NSCLC patients with no prior treatment is currently 7.4 months (95% CI: 6.3, -), although it is
noted that this estimate is preliminary as 15 patients (78.9%) remain in response and only
4 patients (21.1%) have experienced progression. Estimated DOR rates were 87.5% (95% CI:
64.6, 100.0) at 6 months and 46.7% (95% CI: 2.4, 91.0) at 9 months in this group.
It must be noted that, according to the initial study design, patients without prior systemic
treatment had to be ineligible for standard platinum-based therapy before enrolling into the
study. As a result, these initial patients had more advanced cancer and a poor prognosis. With
Amendment 9, more typical, first-line, treatment-naïve patients were enrolled. Thus, most of
the NSCLC patients who had no prior treatment were enrolled later in the study and their
response duration is not as long as that possible in other groups at this data cut.
RET genotype was determined by local testing and/or central analysis of ctDNA.
Median DOR in NSCLC patients with KIF5B genotype (N=56) was not reached (95% CI: 8.8, -),
with 42 patients (75.0%) still in response and 14 patients (25.0%) with progression. Estimated

Clinical Benefit Rate
CBR in this patient population was 68.2%, with 90 patients having confirmed CR, PR, or SD for
≥ 4 cycles (Table 30). CBR results were similar irrespective of prior treatment. In patients with
no prior treatment, CBR was 65.5% (95% CI: 45.7, 82.1).
Disease Control Rate
DCR in NSCLC patients was 87.9% (95% CI: 81.1, 92.9), with 116 patients having CR, PR, or SD
(Table 34). There were no clinically relevant differences in DCR among the prior treatment
groups of NSCLC patients. In patients with no prior treatment, DCR was 79.3% (95% CI: 60.3,
92.0).

Progression-free Survival
A total of 82 patients (62.1%) were censored for analysis and 50 patients (37.9%) had a PFS
event (Table 35). Estimated median PFS was 12.7 months (95% CI: 9.1, -). The estimated PFS
rate was 88.0% at 3 months, 69.1% at 6 months, 60.5% at 9 months, and 51.6% at 12 months.
Median PFS KM estimate was 13.1 months (95% CI: 8.8, -) in NSCLC patients with any prior
systemic treatment, 13.1 months (95% CI: 7.7, -) in NSCLC patients with prior platinum
treatment, and 9.9 months (95% CI: 5.6, -) in NSCLC patients with no prior systemic treatment.

Overall Survival
A total of 95 patients (72.0%) were alive, 26 (19.7%) died, 1 (< 1%) was lost to follow-up, and 10
(7.6%) withdrew consent (Table 36). KM estimate for median OS could not be determined at a
median follow-up of 10.5 months. The estimated OS rate was 85.7% at 6 months, 78.8% at
9 months, and 73.8% at 12 months. Median OS follow-up time KM estimate was 11.5 months
(95% CI: 9.9, 13.3) in NSCLC patients with any prior systemic treatment, 11.0 months (95% CI:
9.9, 13.3) in NSCLC patients with prior platinum treatment, and 8.3 months (95% CI: 6.9, 11.5)
in NSCLC patients with no prior systemic treatment.

The observed DOR, PFS, CBR, DCR, and OS in RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients treated at

population with high unmet medical need.

treatment had to be ineligible for standard platinum-based therapy before enrolling into the
study. As a result, these initial patients had more advanced cancer and a poorer prognosis
contributing to shorter apparent DOR, PFS, and OS. However, despite the fact that these were
poor prognosis patients due to original eligibility criteria, clinically meaningful results were

Efficacy Results – Secondary or Exploratory Clinical Outcome Assessment (Patient-reported
Outcomes) Endpoints
Data:
Quality of Life
EORTC QLQ-C30 were collected for patients enrolled on or after Protocol Amendment 7 during
Phase 2, where all patients’ starting dose was 400 mg QD. Overall, the median (range) global
health status score in NSCLC patients was 83.0 (75 to 100) of 100 possible points at the last
assessment (Week 48) in the EORTC QLQ-C30, with minimal changes (≤ 16 points) from baseline
observed at all timepoints over a duration of 48 weeks, except a change of 25.0 points at
Week 40 in the prior platinum treatment group and for 1 patient in the no prior treatment
group at the last assessment. High mean and median scores have been reported for physical,
role, emotional, cognitive, and social functioning indicating a high level of functioning, with
minimal changes from baseline observed at all timepoints (mostly < 10 points change from
baseline). At baseline, patients reported low levels of clinical symptoms, which remained low
with little changes throughout all timepoints for fatigue, nausea/vomiting, dyspnea, appetite
loss, and diarrhea. From baseline to last assessment, there was a tendency toward less pain and
insomnia (change of ≤ 16 points) and toward more constipation (consistent with what was seen
for AE reporting). Low mean and median scores were observed for the scale financial
difficulties, with no relevant changes from baseline observed at all timepoints.
The Applicant’s Position:
Patient-reported overall quality of life assessments showed that patients’ quality of life was
maintained throughout treatment. Quality of life in respective functioning subscores was
consistent with AE reporting and as expected for this patient population. There were no
clinically relevant differences in quality of life results among the prior treatment groups of
NSCLC patients.
The FDA’s Assessment:
There are many factors that make patient-reported outcome data from Study BLU-667-1101
difficult to interpret. This study was open-label, so patient knowledge of treatment assignment

may induce bias in the completion of PRO instruments. There was no pre-specified analysis plan

analyses for this non-randomized study, it is difficult to attribute any observed within-patient
differences of PROs to the experimental therapy, as the results cannot be put into context for

Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
Data:
Pralsetinib Against Intracranial Metastases
Analysis of best overall response in patients with CNS metastases was performed in the
response-evaluable population, which included CNS patients from the efficacy population who
had a measurable (target) disease per RECIST v1.1 at baseline, ≥ 1 evaluable postbaseline
disease response assessment performed as per BICR, and no major protocol violations
(incomplete baseline imaging; patient in Groups 1, 2, 5, 6, or 7 without evidence of RET
mutation or fusion; patient had another known primary driver alteration other than RET
identified by central ctDNA/tumor assay).
Among the 9 response-evaluable patients treated at 400 mg QD with measurable intracranial
target lesions at baseline, the RECIST v1.1 CNS ORR was 55.6% (95% CI: 21.2, 86.3) (3 patients
with CR, 2 patients with PR) (Table 37). Beyond that, 4 patients (44.4%) had SD.
In the efficacy population, among the NSCLC patients without CNS involvement prior to study
entry, none of the 77 treated at 400 mg QD developed new CNS involvement during the study
according to central review data. Among the 8 NSCLC patients without CNS involvement who
received lower pralsetinib starting doses in the efficacy population, 1 patient developed new
CNS involvement. This patient was treated at 100 mg QD for the first 5.5 months on study, then
escalated to 300 mg QD before developing new CNS disease at 7.3 months on study.

Pralsetinib penetrates the blood-brain barrier and is efficacious in brain metastases.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among the 87 patients with RET fusion-positive NSCLC who had received prior platinum-based
chemotherapy, 8 had measurable CNS metastases at baseline as assessed by BICR. No patients
received radiation therapy to the brain within 2 months prior to study entry. Responses in
intracranial lesions, as assessed by the BIRC per RECIST 1.1, were observed in 4 of these 8
patients, including 2 patients with a CNS complete response; 75% of responders had a DOR of
≥6 months. One patient who had received prior PD-1/PD-L1 inhibitor and had measurable CNS
metastases at baseline achieved a complete response as assessed by BICR per RECIST 1.1.
Subgroup Analyses
Data:
There were no statistically relevant differences in pralsetinib efficacy in NSCLC patients treated
at 400 mg QD based on time to response, DOR, and ORR for the subgroups of age (< 65 years,
≥ 65 years), sex (females, males), geographic region (US, Europe, Asia), or race (Asian,
non-Asian). There were also no statistically relevant differences in ORR by prior anticancer
therapy (prior PD-1/PD-L1 treatment or not, prior MKI treatment or not).
The Applicant’s Position:
No apparent outliers were observed in subgroup analyses.

The following table provides FDA’s exploratory ORR analysis by age, sex, and race separately in
the prior platinum therapy and treatment naïve subgroups. These subgroup analyses are
considered exploratory; however, there were no obvious outliers were observed in these
analyses.

Integrated Review of Effectiveness
The FDA’s Assessment:
The clinical and statistical review teams conclude that the Applicant provided substantial
evidence of effectiveness of pralsetinib in the following populations:
1) adult patients with treatment-naïve RET fusion-positive metastatic NSCLC and
2) adult patients with RET-fusion positive metastatic NSCLC who have previously received
platinum-based chemotherapy.
Assessment of Efficacy Across Trials
This section is not applicable because the application is supported by one trial.
Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
Data:
Based on the efficacy analyses at the time of data cutoff, pralsetinib is highly active in
RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD, across all lines of prior therapy.
The key efficacy results for the efficacy population were:
• ORR based on central radiology review by RECIST v1.1 was 56.8% (95% CI: 47.9, 65.4) in
the overall NSCLC population (N=132);

Overall, there were no clinically significant differences in efficacy among the prior

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The Applicant’s Position:
The efficacy results from NSCLC patients in Study BLU-667-1101 show that pralsetinib
treatment at a dose of 400 mg in a QD schedule offers a substantial improvement over current
standard treatments for patients with RET-altered advanced NSCLC. The ORR for confirmed CR
or PR (primary endpoint) was 56.8% (95% CI: 47.9, 65.4) and was similar irrespective of prior
treatment: ORR was 54.4% with any prior systemic treatment, 53.3% with prior platinum
treatment, and 65.5% with no prior systemic treatment. This is more than double the ORR that
was achieved with the current standard of care for post platinum treatment (9% to 14%)
(Borghaei et al, 2015; Brahmer et al, 2015; Fehrenbacher et al, 2018; Garon et al, 2014; Hanna
et al, 2004) and also higher than the 46% (95% CI: 41, 50) for first-line patients treated with
atezolizumab plus protein-bound paclitaxel plus carboplatin (Atezolizumab USPI, Dec 2019) and
the 47.6% (95% CI: 42.6, 52.5) achieved for first-line patients treated with the triple
combination of pembrolizumab plus pemetrexed plus platinum-based chemotherapy (Gandhi
et al, 2018). Moreover, the higher ORR in first-line NSCLC patients in this study was achieved
despite the fact that these were poor prognosis patients due to the original eligibility criteria.
Furthermore, the DOR rate, DCR, PFS, and estimated 12-month OS rate were all better with
pralsetinib treatment than what has been reported with current standard treatments in the
nonsquamous population, and these efficacy outcomes were seen regardless of whether
patients had any prior systemic treatment or no prior systemic treatment (Table 39).

The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on the review of clinical data from Study BLU-667-1101 (ARROW), the clinical and
statistical review teams conclude that the Applicant provided substantial evidence of
effectiveness of pralsetinib in patients with RET fusion-positive metastatic NSCLC adequate to
support accelerated approval.
Data from Study BLU-667-1101 demonstrated that pralsetinib administered to patients with
RET fusion-positive metastatic NSCLC who have previously received platinum-based
chemotherapy (N=87) results in an ORR per RECIST 1.1 as assessed by a BIRC of 57% (95% CI 46,
68). Tumor responses were durable, with 80% having responses lasting ≥6 months. In the
population of patients with treatment-naïve RET fusion-positive NSCLC (N=27), the ORR was
70% (95% CI 50, 86), with 58% having responses lasting ≥6 months.
ORR of sufficient magnitude in conjunction with a clinically meaningful duration of response has
been accepted by the Office of Oncology to be likely to predict clinical benefit in patients with
rare NSCLC mutations. The reviewer notes that given the small sample size, in particular for the
population of patients with treatment-naïve RET fusion-positive NSCLC (N=27), the magnitude
of ORR and duration of response with pralsetinib is associated with some uncertainty.
Additional data from the ongoing Study BLU-667-1101 (ARROW) are warranted to verify benefit
in this population. Blueprint has also initiated a randomized, controlled trial of pralsetinib
compared to platinum-based chemotherapy with or without pembrolizumab as first-line
treatment for patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC (Study BLUE-667-2303;
AcceleRET Lung), and submission of data from this trial will be requested as a post-marketing
commitment to provide additional information regarding outcomes with pralsetinib in the
treatment-naïve patient population.

Review of Safety
Data:

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The primary evidence for the safety of pralsetinib in patients with RET-positive locally advanced
or metastatic NSCLC comes from the pivotal Study BLU-667-1101. The study included a Phase 1
dose escalation part to determine the MTD and RP2D of pralsetinib, followed by a Phase 2 dose
expansion part to assess the clinical efficacy of pralsetinib in specific tumor types and treatment
settings (measured primarily by ORR) and further define the safety and tolerability at the
MTD/RP2D.
Included are safety data from patients enrolled in Study BLU-667-1101 up to
18 November 2019, the data cutoff date for this marketing authorization application.
(b) (4)
A total of 404 patients have received ≥ 1 dose of pralsetinib with any indication (NSCLC,
(b) (4)
(i.e., consistent with the safety population), including
62 patients enrolled in the Phase 1 and 342 patients in the Phase 2. Of those, 354 patients
received pralsetinib at a starting dose of 400 mg QD in either Phase 1 or Phase 2, regardless of
tumor type or mutation status. A total of 179 patients with NSCLC were treated at 400 mg QD.
Any SAEs from 19 November through 18 December 2019 are reported, for completeness, as
late-breaking safety information.
The assessment of safety is supported by the data from 5 clinical pharmacology studies in
healthy subjects.
The Applicant’s Position:
Data from the pivotal Study BLU-667-1101 allowed for a comprehensive and adequate
assessment of the safety profile of pralsetinib and an evaluation of the overall benefit-risk in
adult patients with RET-positive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC at a dose of 400 mg QD.
The 404 patients included in the safety population includes 354 patients treated at 400 mg QD
and 179 NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD, which are considered adequate for the
detection and characterization of common AEs, and to provide guidance on management of
AEs.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees that the available safety data allow for an adequate assessment of the safety
profile of pralsetinib. The safety population used to inform Section 5 of labeling is all patients
who started pralsetinib at 400 mg once daily, the proposed dosing regimen for this NDA. Data
from additional patients who received a starting dose of 400 mg once daily was available after
the 90-day safety update (DCO February 13, 2020). Thus, 438 patients were included in the
safety analysis population for FDA’s review.
Safety Review Approach
Data:
The data presented here focus on all patients treated with pralsetinib at 400 mg QD (N=354) as

of the NDA data cutoff date (18Nov2019). Summary of safety data from patients with RETpositive locally advanced or metastatic NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD (N=179) is also provided.
Patients included were those exposed to ≥ 1 dose of pralsetinib at 400 mg on the initial dose
prescribed on Day 1 in either phase of Study BLU-667-1101.
Of the 354 patients treated at 400 mg QD, the most common AEs (reported in > 20%) were AST
increased, constipation, anaemia, diarrhoea, ALT increased, hypertension, pyrexia and fatigue.
The events of pneumonia, pneumonitis, and tumor lysis syndrome were identified as AESIs
during the development of pralsetinib, based on the clinical experience taking into
consideration multiple factors such as incidence, severity, relationship to study drug, medical
impact, and clinical outcome of the AEs.
Among the 438 patients treated at 400 mg QD as of Day 90 Safety Update (data cutoff date
13Feb2020), the profile of common AEs (in ≥ 20% of patients) was consistent with what was
reported in the initial NDA. The only additional PTs that met the ≥ 20% incidence threshold
through the update period that had not met this threshold in the initial NDA were white blood
cell count decreased (increase from 16.1% to 20.3%) and neutropenia (19.8% to 20.1%). As in
the initial NDA, most common events in all patients treated at 400 mg QD (in ≥ 20% of patients)
by PTs included AST increased, constipation, anaemia, hypertension, , ALT increased,
diarrhoea, fatigue, WBC count decreased, neutropenia and pyrexia.
The Applicant’s Position:
Safety data from clinical studies showed that the common AEs of pralsetinib among all patients
treated at 400 mg QD were primarily AST increased, constipation, anaemia, diarrhoea, ALT
increased, and hypertension, with AESIs of pneumonia, pneumonitis, and tumor lysis syndrome
identified as part of the comprehensive safety review during the development of pralsetinib.
The FDA’s Assessment: FDA’s assessment of the most common adverse reactions in patients
with RET fusion-positive NSCLC and a discussion of safety issues considered for inclusion in the
Warnings and Precautions section of the pralsetinib label are presented in the sections below.

all doses/schedules, and 179 NSCLC patients received pralsetinib at a starting dose of

Adequacy of the safety database:
Data:
The safety database for this submission included 404 patients who received ≥ 1 dose of
pralsetinib from the pivotal Study BLU-667-1101. Among these 404 patients, 354 patients were
treated at 400 mg QD and 179 had NSCLC and were treated with 400 mg QD. Among the 354
patients, the median (range) treatment duration was 5.52 (0.1, 22.3) months (Table 40). Among
the 179 patients, the median (range) treatment duration was 5.16 (0.2 to 19.8) months.
As of the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), the median (range)
treatment duration was 5.24 (<1, 25.1) in patients treated at 400 mg QD which was consistent
with what was reported in the initial NDA.
The Applicant’s Position:
The safety database was considered adequate to evaluate the safety of pralsetinib in the
intended population at the target dose.

Pralsetinib
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the adequacy of the safety database.
Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
Data:
All safety evaluations conducted during Study BLU-667-1101, including monitoring for AEs,
physical examinations, vital sign measurements, clinical laboratory tests, and ECGs are
considered standard (i.e., widely used and generally recognized as reliable, accurate, and
relevant).
Study BLU-667-1101 was conducted according to procedures that incorporate the ethical
principles of GCP. To ensure compliance with these procedures and to assess the adequacy of
quality control procedures, audits of study documentation, Investigator centers, and the CSR
were performed by an external auditor that operated independently of the study monitors.
The Applicant’s Position:
No meaningful concerns are anticipated in the quality and integrity of the submitted datasets
and individual case narratives; these were sufficiently complete to allow for a thorough review
of safety. Furthermore, no data integrity concerns were reported following completion of site
audits; data in the electronic case report forms and AE databases were consistent.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The data submitted was organized and adequate to perform a complete review of the safety of
pralsetinib. Overall, FDA agrees that there were no significant data quality or reporting issues
identified during the review of this NDA. FDA did issue several information requests during the
review cycle to obtain clarification and additional information regarding safety data included in
the NDA.
Categorization of Adverse Events
Data:
Each patient was carefully monitored for the development of any AEs throughout the study
from the time of signing informed consent to 30 days after the last dose. In addition, SAEs that
were assessed as related to study treatment that occurred > 30 days post-treatment were
reported.
All AEs (serious and nonserious) spontaneously reported by the patient and/or in response to
180
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
an open question from study personnel or revealed by observation, physical examination, or
other diagnostic procedures were recorded in the appropriate section of the electronic case
report form.
Adverse events were evaluated based on TEAEs, which were defined as AEs that occurred
during or after administration of the first dose of pralsetinib through 30 days after the last dose,
any event that was considered pralsetinib-related regardless of start date, or any event that
was present at baseline but worsened in intensity, or was subsequently considered pralsetinib­
related by the Investigator.
All AEs were coded using MedDRA version 19.1. The severity was graded per the NCI CTCAE
version 4.03.
Relationship to study drug administration was determined by the Investigator according to the
following criteria:
• Not related: Exposure to the study treatment did not occur, or the occurrence of the AE
was not reasonably related in time, or the AE was clearly caused by something other
than pralsetinib;
• Related: The AE did not meet the criteria for “not related”.
The safety of treatment with pralsetinib was evaluated on the basis of the following:
• Incidence, type, severity, and causal relationship of AEs to study treatment;
• Incidence of deaths, SAEs, and other clinically significant AEs (including AEs leading to
interruption of study drug, dose reduction of study drug, and permanent
discontinuation of study drug);
• Incidence, type, and severity of AEs by age, race, geographic region, and sex;
• Incidence of AESIs (pneumonia, pneumonitis, and tumor lysis syndrome), where
categories (pneumonia and pneumonitis) were defined by groups of selected and
relevant PTs;
• Incidence of other grouped AEs (neutropenia and thrombocytopenia);
• Exploratory time to event analyses including time to onset, time to improvement, and
time to resolution for AESI terms and neutropenia and hypertension.
The Applicant’s Position:
The approach to the analyses of AEs was appropriate in evaluating the safety data.
The FDA’s Assessment:
For purposes of the FDA review of safety, incidences of adverse events were analyzed without
consideration of relatedness, given the safety data supporting this NDA is from a nonrandomized, non-comparative study.

Routine Clinical Tests
Data:
The tables of study procedures and scheduled assessments in the protocol for Study BLU-667­
1101 specified the timepoints at which laboratory evaluations were conducted.
Clinical laboratory values were graded according to NCI CTCAE version 4.03 for applicable
parameters except creatinine, which was graded according to NCI CTCAE version 5.0 to correct
the grading error for this specific laboratory test used in NCI CTCAE version 4.03.
Overall summary and changes from baseline were presented for hematology, serum chemistry
(including coagulation), and thyroid test laboratory values. Shift tables of laboratory data from
baseline to worst grade during treatment were presented. Urinalysis was presented in listings
only.
For the overall safety population at all doses and NSCLC patients at 400 mg QD, boxplots were
presented by visit for selected laboratory tests including ALT, AST, alkaline phosphatase, total
bilirubin, phosphate, sodium, calcium, corrected calcium, potassium, hemoglobin, leukocytes,
neutrophils (absolute), lymphocytes (absolute), platelets, and thyroid-stimulating hormone.
Results outside predefined normal ranges and NCI CTCAE grade were flagged in the data
listings.
The Applicant’s Position:
The laboratory evaluation (laboratory tests and timepoints) as described in the protocols was
reasonable and adequate to monitor the safety of the population, disease, and indication being
investigated.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s statement. Please refer to Table 15: Schedule of Assessments
for schedule of assessments. For hyperphosphatemia, FDA requested additional analyses and
re-grading of hyperphosphatemia utilizing NCI CTCAE version 5.0.
Safety Results
Deaths
Data:
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354), death due to a SAE were reported in 29
patients (8.2%), including 1 patient due to a related SAE (pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia).
These SAEs included pneumonia cytomegaloviral, pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, dyspnoea,
182
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
respiratory failure, acute myocardial infarction, cerebrovascular accident, sepsis, urinary tract
infection, asthenia, and haemorrhage intracranial (in 1 patient each), medullary thyroid cancer
(2 patients), pneumonia (3 patients), disease progression (14 patients).
In NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD, deaths due to SAEs were reported in 19 patients, and
none of these deaths was assessed as related to pralsetinib by the Investigator. Specifically,
these SAEs were pneumonia (3 patients), pneumonia cytomegaloviral, dyspnoea,
cerebrovascular accident, sepsis, urinary tract infection, asthenia, and haemorrhage intracranial
(1 patient each), and disease progression (9 patients).
Since the data cutoff of 18 November 2019, 5 additional deaths were reported through the
late-breaking data cutoff date of 18 December 2019. One event of pneumonitis was considered
related to pralsetinib treatment. The patient had SAEs of febrile neutropenia (Grade 3), acute
myocardial infarction (Grade 3), sepsis (Grade 3), cerebral infarction (Grade 4), and
pneumonitis (Grade 5), which led to the patient’s death.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), no relevant increase
(≥ 1%) in incidence was noted for treatment-related deaths. Among all patients treated at 400
mg QD, 40 patients (9.1%) died during the study due to AEs and 3 additional deaths were
reported due to a related AE (pneumonitis, and death [2 patients]). The event of pneumonitis
was included in the late breaking section of the initial NDA. Twenty patients (4.6%) died due to
disease progression.
The Applicant’s Position:
In the safety population of patients treated at 400 mg QD as well as in NSCLC patients treated
at 400 mg QD, the SAEs leading to death were mostly associated with patients’ underlying
disease and/or complications. Most deaths were due to disease progression. This is consistent
with data from the overall safety population of patients treated at all doses/schedules.

Patient narratives for deaths due to adverse events of pneumonia, pneumonitis or sepsis were
reviewed in detail and are summarized below. In Section 6 of USPI, which will present data for
the NSCLC safety population (n=220), pneumonia (n=3) and sepsis (n=2) will be listed as fatal
adverse reactions occurring in >1 patient (see details below).
Pneumonia
-

Patient
A 66 year old female patient with NSCLC metastatic to adrenals,
bone, CNS/brain and liver. Patient was hospitalized due to pulmonary embolism; a few
days into her hospital stay she was diagnosed with pneumonia cytomegaloviral
infection. CT revealed patchy areas of increased density with a ground glass pattern and
a tendency toward confluence and diffuse affectation of both lung fields. Patient was
treated with ganciclovir 5 mg/kg IV every 12 hours. However, after 7 days of treatment
patient died due to pneumonia cytomegaloviral.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comments: Although the patient’s death was due to pneumonia
cytomegaloviral and it cannot be ruled out that pralsetinib did not contribute to this
event, the patient was previously taking dexamethasone prior to and while on study,
which may have pre-disposed her to an infection.
-

Patient
: 81 year old male patient diagnosed with MTC was metastatic to
lung and lymph nodes was diagnosed with pneumonitis and hospitalized. CT scan
showed ground glass opacities and areas of consolidation predominantly in the perihilar
region. The next day patient was diagnosed with pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia.
Treatment included sulfamethoxazole and trimethoprim and was admitted to the ICU.
CT angiogram was performed and showed no evidence of pulmonary embolism and
clear intensification of interstitial lung disease. 10 days later patient died in the ICU due
to respiratory insufficiency secondary to pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: Applicant attributed cause of death, pneumocystis jirovecii
pneumonia, to pralsetinib, and FDA agrees based on the information provided.
-

Patient
: 59 year old female patient with PTC metastatic to bone,
CNS/brain, liver, and lung was hospitalized due to pneumonia. Physical exam showed
crepitation in right lung and x-ray showed opacity consistent with right middle lobe
pneumonia. Labs showed neutropenia and severe thrombocytopenia and leucopenia.
Treatment was discontinued, however the following morning patient experienced
cardiac arrest and died due to pneumonia.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: Patient was neutropenic; neutropenia is consistent with the known

-

Patient
: 56 year old male patient with NSCLC metastatic to bone,
CNS/brain, liver and lung with left plural effusion experienced pneumonia 4 different
times while on treatment. Patient experienced grade 3 pneumonia which required
hospitalization. CT scan confirmed pneumonia. The event resolved after appropriate
antibiotic treatment. However, 12 days following the report of resolution, patient
experienced hemoptysis and aggravation of dyspnea. CT scan revealed pneumonia in
the lung and increased irregular ground glass opacity in both lungs and consolidation in
both lungs. A week following event patient experienced Grade 5 pneumonia and died
the same day.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: There is a possibility that pneumonia events were related to
pralsetinib; however, definite attribution is confounded due to risk factors as well as
patient’s poor condition. .
-

Patient
: 77 year old male patient with NSCLC metastatic to CNS/brain,
lung, lymph nodes, enterocoelia, and pleura experienced hypophosphatemia which
required hospitalization. The day after the patient experienced Grade 4 pneumonia.
Patient was treated with cefoperazone sodium/sulbactam sodium, cilastatin
sodium/imipenem and carbazochrome. However, the patient’s condition worsened the
following day and an X-ray showed right lung cancer and bilateral pneumonia. Patient
died the following day due to lung infection.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: Patient’s pneumonia may be attributed to complication of disease;
however, pralsetinib’s role in patient’s pneumonia cannot be ruled out.
Sepsis
-

Patient
: 59 year old female patient with NSCLC metastatic to bone,
CNS/brain, liver, lung an lymph nodes experienced sepsis which required hospitalization.
Patient had bacterial peritonitis post cholecystectomy. Labs showed neutrophils,
leukocytes, platelets and hemoglobin to be abnormal. Klebsiella pneumonia was
detected in the blood culture. Patient was treated with meropenem, vancomycin, and
linezolid. Patient died due to worsening of septic condition.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comments: Pneumonia may have contributed to sepsis. While assessment of
attribution is difficult due to confounding factors of bacterial peritonitis and patient’s
prolonged and complicated hospital stay, contribution of pralsetinib cannot be ruled out.

-

Patient
: 60 year old male patient diagnosed with MTC metastatic to bone
and lung experienced Grade 3 pneumonitis and was admitted to the hospital. Ongoing
conditions included poor appetite, productive cough and waist pain. CT angiogram was
done, and pneumonitis was confirmed. Treatment with study drug was interrupted.

Patient
: 57 year old female patient with NSCLC metastatic to the liver;
prior anti-cancer therapies included carboplatin, pemetrexed, and selpercatinib. On
Study Day 30 this patient experienced febrile neutropenia which required
hospitalization. Patient’s stay was complicated by MI and sepsis. Her hospital stay was
then prolonged due to pneumonitis. CT scan showed extensive ground glass densities
and interlobular septal thickening in both lungs, which was consistent with
inflammatory process rather than infectious process. All infectious work up was
negative. Dexamethasone was started. She continued her hospital stay but patient
experienced cerebral infarction and pneumonitis was considered resolved. However,
the following day patient experienced pneumonitis which led to patient’s death on the
same day.
(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: Xrays and cultures did not indicate pneumonia and Applicant noted
pneumonitis was related to study drug. FDA agrees with this assessment.

Pralsetinib
Serious Adverse Events
Data:
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354), serious adverse events were reported in 157
patients (44.1%), of whom 58 (16.4%) had related SAEs. In patients with NSCLC treated at 400
mg QD, SAEs were reported for 91 patients (50.8%), of whom 36 (20.1%) had related SAEs. The
most common SAEs in all patients treated at 400 mg QD (occurring in ≥ 2 patients) were
pneumonia (8.2%), disease progression (5.1%), pneumonitis (4.0%), sepsis (2.5%), urinary tract
infection (2.8%), and pyrexia (2.5%), which were also the most common SAEs in NSCLC patients
treated at 400 mg QD.
The most common related SAEs (occurring in ≥ 2 patients in all patients treated at 400 mg QD)
were pneumonitis (3.1%), pneumonia (2.0%), and anaemia, hypertension, neutropenia,
pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, sepsis, and thrombocytopenia (< 1% each) (Table 47). Of the
13 patients with related SAEs of pneumonitis, 11 patients were treated with 400 mg QD as their
starting dose.
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, Grade 3 related SAEs were reported by 39 patients
(11.0%), Grade 4 related SAEs were reported in 10 patients (2.8%), and Grade 5 related SAEs
were reported in 1 patient (<1%). Similar percentages of patients reported each grade in the
NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD.

Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), among all patients
treated at 400 mg QD, no notable differences in the profile of SAEs were seen compared to
what was reported in the initial NDA. Serious adverse events were reported in 195 patients
(44.5%), of whom 77 (17.6%) had related SAEs. The most common SAEs in all patients treated
at 400 mg QD (occurring in ≥ 2 patients) were pneumonia (7.5 %), disease progression (5.7%),
pneumonitis (4.6%), urinary tract infection (3.0%), sepsis (2.5%), and pyrexia (2.3%).
The Applicant’s Position:
The most common SAEs in all patients treated at 400 mg QD (occurring in ≥ 2 patients) were
pneumonia (8.2%), disease progression (5.1%), pneumonitis (4.0%), sepsis (2.5%), urinary tract
infection (2.8%), and pyrexia (2.5%), which were also the most common SAEs in NSCLC patients
treated at 400 mg QD.

Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The most common related SAEs (reported in ≥ 2 patients) were pneumonitis (3.1%) and
pneumonia (2.0%). Pneumonitis is a known risk associated with the use of TKIs as well as other
cancer therapies such as immune checkpoint inhibitors. The risk of pneumonia increases in
patients with lung cancer.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA confirmed the results in the above table based on the Applicant’s assessment of
relatedness. FDA completed additional analyses on serious events based on the 90 day update
and regardless of is the assessment of relatedness to pralsetinib. In order to fully characterize
the differences between the safety populations of patients with NSCLC and all patients treated
at 400 mg daily, all SAE which occurred in ≥ 1% of patients are presented. The incidence of SAE
was similar in the subgroup of patients with NSCLC and the all patients treated at 400 mg QD
safety population.

thromboembolic events and vision disorders were further examined. FDA requested additional
analyses of some of these events from the Applicant. An overview of these analyses, which are
based on the safety population of 438 patients treated with pralsetinib 400 mg QD, is provided
below.
FDA conducted additional assessments/analyses for potential adverse reactions related to
inhibition of the VEGF pathway, FGFR pathway and JAK1/2 pathway. Regarding potential
adverse reactions associated with the VEGF pathway, hypertension is discussed separately in
Section 8.2.5, since the Applicant included this in the evaluation of “Significant Adverse Events”.
VEGF pathway
Hemorrhagic Events
Hemorrhagic events (any grade) were reported in 16% of patients as identified by the SMQ
hemorrhage, with 2.5% patients experienced Grade 3 events or higher. Dose modifications
occurred as a result of these events. One fatal hemorrhagic event was reported. The Applicant
noted that the fatal event of intracranial hemorrhage occurred in a 71 year old male patient
with advanced NSCLC and metastasis to the brain, liver and mediastinal lymph nodes. He was
receiving aspirin and clopidogrel at that time. He fell and imaging demonstrated a small
hemorrhagic left cerebellar metastatic lesion; the fatal event of intracranial hemorrhage
occurred about 3 weeks after initiation of pralsetinib. Although there are confounding factors
of CNS metastatic disease as well as dual anti-platelet therapy, there is a possibility that
pralsetinib may have contributed to the patient’s outcome.
Narratives were reviewed for all serious Grade 3 or higher events of hemorrhage were
reviewed, and all SAEs were associated with potential confounders/risk factors such as
concurrent anticoagulant treatment, biopsy procedure, accidental fall, and/or hemorrhagic
brain metastasis. However, based on non-clinical data inhibition of the VEGF pathway may
occur with pralsetinib. Thus, it’s possible pralsetinib may contribute to risk of experiencing a
hemorrhagic event. Given this and the potential seriousness of these events, Hemorrhagic
Events will be included in Section 5 of the USPI.

Hepatotoxicity
FDA requested additional information for Grade 3 or higher serious hepatic events. The
Applicant provided an analysis of hepatic events. There were 10 SAEs of Grade 3 or higher
hepatic events in 7 patients identified (see table below). Of these 7 patients, 5 patients
required dose modifications. No fatalities attributed to hepatic events were identified and no
events met Hy’s law criteria. Hepatic laboratory abnormalities of increased AST (69% Grade 1-4)
and increased ALT (46% Grade 1-4) were captured in assessment of laboratory shift tables
(Table 59 Select Lab Abnormalities (≥20%) Worsening from Baseline in Patient who received
400 mg of GAVRETO in ARROW) . The median time to first onset for increased AST was 15 days
and for increased ALT was 22 days. Based on this analysis, health care providers need to be
aware of recommended dose modifications for patients experiencing hepatic laboratory
abnormalities, and Hepatotoxicity will be included in Section 5 of the USPI.

GI perforations/fistulas
FDA requested an analysis of cases of GI perforations/fistulas. The Applicant’s analysis revealed
11 events of GI perforation/fistula in 8 patients. Confounders or risk factors of prior medical
history were present in 6 out of 8 patients as described in the table below. Based on the
currently available information, FDA did not deem it necessary to place GI perforations/fistulas
in Section 5 of the USPI at this time; however, based on the potential for pralsetinib to impact
the VEGF pathway and the fact that the limited available data is from a single arm study, a PMR
was requested to further characterize the incidence, clinical presentation, management and
outcome of GI perforations/fistulas.

Increased Risk of Impaired Wound of Healing
FDA requested additional information regarding impaired wound healing. The Applicant noted
there were only 4 events of impaired wound healing in 4 patients. One had a Grade 3 event and
the other 3 patients had a Grade 1 event. No events resulted in pralsetinib dose modifications.
Based on review of preclinical data for pralsetinib and clinical data for multiple drug products
that inhibit the VEGF pathway, FDA determined this may be a class effect and Section 5 of the
USPI will include Risk of Impaired Wound Healing with instructions to withhold pralsetinib for at
least 2 weeks following surgery and until adequate wound healing.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Cardiac failure
SMQ analyses for cardiac failure and cardiomyopathy were performed by the Applicant at the
request of FDA.
The SMQ analysis for cardiac failure did not generate any ≥ Grade 3 events with the preferred
terms peripheral edema, peripheral swelling, cardiac failure, cardiac failure congestive, edema,
orthopenea, and pulmonary edema. Although the search generated any grade events in 15% of
patients, the vast majority of these events were for the preferred term peripheral edema. The
SMQ for cardiomyopathy generated 22 events that were ≥ Grade 3, which included 8 events of
dyspnea and 7 events of syncope. This included one event of chest pain, three events of ascites,
one event of orthostatic hypotension and three events of mental status change. Events of
dyspnea and syncope were reviewed, but given confounding factors it was considered unlikely
that pralsetinib contributed to the occurrence of these events of cardiac dysfunction.
Thromboembolic events
SMQ analyses for thromboembolic events were performed by the Applicant at the request of
the FDA.
The SMQ analyses for embolic and thrombotic events, venous revealed 11 ≥ Grade 3 events
with the preferred terms pulmonary embolism, deep vein thrombosis, portal vein thrombosis,
thrombophlebitis superficial, and superior vena cava syndrome. Nine events of pulmonary
embolism occurred. These narratives were reviewed; most patients had NSCLC or had
metastases to the lungs as well as disease progression. The incidence is not higher than
expected in a population of patients with advanced malignancy. The SMQ for embolic and
thrombotic events, arterial generated 3 ≥ Grade 3 events which included preferred terms acute
myocardial infarction and myocardial infarction. There was one fatal event due to myocardial
infarction in a patient with multiple risk factors for cardiovascular disease, such as age,
hypertension, and diabetes. These analyses do not suggest a safety signal for pralsetinib for
thromboembolic events.
FGFR Pathway
Hyperphosphatemia
FDA requested the Applicant to provide additional analyses on patients with
hyperphosphatemia. At FDA’s request, the Applicant re-graded hyperphosphatemia using
CTCAE v5.0 for Grade 1-4. There were no serious cases of hyperphosphatemia, no patients had
phosphate values of >9 mg/dL and phosphate elevations were generally moderate.
FDA initially considered adding this event to Section 5 of the USPI as dose modifications and
196
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

management guidelines, including administration of phosphate binders, for
hyperphosphatemia were included in the amended protocol for ARROW. It was later revealed
by the Applicant that the Korean health authority, based on non-clinical findings, requested this
addition of management guidelines for hyperphosphatemia. Of note, the Applicant’s ongoing
randomized clinical trial, Study BLU-667-2303, does not include management guidelines for
hyperphosphatemia. Additionally, as indicated by the Applicant’s table below, approximately
50% of patients with abnormal phosphate values were not treated with phosphate binders.
Information submitted by the Applicant demonstrated that patients who were not treated with
phosphate binders did not have worsening hyperphosphatemia over time and administration of
phosphate binders did not appear to decrease the duration of hyperphosphatemia.
FDA added hyperphosphatemia to Section 6 of the USPI as a potentially clinically significant
adverse reaction of pralsetinib.

abnormal phosphate value (>=5.5 mg/dL) were included. Patient
phosphate level at Cycle 1 Day 15 (Day
15) was corrected from 13.2 mg/dL to 4.74 mg/dL after data cutoff date (13FEB2020).
[2] Days were calculated from the observed post-baseline phosphate level above the normal range to the follow-up
observed normal phosphate level cumulatively.
[3] Number of times that the post-baseline phosphate value starts above the normal range (<5.5 mg/dL).
Patients who had post-baseline phosphate value above the normal range before data cut but missing follow-up
assessment visit will be imputed with last contact date.
Percentages were calculated based on patients who had phosphate assessment value above the normal range (<5.5
mg/dL) in each corresponding column.

Vision Disorders
An SMQ analyses of vision disorders was provided by the Applicant at the request of the FDA.
The results revealed two ≥ Grade 3 events, one event of keratitis and one of cataract. These
analyses do not suggest a safety signal for pralsetinib for vision disorders.
Jak1/2 Pathway
Increased risk of infections/Myelosuppression
FDA requested the Applicant to perform additional analyses to assess the potential association
of underlying neutropenia to sepsis events. The Applicant’s analysis revealed 13 serious adverse
events of sepsis; 7 events were Grade 3, 5 events were Grade 4 and 1 event was Grade 5. Nine
events resolved, 2 events resolved with sequelae and 1 event was ongoing; 1 event had a fatal
outcome. In all events, baseline absolute neutrophil counts were within normal range. Six
events were occurred in association with decreased neutrophil counts.
The primary infection was UTI in 5 events and pneumonia in 2 events. Confounders and risk
factors were present in 11 of the 13 patients including history of neutropenia, ongoing urinary
tract infection and disease progression. As infections are commonly reported in cancer patients
and in ARROW these events were not consistently associated with neutropenia, FDA
determined this did not warrant inclusion in the USPI. Sepsis, pneumonia and UTI are included
in the list of the most frequent SAE in Section 6 of the USPI.

Dose Interru ption/Red uctio n Due to Adverse Effects

203
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as '7he Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, adverse events leading to dose modifications were
reported by 232 patients (65.5%) and 117 (65.4%) of NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD. In
both populations most of the dose modifications (approximately 60%) were dose interruptions
while only 1 of 3 were dose reductions (33% to 34%).
The most common adverse events leading to dose interruption (reported in > 3% of patients)
were similar in all patients treated at 400 mg QD and in NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD.
In all patients treated at 400 mg QD these were neutropenia (9.3%), hypertension (7.3%),
pneumonitis (7.1%), anaemia and pneumonia (5.9 % each), AST increased (4.5%), blood
creatine phosphokinase increased (3.7 %), and pyrexia and diarrhoea (3.4 % each). The only
differences in the events reported by NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD compared with the
all patients treated at 400 mg QD was that vomiting was also reported in > 3% of patients
(3.4%) while blood creatine phosphokinase increased was reported in < 3% of patients.
The most common adverse events leading to dose reduction (reported in ≥ 2% of patients)
were also similar in all patients treated at 400 mg QD and in NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg
QD. In all patients treated at 400 mg QD these were neutropenia (7.3 %), anaemia (5.6%),
pneumonitis (4.0%), and hypertension (3.1%), and neutrophil count decreased (2.0%). The
same pattern of adverse events resulted in dose reductions in NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg
QD, but included pneumonia and AST increased (2.2% each).
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), among all patients
treated at 400 mg QD, no notable differences in incidence of adverse events leading to
permanent treatment discontinuations were seen compared to what was reported in the initial
NDA. Adverse events leading to treatment interruption were reported in 267 patients (61.0%)
and dose reductions in 121 patients (34.2%). The most common AEs leading to dose
interruption and dose reduction were also consistent with what were reported in the initial
NDA.
The Applicant’s Position:
The most common AEs leading to dose interruption (reported in > 3% of patients) in patients
treated at 400 mg QD were neutropenia (9.3%), hypertension (7.3%), pneumonitis (7.1%),
anaemia and pneumonia (5.9 % each), AST increased (4.5%), blood creatine phosphokinase
increased (3.7 %), and pyrexia and diarrhoea (3.4 % each) and these were similar in NSCLC
patients treated at 400 mg QD.
The most common AEs leading to dose reduction (reported in > 2% of patients) in patients
treated at 400 mg QD were neutropenia (7.3 %), anaemia (5.6%), pneumonitis (4.0%), and
hypertension (3.1%), and neutrophil count decreased (2.0%) and these were similar in NSCLC
patients treated at 400 mg QD.

Significant Adverse Events
Data:
In addition to the AESls of pneumonia, pneumonitis, and tumor lysis syndrome, which are
discussed in detail in Section 8 .2.5, the grouped adverse events of neutropenia and
thrombocytopenia, and hypertension (not grouped) were selected for further analysis.

208
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as '7he Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Neutropenia and thrombocytopenia are adverse events commonly observed with TKIs such as
cabozantinib and vandetanib (Lyseng-Williamson, 2018; Morabito et al, 2010). In addition,
nonclinical studies have demonstrated that pralsetinib inhibits JAK2, which has been shown to
reduce red blood cell, neutrophil, and/or platelet counts (Huang and Luo, 2019; Pardanani and
Tefferi, 2014; van Vollenhoven et al, 2012; Verstovsek et al, 2010).
Based on nonclinical evidence, pralsetinib doses ≥ 25 mg/kg resulted in increased blood
pressure in rats, which was attributed to VEGFR inhibition. Based on clinical evidence,
hypertension occurred with increased frequency in the clinical development program. Adverse
events such as hypertension and plausible mechanism of action due to VEGFR inhibition were
also seen in other TKIs.
Neutropenia
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354), 115 patients (33%) reported events of
neutropenia or neutrophil count decreased (referred to as grouped neutropenia events). These
included 37 patients (11%) reported neutropenia events of ≥ Grade 3 in severity and 15 patients
(4%) reported neutrophil count decreased events ≥ Grade 3 in severity. One hundred five of the
354 patients (30%) treated at 400 mg QD reported grouped neutropenia events that were
assessed as related to study drug.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), among patients
treated at 400 mg QD (N=438), 158 patients (36.1%) reported grouped neutropenia events,
including 64 (14.6%) patients with Grade 3 events, and 10 (2.3%) experienced Grade 4 events;
no patient experienced Grade 5 events. A total of 146 patients (33.3%) reported grouped
neutropenia events that were assessed as related to pralsetinib.
Overall, neutropenia events included in the Day 90 Safety Update are consistent with those in
the initial NDA.
Thrombocytopenia
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, 50 patients (14.1%) reported events of
thrombocytopenia or platelet count decreased (referred to as grouped thrombocytopenia
events). A total of 5 patients (1.4%) had Grade 3, and 3 patients (< 1%) had Grade 4
thrombocytopenia AEs. None of the thrombocytopenia AEs was Grade 5. A total of 2 patients
(< 1%) had Grade 3, and 1 had (< 1%) Grade 4 platelet count decreased AEs. None of the
platelet count decreased AEs were Grade 5. Forty-five of the 354 patients (13%) treated at 400
mg QD reported grouped thrombocytopenia events that were assessed as related to study
drug.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), among all patients
treated at 400 mg QD (N=438), 66 (15.1%) reported grouped thrombocytopenia events,
209
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
including 9 (2%) patients with Grade 3 events and 5 (1.1%) with Grade 4 events. No patient
experienced Grade 5 events. A total of 58 patients (13.2%) reported grouped
thrombocytopenia events that were assessed as related to pralsetinib.
Overall, thrombocytopenia events included in the Day 90 Safety Update are consistent with
those in the initial NDA.
Hypertension
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354), 99 patients (28%) reported events of
hypertension. A total of 14 patients (4%) patients reported Grade 1 events, 36 patients (10%)
reported Grade 2 events, 49 patients (14%) reported Grade 3 events. No patients experienced
Grade 4 or Grade 5 events. Seventy-two of the 354 patients (20%) treated at 400 mg QD
reported hypertension events that were assessed as related to study drug.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), among all patients
treated at 400 mg QD (N=438), 128 (29.2%) reported events of hypertension, including 20
patients (4.6%) with Grade 1 events, 45 (10.3%) with Grade 2 events, and 63 (14.4%) with
Grade 3 events. No patient experienced Grade 4 or Grade 5 events. A total of 96 (21.9%)
patients reported hypertension events that were assessed as related to pralsetinib.
Overall, hypertension events included in the Day 90 Safety Update are consistent with those in
the initial NDA.
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on the biological plausibility and events reported as summarized above, a causal
association to pralsetinib appears possible. These events were manageable by dose
modification and routine medical care.
The available safety database was adequate to identify and characterize these events.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA does not have any comments regarding thrombocytopenia but has the following
comments for neutropenia and hypertension.
Neutropenia: FDA requested additional information on adverse event of increased risk of
infection and requested Applicant to provide analyses of any cases of sepsis associated with
neutropenia and to provide a summary of febrile neutropenia cases (see 8.2.4 for details).
Applicant did not provide additional analysis of febrile neutropenia, however There were 4
patients who experienced febrile neutropenia (Grade 3); no patient had pralsetinib
permanently discontinued for febrile neutropenia.
210
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Hypertension: FDA did not request additional analyses of hypertension. Of note, no Grade 4 or
5 events of hypertension occurred with pralsetinib; however, hypertension occurred in 29% of
patients, and 14% experienced Grade 3 events. Pralsetinib was interrupted for hypertension in
7% of patients and 3.2% had dose reduction of pralsetinib for hypertension. One patient
discontinued treatment due to hypertension. Hypertension will be included in Section 5 of the
USPI as the awareness of this adverse reaction and the need to implement appropriate
management strategies are important in this population in order to mitigate any adverse
outcomes.
Treatment Emergent Adverse Events
Data:
Overview of Adverse Events
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354), 339 patients (95.8%) experienced AEs,
320 (90.4%) experienced AEs related to pralsetinib as assessed by the Investigators, 155 (43.8%)
experienced ≥ Grade 3 related AEs, 156 (44.1%) experienced SAEs, and 58 (16.4%) experienced
related SAEs (Table 57). Treatment with pralsetinib was discontinued due to AEs as the primary
or contributing reason in 41 patients (11.6%) treated at 400 mg QD ( 17 patients (4.8%) due to a
related AE).
In general, the safety profile of patients treated at 400 mg QD was similar to that of NSCLC
patients treated at 400 mg QD. Of the 179 RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients treated at
400 mg QD in the safety population (in either Phase 1 or Phase 2 of the study), 169 (94.4%)
experienced any AEs, 161 (89.9%) experienced AEs related to pralsetinib, 78 (43.6%)
experienced ≥ Grade 3 related AEs, 91 (50.8%) experienced SAEs, and 36 (20.1%) experienced
related SAEs (Table 57). Treatment with pralsetinib was discontinued due to AEs in 35 patients
(2 due to a related AE).

Common Adverse Events
The most common AEs (reported in > 20% of patients) in patients treated at 400 mg QD were
AST increased (38.7%), followed by constipation (34.2%), anaemia (31.9%), diarrhoea (27.7%),
ALT increased (27.1%), hypertension (28.0% ), and fatigue (21.5%). There were no clinically
meaningful differences between patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354) and all patients
(N=404) in the safety population.
In NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD, the most common AEs were the same as those in all
patients treated at 400 mg QD. There were no clinically meaningful differences between NSCLC
patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=179) and all NSCLC patients treated at all doses/schedules
(N=197).
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), the profile of the
most common AEs remains consistent with what was reported in the initial NDA.
Grade ≥ 3 Adverse Events
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, an AE of Grade ≥ 3 was reported by 232 patients
(65.5%) and by 114 (63.7%) NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD. The most common AEs of
Grade ≥ 3 (reported in ≥ 10% patients) in all patients treated at 400 mg QD were hypertension
(13.8%), anaemia (12.1%), and neutropenia (10.5%) and were the same in NSCLC patients
treated at 400 mg QD. There were no clinically meaningful differences between all patients
treated at 400 mg QD and NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), the profile of the
severity of AEs remains consistent with what was reported in the initial NDA.

Pralsetinib
Related Adverse Events
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, 320 patients (90.4%) experienced at least 1 related
AE. In the NSCLC safety population treated at 400 mg, 161 patients (89.9%) experienced at least
1 related AE.
The most common related AEs (reported in > 20% of patients) in all patients treated at 400 mg
QD were AST increased (31.4%), ALT increased (21.5%), anaemia (21.8%), and hypertension
(20.3%). There were no clinically meaningful differences between all patients treated at
400 mg QD and NSCLC patients at 400 mg QD.
A total of 155 patients (43.8%) experienced related AEs of Grade ≥ 3. These were mainly
hypertension (10.5%), neutropenia (9.6%), anaemia (7.9%), neutrophil count decreased (4.0%),
WBC count decreased, lymphopenia and blood creatine phosphokinase increased (3.1% each)
and AST increased (2.0%).
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), the profile of the
related AEs remains consistent with what was reported in the initial NDA.
The Applicant’s Position:
The observed TEAEs and related TEAEs in all patients treated at 400 mg QD and NSCLC patients
treated at 400 mg QD were relevant to the mechanism of action of the study drug and have
also been observed with other TKIs.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Below is an updated table presenting treatment-emergent adverse events occurring in ≥15% of
patients in the population of patients with NSCLC based on the Applicant’s 90-day update data.
These adverse events were similar to results from the initial analysis. Additionally, AE profile
was similar in patients with NSCLC versus all patients treated at 400 mg QD.

Coagulation
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, adverse events associated with coagulation
abnormalities were rare and included activated partial thromboplastin time prolonged (1
patient with a Grade 1, unrelated event) and international normalized ratio increased (1 patient
with 1 related Grade 1 AE and 1 patient with 1 unrelated Grade 2 AE).
The Applicant’s Position:
Generally, the laboratory abnormalities observed during the study in all patients treated at 400
mg QD were transient, resolved/resolved with sequelae with study drug interruption and/or
supportive care, and not associated with significant clinical outcomes.
The FDA’s Assessment:
