laboratory abnormalities for the all patients treated at 400 mg QD safety analysis population
and the NSCLC population based on the 90 day update, and results were similar between the
two populations. Results for patients with NSCLC treated at 400 mg QD are presented in the
table below. Decreased albumin (all Grade 34%, no Grade 3-4) and decreased leukocytes (all
grade 62%, grade 3-4 4.3%) are not included in the table below since Grade 1-2 decreased
albumin is of limited clinical relevance and decreased leukocytes is better defined by specific
values for decreased neutrophils and decreased lymphocytes, which are included in the table.
There were no Grade 3-4 laboratory abnormalities with an incidence of ≥2% other than those
listed in the table below.

Vital Signs
Data:
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, median blood pressure levels had increased above
baseline at Week 1 of treatment for both systolic blood pressure (from 120.0 mmHg [range: 87
to 174] to 130.0 mmHg [range: 91 to 197]) and diastolic blood pressure (from 74 mmHg to 81.5
mmHg), and then systolic blood pressure remained close to 130.0 mmHg through Week 48
(while the N = 74); at the EOT the median systolic level was 120.0 mmHg and diastolic was 79.0
mmHg. This increase in blood pressure is reflected in the reports of hypertension as an AE (in
28.0% of all patients).
Among all patients treated at 400 mg QD, although median body temperature remained
constant from baseline throughout the study, pyrexia was reported as an AE in 20.1% of all
patients treated with 400 mg QD and was one of the most common AEs in the System Organ
Class of General Disorders and Administration Site Conditions.

Pralsetinib
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), vital signs in all
patients treated at 400 mg QD remains consistent with what was reported in the initial NDA.
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on the frequency of hypertension events and the biological plausibility, a causal
association to pralsetinib appears possible. The events were routinely managed by standard of
care anti-hypertensive treatment resulting in event resolution.
Other than an increase in blood pressure, none of the assessments of vital signs indicated a
clinically meaningful change in either all patients treated at 400 mg QD and the population of
NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees. For assessment of hypertension see Significant Adverse Events.
Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
Data:
None of the abnormal ECG results in all patients treated at 400 mg QD were clinically significant
at baseline except for 2 patients (< 1%). Change from baseline for all doses/schedules showed a
change for 5.4% to 13.8% of patients at any visit. At the EOT, change from baseline was
observed for 1.1% of patients. Electrocardiogram QT prolongation as an AE was reported in
16 patients (4.5%) (10 related and 2 unrelated Grade 1 events, 2 related and 1 unrelated Grade
2 events, and 1 related Grade 3 event).
None of the abnormal ECG results in NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD were clinically
significant at baseline except for 2 patients (1.1%). Over the course of the study, 4.5% to 14.0%
of patients experienced a change at any timepoint. At the EOT, change from baseline was
observed for < 1% of patients.
There were no clinically meaningful differences in change from baseline in ECG results between
patients treated at 400 mg QD (N=354) and NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period, the abnormal ECG results among all patients treated
at 400 mg QD were clinically significant for an additional 6 patients compared to what was
reported in the initial NDA. Changes from baseline and deterioration from baseline at the end
of treatment for all patients at 400 mg QD was similar to what was reported in the initial NDA.
ECG QT prolongation as an AE was reported in 23 patients (5.3%) which was similar to what was

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
QT
Data:
An analysis of continuous ECG (Holter) data from the Phase 2 part of the Study BLU-667-1101
was performed. A total of 34 patients were included in the QT/QTc population. The primary
outcome measure for cardiac safety was an analysis of the regression relationship between
ΔQTcF and the plasma concentration of pralsetinib at matching times post-dose.
Key observations from the QT cardiac assessment in Study BLU-667-1101 were as follows:
• The primary outcome measure indicated that pralsetinib had no clinically relevant or
statistically significant effect on QT prolongation with a linear slope
of -0.0003 ms/ng/mL and a p-value of 0.841;
• The secondary analysis results showed Least Squares Mean values of ΔQTcF ≤ 7.8 ms;
• No patients had a QTcF value > 500 ms;
• Analysis of HR, PR interval, and QRS duration were likewise negative, i.e., there was no
effect of pralsetinib treatment on these measures;
• Pralsetinib administration at 400 mg QD was observed to have no clinically relevant and
statistically significant effects on the ECG or specifically was not associated with
evidence for prolongation of the QTc interval.
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on the results of ECG analyses, pralsetinib was not found to cause QT prolongation and
there was no evidence of cardiac repolarization prolongation.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with Applicant’s position.
Immunogenicity
Not applicable.
Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
The events of pneumonia, pneumonitis, and tumor lysis syndrome were identified as AESIs
during the development of pralsetinib, based on the clinical experience taking into
consideration multiple factors such as incidence, severity, relationship to study drug, medical
impact, and clinical outcome of the AEs.
8.2.5.1 Pneumonia
Data:
219
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
Overall, 47 (13%) of the 354 patients in the safety population who started treatment at 400 mg
QD reported events of pneumonia (referred to as grouped pneumonia events), including 40
patients (11%) who reported pneumonia, 3 patients (<1%) who reported pneumocystis jirovecii
pneumonia, and single reports (<1%) of lung infection, atypical pneumonia, pneumonia
bacterial, pneumonia cytomegaloviral, and pneumonia staphylococcal. Of these 47 patients, 34
(72%) reported events that were ≥ Grade 3 in severity. Fourteen (4%) of the 354 patients
reported grouped pneumonia events that were assessed as related to study drug. Thirty-four
patients (10%) reported serious grouped pneumonia events, of which 10 patients (3%) reported
serious grouped pneumonia events that were assessed as related to study drug.
The 10 serious related pneumonia events included 8 patients who had NSCLC and 2 patients
who had thyroid cancer. The events included 7 patients with pneumonia and 3 patients with
pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia. All 10 patients had confounders/risk factors including NSCLC
which is known to be associated with an increased risk of pulmonary infections, pre-existing
history of diabetes, asthma, hilar adenopathy, recent external beam radiation around/near the
chest area, former smoking, concurrent SAEs of sepsis, and ongoing lymphopenia or
neutropenia at or around the time of the pneumonia event. In addition, 3 pneumonia events
were caused by opportunistic infections (pneumocystis jirovecii), and all 3 of these patients had
lymphopenia at the time of the event.
Of the 179 patients with NSCLC who initiated treatment at 400 mg QD, 29 patients (16%)
reported grouped events of pneumonia, including 11 patients (6%) reported grouped
pneumonia events that were assessed as related to study drug. Twenty patients (11%) with
NSCLC who initiated treatment at 400 mg QD reported serious grouped pneumonia events, of
which 8 patients (4.5%) reported serious related pneumonia events. One patient with NSCLC
who initiated study drug treatment at 400 mg QD reported Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia.
In general, there were no clinically meaningful differences between all patients treated at 400
mg QD and NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), 60 (14%) of the 438
patients in the safety population who started treatment at 400 mg QD reported events of
pneumonia, including 48 patients (11%) who reported pneumonia, 3 patients (<1%) who
reported pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia, 2 patients each (<1%) who reported lung infection
and pneumonia bacterial, and single patients who reported pneumonia influenza, atypical
pneumonia, pneumonia cytomegaloviral, pneumonia haemophilus, pneumonia moraxella,
pneumonia staphylococcal and pneumonia streptococcal. Sixteen (4%) of the 438 patients
reported grouped pneumonia events that were assessed as related to study drug. Forty-one
patients (9%) reported serious grouped pneumonia events, of which 12 patients (3%) reported
serious grouped pneumonia events that were assessed as related to study drug.

The Applicant’s Position:
The incidence of pneumonia and grouped pneumonia reported in patients treated at 400 mg
QD in Study BLU-667-1101 was consistent with what one would expect with a TKI treatment for
this population of patients with advanced NSCLC (Burotto et al, 2015; Inoue et al, 2003).
The FDA’s Assessment:
Patients in the NSCLC population and patients who have metastases to the lungs do have a
higher risk for developing pneumonia; however, it cannot be ruled out that pralsetinib
contributed to pneumonia. In the safety population of 438 patients treated with pralsetinib 400
mg QD, the incidence of pneumonia was 14%. Among patients with adverse reaction of
pneumonia, 1.4% of patients permanently discontinued pralsetinib, 6% had treatment
interruptions and 1.6% had dose reductions (see Table 54 Adverse events leading to treatment
discontinuation 90 day update, Table 55 Adverse events leading to treatment interruption 90­
day update, Table 56 Adverse events leading to dose reduction 90-day update). Five deaths due
to pneumonia were reported (refer to Table 46 Original NDA and 90-Day Update: Death within
30 Days of Last Pralsetinib Dose).
8.2.5.2 Pneumonitis
Data:
Overall, 35 (10%) of the 354 patients in the safety population who started treatment at 400 mg
QD reported events of pneumonitis. Of these 35 patients, 9 (26%) reported events that were ≥
Grade 3 in severity. Thirty (9%) of the 354 patients reported pneumonitis events that were
assessed as related to study drug. Fourteen patients (4%) reported serious pneumonitis events,
of which 11 patients (3%) reported serious pneumonitis events that were assessed as related to
study drug.
The 14 serious pneumonitis events included 8 patients who had NSCLC and 6 patients who had
thyroid cancer. Eleven of the 14 patients had confounders/risk factors that included prior
history of pneumonitis that predated initiation of BLU-667, external beam radiation to the
chest/back/spine area, or prior treatment with chemotherapeutic regimens before initiating
BLU-667 that were known to be associated with causing pneumonitis (e.g. Pemetrexed,
Pembrolizumab, Vandetanib, Docetaxel, Osimertinib).
Seventeen of the 179 patients with NSCLC who initiated treatment at 400 mg QD (9%) reported
pneumonitis events that were assessed as related to study drug. Seven patients (4%) with
NSCLC who initiated treatment at 400 mg QD reported serious pneumonitis events. In general,
there were no clinically meaningful differences between patients treated at 400 mg QD and

Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), 45 (10%) of the 438

was reported in the initial NDA.
The Applicant’s Position:
The incidence of pneumonitis reported in patients treated at 400 mg QD in Study BLU-667-1101
was generally consistent with a TKI treatment for this population of patients with advanced
NSCLC (Burotto et al, 2015; Inoue et al, 2003). In addition, among the patients with related SAEs
of pneumonitis (3%) in this population, the majority had received prior therapies (before
initiation of pralsetinib) that are known to be associated with causing pneumonitis (e.g.,
pemetrexed, pembrolizumab, vandetanib, docetaxel, osimertinib).
The FDA’s Assessment:
Among the 438 patients in the safety population of patients treated with pralsetinib 400 mg
QD, 43 patients (10%) experienced Grade 1-5 events of pneumonitis, 10 Grade 3 events and 2
Grade 4 events (grade 3-4, 2.7%). There were two deaths (0.5%) attributed to pneumonitis
based on FDA’s review of death narratives (see section 8.2.4). Pneumonitis/ILD is a known
serious adverse event of tyrosine kinase inhibitors and requires appropriate management. FDA
requested the Applicant to complete additional analyses of patients who experienced Grade 1
and 2 pneumonitis events to explore if continuing pralsetinib after dose reduction would be
appropriate. The Applicant’s analysis is presented below:

Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
Data:
Adverse events were analyzed by age (< 65 years, ≥ 65 years), sex, geographic region (US,
Europe, Asia), and race group (Asian, Non-Asian, Unknown).
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, there were no clinically meaningful differences in the incidence of AEs, SAEs, AEs of
Grade ≥ 3, related AEs, related SAEs, and related Grade ≥ 3 AEs between the subgroups of age,
sex, region, or race either in the overall safety population or in NSCLC patients treated at
400 mg QD.
No notable differences were seen through the Day 90 Safety Update period compared to what
was reported in the initial NDA.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Please see tables below for safety analyses by demographic subgroup. FDA does not agree with
the Applicant’s position that there were no clinically meaningful differences in safety between
the subgroups based on age. The incidence of grade ≥3 TEAEs and serious TEAEs were
approximately 20% higher in the subgroup of patients age ≥65 years old than in the subgroup
<65 years old, and the incidence of discontinuation of pralsetinib for TEAE was approximately
10% higher.

Pralsetinib
Additional Safety Explorations
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
Data:
No clinical carcinogenicity studies have been conducted.
In patients treated at 400 mg QD, 15 of 354 patients (4.2%) experienced AEs within the
MedDRA System Organ Class of Neoplasms Benign, Malignant and Unspecified. Tumor pain was
reported in 4 patients, basal cell carcinoma, cancer pain, lipoma, MTC and anaplastic large-cell
lymphoma in 2 patients each. Hemangioma of skin, malignant neoplasm progression and
melanocytic naevus were reported in 1 patient each.
Through Day 90 Safety Update period (13Feb2020), the number of patients with events was
updated to 14 patients (3.2%).
The Applicant’s Position:
No clinical carcinogenicity studies have been conducted with pralsetinib. In Study BLU-667­
1101, TEAEs within the MedDRA System Organ Class of Neoplasms Benign, Malignant and
Unspecified were reported in patients treated with pralsetinib. Currently, limited long-term
data preclude definitive evaluation of carcinogenicity.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s statement that carcinogenicity studies have not been
conducted with pralsetinib. Consistent with the principles of the ICH S9 guidance,
carcinogenicity studies are not necessary to support the use of pralsetinib in patients with
advanced NSCLC.
If assessing tumor development, the only AEs from the Applicant’s list above which would be
considered relevant to such an assessment are basal cell carcinoma and anaplastic large cell
lymphoma. The currently available data do not raise concern for a safety signal; however, as
noted by the Applicant, there are no long-term data available to allow definitive evaluation of
the risk of tumor development.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data:
There were no positive pregnancy test results and no pregnancies were reported during
Study BLU-667-1101.
The Applicant’s Position:
No clinical reproductive and developmental toxicity studies have been conducted and the
229
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
teratogenic potential of pralsetinib has not been established.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that no clinical reproductive and developmental toxicitiy studies have been
conducted with pralsetinib; however, based on findings from animal studies and its mechanism
of action, pralsetinib can cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman. Oral
administration of pralsetinib to pregnant rats during the period of organogenesis resulted in
malformations and embryolethality at maternal exposures below the human exposure at the
clinical dose of 400 mg once daily.
This information will be included under the heading of Embryo-Fetal Toxicity in Section 5
(Warnings and Precautions) and in Section 8.1 (Pregnancy) of the USPI for pralsetinib, along
with instructions to advise pregnant women of the potential risk to a fetus. The USPI will also
instruct providers to advise females and males of reproductive potential to use effective nonhormonal contraception during treatment with pralsetinib and for 2 weeks after the final dose.

Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Data:
No cases of overdose have been reported. The highest dose of pralsetinib studied clinically is
600 mg QD.
No cases of drug abuse have been reported with pralsetinib.
No formal studies of withdrawal or rebound effects associated with pralsetinib treatment have
been conducted. No cases of withdrawal or rebound have been reported with pralsetinib.
The Applicant’s Position:
There is no known antidote for pralsetinib overdose. In the event of suspected overdose,
pralsetinib dosing should be interrupted, supportive care instituted, and the patient observed

Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Data:
The 404 patients included in the overall safety population of Study BLU-667-1101 and 354
patients treated at 400 mg QD are considered adequate for the detection and characterization
of common AEs and to provide guidance on AE management.
The Applicant’s Position:
Safety of pralsetinib in the postmarket setting is not expected to differ significantly from that
observed in Study BLU-667-1101 as the study population is reflective of the patients expected
to be treated with pralsetinib in the postmarket setting.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Pralsetinib is expected to be administered by oncologists; management of and monitoring for
adverse effects of anti-cancer medications, including potentially serious adverse effects, is
standard practice in oncology. FDA does not anticipate that the safety of pralsetinib will differ
significantly in the post-market setting significantly.

Pralsetinib
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data:
The safety analyses in all patients treated at 400 mg QD in Study BLU-667-1101 demonstrated
that pralsetinib treatment is generally well-tolerated.
In the safety population, median (range) treatment duration was 5.52 (0.1, 22.3) months for all
patients treated at all doses/schedules. The key safety results for all patients treated at 400 mg
QD were:
• 339 patients (94.4%) experienced any AEs. The most common AE (in > 25% of patients)
was AST increased (38.7%), followed by constipation (34.2%), anaemia (31.9%),
diarrhoea (27.7%), ALT increased (27.1%), and hypertension (28.0%). Overall,
232 patients (65.5%) experienced any ≥ Grade 3 AEs;
• 320 patients (90.4%) experienced AEs related to pralsetinib. The most common related
AEs (in > 20.0% of patients) were AST increased (31.4%), anaemia (21.8%), ALT increased
(21.5%), constipation (21.2%), and hypertension (20.3%). A total of 155 patients (43.8%)
experienced related ≥ Grade 3 AEs;
• 156 patients (44.1%) experienced any SAEs. The most common SAEs (in > 2 patients)
were pneumonia (8.2%), disease progression (5.1%), pneumonitis (4.0%), urinary tract
infection (2.8%), and sepsis and pyrexia (2.5% each). A total of 58 patients (16.4%)
experienced related SAEs. The most common related SAE was pneumonitis (3.1%).
Overall, 40 patients (9.1%) died due to AE during the study (< 1% due to related AE);
• 3 patients (<1.0%) had ≥ 1 dose escalation and 215 (60.7%) had ≥ 1 dose reduction,
interruption, or missing dose due to an AE during the study. A total of 58 patients
(16.4%) experienced AEs as primary or contributing reason leading to permanent
treatment discontinuation (including “disease progression” reported as an AE term and
AEs that represented symptoms of disease progression). The most common AEs (in > 1%
of patients) leading to treatment discontinuation were disease progression (3.4%),
pneumonia (1.4%), and pneumonitis (1.1%);
• AESIs:
─ 40 patients (11.3%) experienced ≥ 1 AESI of pneumonia (related in 2.8% of
patients, SAEs in 8.2%, and leading to permanent treatment discontinuation in
1.4%);
─ 35 patients (9.9%) experienced ≥ 1 AESI of pneumonitis, related in 11.0% of
patients, SAEs in 4.0%, and leading to permanent treatment discontinuation in
1.1%);
─ Tumor lysis syndrome was not reported in any patient who was treated at 400
mg QD;
• Generally, the laboratory abnormalities observed during the study were transient
resolved/resolved with sequelae following study drug interruption and/or supportive
care, and not associated with significant clinical outcomes.

Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
•

Pralsetinib administration did not have any clinically meaningful nor statistically
significant effects on ECGs and was not associated with evidence of cardiac
repolarization prolongation.

The safety analyses in all patients treated at 400 mg QD and NSCLC patients treated at
400 mg QD in Study BLU-667-1101 demonstrated that pralsetinib treatment at a dose of
400 mg in a QD schedule is appropriate for this patient population. This safety profile is
consistent with the overall safety profile of all patients in the study.
For the 179 NSCLC patients treated at 400 mg QD in the safety population, median (range)
treatment duration was 5.16 (0.2 to 19.8) months. The key safety results for these patients
were:
• 169 patients (94.4%) experienced any AEs. The most common AE (reported in > 25% of
patients) was AST increased (41.3%), followed by constipation (32.4%), anaemia and ALT
increased (28.5% each), and hypertension (25.7%). Overall, 114 patients (63.7%)
experienced any ≥ Grade 3 AEs;
• 161 (89.9%) experienced AEs related to pralsetinib. The most common related AE was
AST increased (35.2%), followed by ALT increased (24.6%), and constipation and
anaemia (20.1% each). A total of 78 patients (43.6%) experienced related ≥ Grade 3 AEs.
The most common ≥ Grade 3 AE was hypertension (12.8%), followed by anaemia and
neutropenia (11.2% each);
• 91 patients (50.8%) experienced any SAEs. The most common SAEs (occurring in > 2% of
patients) were pneumonia (10.1%), disease progression (6.7%), pneumonitis and sepsis
(3.9% each), and anaemia, hypertension, pyrexia, and urinary tract infection (2.2%
each). A total of 36 patients (20.1%) experienced SAEs related to pralsetinib. The most
common related SAEs (occurring in > 1 patient) were pneumonia (3.9%), pneumonitis
(3.4%), sepsis, anaemia, and hypertension (1.7% each), and neutropenia (1.1%). Overall,
19 patients (10.6%) died due to AE during the study (none were due to related AE);
• 1 patient (< 1%) had any dose escalation and 104 (58.1%) had any dose reduction,
interruption, or missing dose due to AE during the study. A total of 117 patients (65.4%)
experienced AEs leading to dose modification and 35 (19.6%) experienced AEs as
primary or contributing reason leading to permanent treatment discontinuation
(including “disease progression” reported as an AE term and AEs that represented
symptoms of disease progression). The most common AEs leading to treatment
discontinuation (reported in > 1% of patients) were disease progression (4.5%),
pneumonia (2.2%), pneumonitis (1.7%), and dyspnoea and sepsis (1.1% each);
• AESIs: there were no clinically meaningful differences in AESIs between these patients
and all patients treated at 400 mg QD;
• Laboratory abnormalities were similar to those observed in all patients treated at 400
mg QD: transient, and resolved/resolved with sequelae following study drug
interruption and/or supportive care and not associated with significant clinical

Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
outcomes.
Through the Day 90 Safety Update period (cutoff date 13 February 2020), the safety profile of
pralsetinib remained consistent with that reported in the initial NDA submission (data cutoff
date of 18 November 2019).
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment with 400 mg QD oral pralsetinib in 354 patients with RET-fusion positive NSCLC, MTC
and other solid tumors was associated with a generally well-tolerated, predictable, and
manageable safety profile, allowing for prolonged treatment in patients with unresectable,
metastatic NSCLC, regardless of prior line of treatment. The most common AEs (in > 25% of
patients) in all patients treated at 400 mg QD were AST increased (38.7%), followed by
constipation (34.2%), anaemia (31.9%), diarrhoea (27.7%), ALT increased (27.1%), and
hypertension (28.0%). The proportion of patients who experienced TEAEs of ≥ Grade 3 (65.5%)
regardless of causality was typical for studies in patients with advanced NSCLC and most were
manageable, reversible, and seldom led to treatment discontinuation. The most common
treatment-emergent SAEs (> 2% of patients) were pneumonia (8.2%), disease progression
(5.1%), pneumonitis (4.0%), urinary tract infection (2.8%), and sepsis and pyrexia (2.5% each).
The most common related AEs (reported in > 20% of patients) as assessed by the Investigators
were AST increased (31.4%), ALT increased (21.5%), anaemia (21.8%), and hypertension
(20.3%), and the most common related SAEs (occurring in > 2 patients) were pneumonitis
(3.1%), pneumonia (2.0%), and anaemia, hypertension, neutropenia, pneumocystis jirovecii
pneumonia, sepsis, and thrombocytopenia (< 1% each).
In general, the risks of treatment with pralsetinib were less than what these RET-fusion positive
patients would have been exposed to by treatment with the non-selective, intravenous,
standard of care agents currently available. In particular, pneumonitis has been reported to
occur in up to 19% of patients treated with immune checkpoint inhibitors and is higher when
used in combination with other therapies (Suresh et al, 2018). In comparison, 10.6% of NSCLC
patients treated with 400 mg QD reported pneumonitis. Additionally, dermatological events
commonly seen with other treatments (30% to 40% with rash and pruritus in patients treated
with nivolumab or pembrolizumab; Naidoo et al, 2015) were less common with pralsetinib
400 mg QD in these RET-fusion positive NSCLC patients (6.7% with rash and 2.8% with pruritus).
Due to the embryofetal toxicity associated with ‘on-target’ RET inhibition, pralsetinib should
not be used in pregnant women.
While pralsetinib is a selective RET inhibitor, there are other kinases; it can inhibit at lower
levels that may result in some ‘off-target’ toxicities. Some of the toxic effects seen in nonclinical
studies with pralsetinib were the result of effects on off-target VEGFR, JAK2, and/or fibroblast
growth factor receptor proteins. The AE profile seen in humans, which included hypertension
234
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
(i.e., VEGF) and abnormalities in hematological parameters (i.e., JAK2), was consistent with
these nonclinical observations. In the clinical Study BLU-667-1101 at the dose of 400 mg QD,
some of the most common AEs were hypertension (28.0% of patients), anemia (31.9%), and
neutropenia (19.8 %), which were also the most common ≥ Grade 3 AEs (13.8% for
hypertension, 12.1% for anaemia, and 10.5% for neutropenia). These effects on blood pressure
and blood counts are known to be reversible and manageable. Reduction in neutrophils and
lymphocytes is associated with an increased risk for lung infections, which is why patients
should also be monitored for signs and symptoms of pneumonia or other lung infections.
Pralsetinib did not result in QT prolongation in the clinical setting, it did not have any clinically
meaningful or statistically significant effects on the ECG, and specifically it was not associated
with evidence of cardiac repolarization prolongation. Thus, there is no significant risk of QT
prolongation expected with the treatment of pralsetinib in patients.
The overall safety experience suggests that pralsetinib has a favorable tolerability profile in
advanced cancer patients in relation to current available therapies. The risks associated with
pralsetinib are readily detected and commonly seen with TKIs and/or in association with the
underlying disease and were mostly of low severity and without significant impact on the
course of treatment. Management of these events is part of standard clinical practice, including
supportive medications, dose interruption, and potential dose reduction, and seldom led to
treatment discontinuation. These risks are considered acceptable in relation to the severity and
clinical outcome of the indication being treated.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the description of the safety database provided by the Applicant and the
Applicant’s statement that through the Day 90 Safety Update period, the safety profile of
pralsetinib remained consistent with that reported in the initial NDA submission.
In addition to pneumonitis identified by the Applicant for inclusion in the Warnings and
Precautions section of the USPI, FDA identified the following additional adverse reactions for
inclusion based on severity, incidence, and/or plausible mechanism of action: hepatotoxicity,
hemorrhagic events, hypertension, and delayed wound healing.
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s statement that management of adverse reactions reported
with pralsetinib are part of standard clinical practice for oncologists, and the toxicity profile of
pralsetinib is acceptable when considering the seriousness of the disease pralsetinib will be
treating.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib

Statistical Issues

The FDA’s Assessment:
There were no major statistical issues in the review of this NDA. However, the single-arm design
of the main trial supporting efficacy may limit the interpretation of results, as a randomized
comparison to standard of care for this disease setting is not available to provide context for
the observed patient data. This is particularly problematic for time-to-event endpoints, such as
PFS and OS, given the absence of an active control or comparison to the natural history of
disease.

Conclusions and Recommendations

The FDA’s Assessment:

Based on the review of clinical data from Study BLU-667-1101, the clinical review team
recommends accelerated approval under the provisions of 21 CFR subpart H of this NDA for
pralsetinib for the treatment of patients with RET-fusion positive metastatic NSCLC.
The basis of this recommendation is the favorable benefit: risk profile for pralsetinib as a single
agent in patients with RET fusion-positive metastatic NSCLC. In the subgroup of patients who
have previously received platinum chemotherapy (N=87) the ORR per RECIST 1.1 as assessed by
a BIRC was 57% (95% CI 46, 68). Tumor responses were durable, with 80% having responses
lasting ≥6 months. In the population of patients with treatment-naïve RET fusion-positive
metastatic NSCLC (N=27), the ORR was 70% (95% CI 50, 86), with 58% having responses lasting
≥6 months.
The safety review of pralsetinib included data from 438 patients from ARROW. The primary
risks identified as warranting inclusion in the Warnings and Precautions section of the USPI for
pralsetinib are interstitial lung disease/pneumonitis, hypertension, hepatoxicity, hemorrhagic
events, and potential for risk of impaired wound healing. These risks are described in detail in
this review and are addressed in product labeling. Overall the toxicity profile of pralsetinib is
considered acceptable when assessed in the context of the treatment of this life-threatening
disease.
Data to verify the clinical benefit will consist of additional data from the single arm trial, Study
BLU-667-1101 (ARROW). Blueprint has also initiated a randomized, controlled trial of
pralsetinib compared to platinum-based chemotherapy with or without pembrolizumab as firstline treatment for patients with metastatic RET fusion-positive NSCLC (Study BLUE-667-2303;
AcceleRET Lung), and submission of data from this trial will be requested as a post-marketing
commitment to provide additional information regarding outcomes with pralsetinib in the
treatment-naïve patient population.

12

Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)

The FDA’s Assessment:
The clinical review team determined that a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) was
not required to ensure safe and effective use of pralsetinib for the indicated population given
the current safety profile of pralsetinib and the experience of the medical oncology community
in monitoring and managing adverse reactions related to targeted therapies, based on use of
other marketed products of the same class. Recommendations for the safe and effective use of
pralsetinib will be made in labeling, including a patient package insert (PPI). There are no
additional risk management strategies required beyond the recommended labeling.

Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment

The FDA’s Assessment: Blueprint has agreed to the following postmarketing requirements
(PMR) and postmarketing commitments (PMC). Please refer to the approval letter for expected
completion dates.
Clinical PMRs
PMR 3916-1:
Submit the final report, including datasets, from an ongoing clinical trial to verify and further
characterize the clinical benefit of pralsetinib for the treatment of patients with 1) treatment­
naïve RET fusion-positive NSCLC and with 2) RET fusion-positive NSCLC who have previously
received platinum chemotherapy to provide a more precise estimation of the BICR-assessed
overall response rate and duration of response after all responders in the population of
patients with treatment-naïve NSCLC (approximately 120 patients) have been followed for at
least 12 months from the date of initial response (or until disease progression, whichever
comes first) and after all responders in the population of patients with NSCLC previously
treated with platinum therapy (87 patients) have been followed for at least 6 months.
PMR 3916­ (4)
(b)

Conduct a comprehensive analysis evaluating and characterizing the incidence, clinical
presentation, management, and outcome of the potential serious risk of pralsetinib associated
gastrointestinal perforations and fistulas. Submit an integrated final report containing data
from patient-level and pooled analyses of on-going and completed clinical trials, post-marketing
reports and/or literature reports and a comprehensive pharmacovigilance assessment for this
potential serious risk. The results from this report may inform product labeling.
Clinical Pharmacology PMRs

inhibitor on the pharmacokinetics of pralsetinib and to inform appropriate dosing strategies for
safe coadministration of pralsetinib with P-gp inhibitors. Design and conduct the study in
accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled: “Clinical Drug Interaction Studies Cytochrome P450 Enzyme and Transporter- Mediated Drug Interactions”. Submit the datasets

The FDA's Assessment:
19.4.1 Popu lation Pharmacokinetic Analyses

The goal of popu lation PK analysis (popPK) was to develop a popu lation pharmacokinetic (PK)
model to assess sources of variabilit y (intri nsic and extrinsic covariates) of pra lsetinib in healthy
vo lunteers, patients wit h NSCLC and patients w ith thyroid cancer.

263
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)

Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as '7he Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The population PK model for pralsetinib included 491 subjects (193 healthy subjects, 161
patients with NSCLC, 124 patients with RET-mutation positive MTC and 13 patients with RETfusion positive thyroid) with 7566 quantifiable pralsetinib concentrations pooled from 5 clinical
studies. A summary of the demographics and selected baseline lab assessments included in the
popPK analysis was provided in Table 63 and Table 64.
The popPK analysis was conducted by the Applicant and evaluated by the pharmacometrics
reviewer. The PK of pralsetinib was characterized by a one-compartment model with linear
elimination. Absorption was described by a series of transit compartments (7, 4, and 7 transit
compartments for capsule Process I, II, and III, respectively). Between-subject variability (BSV)
on CL/F, V/F, and KTR was included. Between-occasion variability (BOV) on KTR and oral
bioavailability (F) was included. An additive error model on the log-scale was used to describe
the observed concentrations.
A stepwise covariate modeling approach was implemented to investigate the effects of
covariates on pralsetinib. The most significant covariates in terms of ΔOBJ change were
included in the forward inclusion model. From this model, all covariates that were previously
identified as significant were re-investigated. This step was repeated until there were no more
significant covariates to be included. From the full model, covariates were deleted one at a time
in a backward elimination process. Covariates which could be deleted from the full model
(without an associated increase in OBJ ≥10.8 (p < 0:001)) were sorted by OBJ and the covariate
with the smallest ΔOBJ was removed from the model. This process was repeated until no more
covariates can be removed from the model.
Parameter estimates of final model were provided in Table 65. The covariates included in the
final model were as follows: weight on CL/F and V/F, patients with RET-altered thyroid cancer
on KTR on Cycle 1 Day 1 (C1D1), patients with NSCLC on F, patients with NSCLC administered
capsule Process I on F, age on CL/F, concomitant CYP3A4 inducer use on CL/F, and capsule
Process on KTR.
The goodness of fit plots of model evaluation was presented in Figure 4. Plots of the observed
versus population predicted concentrations demonstrated bias of fit in the low concentration
range, but the majority of data are evenly scattered around the line of unity. No bias was found
in other diagnostic plots, suggesting the model fitted the data with good precision and minimal
bias in general. Prediction-corrected visual predictive check also showed that the final model
adequately described the observed PK profile of pralsetinib across different tumor types (Figure
5).
The effects of all evaluated covariates on the pralsetinib exposure (AUC0-24hr,ss and Cmax,ss) were
illustrated in the forest plot (Figure 6 and Figure 7). Overall, the magnitude of the effect of all
covariates on pralsetinib exposure was within <0.5-fold change compared to the reference
264
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
group, defined as patients with RET-altered thyroid cancer with median age (52 years) and
weight (73.5 kg), with the 95% prediction interval (PI) overlapping with the 80-125% range. The
post-hoc pralsetinib CL were similar between patients with normal liver function (N=241) and
patients with mild hepatic impairment (N=42 (Figure 8). The post-hoc pralsetinib CL values were
also similar between patients with normal renal function (N=121), patients with mild renal
impairment (N=138) and patients with moderate renal function (N=26) (Figure 9).
These results suggest no clinically significant differences in the pharmacokinetics of pralsetinib
were observed based on age (19 to 87 years), sex, race (White, Black, or Asian) and body weight
(32.1 to 128.0 kg). Mild to moderate (CLCR 30 to 89 mL/min estimated by Cockcroft-Gault)
renal impairment, or mild hepatic impairment (total bilirubin ≤ ULN and AST > ULN or total
bilirubin > 1 to 1.5 times ULN and any AST) were found to have no effect on the exposure of
pralsetinib.
The use of concomitant weak CYP3A4 inducers for 5 consecutive days was estimated to result in
an approximately 20% reduction in Cmaxss and AUCss. The capsule Process III was found to
achieve similar exposure compared to capsule Process II, while the capsule Process II was
estimated to result in 32% higher exposure compared to capsule Process I.
The majority of healthy subjects or patients received a starting dose of 400 mg (N=400), a few
healthy subjects in the analysis also received a dose of 200 mg (N=45), but very few patients
with thyroid cancer received starting dose of 200 mg (N=9) or 300 mg (N=7). Dose-normalized
concentrations are similar between 200 mg and 400 mg in healthy subjects, suggesting dose
proportionality is observed in healthy volunteers. A small over proportional increase in dosenormalized concentration time profiles for the dose range between 200 mg – 400 mg is
observed in patients with RET-fusion positive thyroid cancer. This finding is challenging to
interpret given the small number of subjects on doses <400 mg and is inconsistent with what
was seen in the healthy volunteers.

19.4.2 Exposure-Response Analyses
1) Methods and Data
Exposure-response analyses were conducted by the Applicant to explore the relationship
between exposure of pralsetinib and efficacy and safety in NSCLC patients who received
pralsetinib.
The ER dataset contained all patients with valid safety and efficacy data and matching
pralsetinib exposures. A total of 206 subjects were enrolled by the 18NOV2019 cutoff date with
safety data. A total of 52 subjects were excluded as no pralsetinib PK was available (n=43) or
subjects had prior RET therapy (n=9). Thus, 154 subjects with a matching pralsetinib exposure
were included in the ER analysis for efficacy.
The ER analysis for efficacy explored the relationship between pralsetinib exposure and best
overall response (BOR) and progression free survival (PFS).
272
Version date: July 24, 2019 (ALL NDA/BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant
and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 4667100

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {213721}
Pralsetinib
The ER analysis for safety explored the relationship between pralsetinib exposure variables and
the following measures of safety in the safety population.

The primary exposure metrics for exposure-efficacy and exposure-safety assessment are model
predicted average plasma concentration (Cave) from the start of treatment until the first
occurrence of an event of interest or censoring.
2) Exposure-Efficacy Relationships
Kaplan-Meier curves of PFS by quartiles of Cave are displayed in Figure 10. Overall, no clear
trend was evident between PFS and pralsetinib exposure in the primary efficacy population.
However, subjects with the highest exposure (4th quartile of Cave) appeared to have the
shortest PFS. This finding is likely biased due to many patients having dose reduction and drug
holidays in the clinical trial. Patients with early progression are less likely to have dose holidays
or dose reductions, and therefore will likely have a higher Cave.
The average concentration up to the time of event (disease progression/death) or censoring is
found to have no association with PFS based on a Cox proportional hazard model (P-value =
0.261). The baseline covariates including age group, BSA, geographic region, race, RET fusion,
smoking history, gender, prior MKI therapy and prior platinum therapy were included in the
hazard model. BSA, gender and baseline ECOG status were found to affect PFS.
Boxplots of Cave by best overall response (BOR) was displayed in Figure 11. The distribution of
Cave appears to be similar across BOR. The proportion of CR/PR over the quartiles of Cave was
also displayed in Figure 12. No trend between BOR and quartiles of Cave was evident. The
baseline covariates across four exposure quartiles in the exposure-efficacy analyses appeared
to be balanced (Table 66).

2) Exposure-Safety Relationships
Time-to-event analysis suggests higher exposures of pralsetinib appeared to be correlated with
the time to any grade 3+ adverse events, grade 3+ anemia AEs and grade 3+ pneumonia AEs
(Figure 13 and Figure 14). Subjects in the 4th quartile of average exposure appeared to have a
faster rate and higher probability of experiencing the first grade 3+ adverse event. The baseline
covariates across four exposure quartiles in the exposure-safety analyses appeared to be
balanced (Table 67). There was no clear relationship between exposure metrics and grade 3+
pneumonitis, hypertension, or lymphopenia AEs, but the analysis is limited as the rates of grade
3+ pneumonitis and lymphopenia were low.
Increases in pralsetinib exposure appeared to correlate with decreases in hemoglobin and ANC
and minor increases in ALT (Figure 15). There did not appear to be any trend evident between
pralsetinib and changes in AST, lymphocyte count or diastolic/systolic blood pressures.

19.4.3 Bioanalytical Review
19.4.3.1 Summary of Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
Bioanalytical method review summary: Validation for method BTM-2256-R0 was adequate for
measurement of the concentration of pralsetinib in plasma to support studies BLU-667-0101,

Method Description
1. Determination of pralsetinib in plasma samples (method BTM-2256-R0)
A validated LC-MS/MS method was used for the determination of pralsetinib (X581238) in
K2EDTA human plasma using isotopically labelled internal standard (X581238-[13C,2H3]). The
method is suitable for measurement of the concentration of pralsetinib in plasma in studies
BLU-667-0101, BLU-667-0102, BLU-667-0103, BLU-667-0104, BLU-667-0105, and BLU-667-1101.
(b) (4)
Both the method validation and sample analysis were performed at
The validation report and addenda (BLU-R6395, BLU-R6395A1, and BLU-R6395A2)
and bioanalytical study reports for studies BLU-667-0101, BLU-667-0102, BLU-667-0103, BLU­
667-0104, BLU-667-0105, and BLU-667-1101 (interim report) were reviewed. The Applicant’s
responses dated June 5, 2020; June 26, 2020; and July 31, 2020 to information requests were
also reviewed. A summary of the method validation for measurement of pralsetinib is shown in
Table 68 below.
In addition, review of an addendum validation report BLU-R6395A1 reveals stock and spiking
solutions were stable at -20 °C for 182 days. Results of the long-term storage stability in matrix
study demonstrated that pralsetinib was stable for 184 days when stored at temperatures of ­
20 °C and -70 °C. The long-term storage stability of pralsetinib in matrix at -70 °C was further
extended to 802 days as stated in addendum validation report BLU-R6395A2.
In general, the bioanalytical method was fully validated and found to be sensitive and accurate
for the determination of pralsetinib in human plasma samples. All samples were analyzed
within the demonstrated stability window. In the responses dated June 26, 2020 and July 31,
2020 to information requests, Blueprint confirmed that the longest duration of sample storage
for studies that were reviewed at the clinical site or central laboratory was 308 days and at the
analytical site was 369 days with storage temperatures of approximately -75 °C to -70 °C at the
clinical, central laboratory, and analytical sites.
2. Determination of cortisol and 6β-hydroxycortisol in human urine (method BTM-1132)
A validated LC-MS/MS method was used for the determination of cortisol and 6β­
hydroxycortisol in human urine using isotopically labelled internal standard (cortisol-d4]). The
method is suitable for measurement of the concentration of cortisol and 6β-hydroxycortisol in
urine in study BLU-667-0104. Both the method validation and sample analysis were performed
(b) (4)

measurement of cortisol and 6β-hydroxycortisol is shown in Table 69 below.
